CHAPTER XVIII NATURE AND CHARACTERISTICS OF THE The Corporation 

Defined

CORPORATION 

During the past half century the corporate form of business organization has rapidly increased in importance. With the growth of large business units has come a demand for amounts of capital so large that they are obtainable only from many investors. Such investors desire income and limited liability, but they are unable to undertake active management. The corporation satisfies these needs and has today become the dominant form of business organization. 

The most commonly quoted definition of a corporation is perhaps that of Chief Justice Marshall. "A corporation is an artificial being, invisible, intangible, and existing only in contemplation of the law." The concept underlying this definition has become the foundation for the prevailing legal doctrine that a corporation is an artificial person, created by law and having a distinct existence, separate and apart from the natural persons who are responsible for its creation and operation. Although the doctrine is admittedly a legal fiction, it is disregarded only when a court believes that justice requires such action. The owners of the corporation are called shareholders, but the shareholders do not constitute the corporation. The corporation is a "separate entity." 

Characteristics of the Corporation 

As a legal entity the corporation has certain characteristics that distinguish it from other types of business organization. The most important of these characteristics will be considered briefly. (1) Separate Legal Existence. Being a distinct legal entity, the corporation may act under a corporate name. It may obtain, hold, and dispose of property in its corporate capacity. It may borrow funds and assume other obligations. It may enter into contracts with outsiders or with its own stockholders and, if necessary, may enforce such contracts through the courts. 427

[Ch. 18 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES (2) Transferable Units of Proprietorship. The interests in the proprietorship of a corporation are divided into transferable units known as shares of stock. The owners of the corporation, known as stockholders, may sell this stock representing their interest in the proprietorship without interfering with the operations of the corporation. 

(3) Limited Liability of Stockholders. Since a corporation is a separate legal entity, it is responsible for its own acts and obligations. Creditors of a corporation may not look beyond the assets of the corporation for satisfaction of their claims. Thus, the loss that a stockholder may suffer is usually limited to the amount of his investment. (4) Continuity of Existence. The life of a corporation depends upon the terms of its charter; it may be perpetual, or it may continue for a specified period of years. It is not affected by the death or incapacity of its stockholders. (5) Limitation of Action. A corporation may perform only those actions which its charter and the law allow it to perform. Its action is thus necessarily limited. In most cases, however, the powers granted are sufficiently broad to enable it to perform all activities necessary to the proper conduct of its affairs. 

Comparison of the 

Corporation and the Partnership A comparison of the corporation and the partnership reveals more completely the nature of the corporation. The chief differences between the two forms of organization are shown in the following outline: 

CORPORATION 1. Separate legal entity. 

2. Continuity of existence.

PARTNERSHIP 

1. Identification of organization with the individuals who compose it. 2. Automatic dissolution arising from death, withdrawal, or incapacity of a partner. 

3. Limited liability of the stock- 3. Unlimited personal liability of holders. 

4. Obligations arising from only the acts of agents or officers. 

5. Profits do not belong to stockholders unless dividends are declared. the partners. 

4. Obligations arising from the acts of any partner. 

5. Profits are personal profits of the partners. 

6. Control of stockholders is in- 6. Control of partners is direct direct. and personal.

From the preceding comparison it can be seen that a partnership, like a sole proprietorship, is legally identified with the persons who own it and is therefore a personal form of business organization. By contrast, the corporation is an impersonal form since it exists apart from the lives of any persons. 

Working Organization of the Corporation 

In the corporation the proprietorship is vested in the stockholders, acting as a "legal entity." The ownership of the stockholders is evidenced by shares of stock. Each share of stock gives the holder the right to one vote in the meetings of stockholders unless, at the time of its issue, the stock is designated as nonvoting stock. The power of each member therefore depends upon the number of shares he owns. 

In many corporations the membership is so large and widespread that most stockholders are unable to attend the annual meeting. Such stockholders may delegate their authority to an agent who votes their stock. This delegation is known as voting by proxy. Requests for such proxies are commonly mailed stockholders by the existing management. Signing such a proxy constitutes a vote for the retention of the present board of directors. 

Although the ultimate control of the corporation is vested in the stockholders, the latter exercise this control only indirectly. 

They elect at regular intervals, usually annually, a board of directors, to whom they delegate the duties of supervising and controlling the operations of the business. The directors usually submit to the stockholders annual reports showing the results of the operations of the past year. These reports sometimes contain recommendations as to plans and policies for the coming year. 

The recommendations are approved or disapproved by the stockholders at the annual meeting. 

The board of directors select the general executives, to whom they delegate the responsibility of administering the activities of the corporation. These executives usually consist of a president, one or more vice presidents, a secretary, and a treasurer. Each vice president may be in charge of a specific function of the business. For example, there may be a vice president in charge of sales and another in charge of production. The president is usually the chief executive of the business. The other executives, although they may be selected by the board of directors, act under the authority and the control of the president. The general executives

[Ch. 18 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

delegate the execution of the policies of the business to junior executives; these in turn employ the services of assistants. 

The chart below shows this narrowing down of the responsibility from the numerous stockholders to the executive head. It then shows the delegation of administrative duties from the chief executive to the employees of the corporation. Authority Stockholders Board of Directors 

Executive Committee 

Executive 

Head General Executives 

Junior Executives 

Assistants to Junior Executives

Responsibility— Workers

ILLUSTRATION No. 67, CORPORATE FORM OF ORGANIZATION 

A good illustration of this delegation of duties is found in a manufacturing firm that has a vice president in charge of production and under him a superintendent of shops. The latter, in turn, has subordinate to him departmental foremen, who direct and control the activities of the workers. 

Proprietorship in the

Corporation 

The stockholders of a corporation have no direct personal ownership in the assets of the enterprise. Instead they have a contract with the corporation giving them an interest in the corporate proprietorship and the right to participate in the management of the enterprise. The proprietorship of a corporation, like the proprietorship of an

individual, is the difference between the assets and the liabilities. 

The corporate proprietorship, however, is composed of two elements; a stated proprietorship, represented by a given number of shares at a specified amount per share, and a residual proprietorship, showing the results of the operations of the corporation. The stated proprietorship is the value of the capital stock. When the proprietorship of a corporation is greater than the amount of the capital stock, the excess is known as surplus; when it is smaller than the amount of the capital stock, the difference is known as deficit. The total proprietorship of a corporation, consisting of the capital stock plus the surplus or minus the deficit, is the net worth.

Into Shares 

Capital Stock Divided At the time of its formation a corporation receives a charter from the state, granting it authority to issue a specified amount of capital stock divided into a certain number of equal units known as shares. The amount of stock that it may issue is referred to as the authorized capital stock. A share of stock is, in effect, a contract between the stockholder and the corporation entitling the former to his proportionate share in the earnings of the corporation. 

The charter may assign a specific pecuniary value to the total authorized capital stock of the corporation and consequently a proportionate value to each share. The value so assigned to each share is known as the par value. For example, the charter may specify that the total capital stock is to be $500,000, divided into 5,000 shares, each having a par value of $100. 

In some cases the charter specifies no pecuniary value .for the stock but states merely the number of shares to be offered for sale. Such stock is of no par value. Each share of stock, however, represents a proportionate equity in the corporation. 

Ownership Evidenced Each stockholder receives a certificate of by Stock Certificates stock, which is evidence of the number of shares he owns. When a stockholder sells his stock, he fills in a blank form on the certificate, thus authorizing the corporation to transfer the specified part of his interest in the corporation to whatever person he designates. The certificate is forwarded to the corporation, which cancels it. The corporation then issues a certificate to the new stockholder and, if the original stockholder

[Ch. 18 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

has not transferred all of the shares shown on the original certificate, it issues a new certificate to him for the remainder. Proper entries are made in the corporate records. If a stock certificate is lost or destroyed, a new one may be issued without affecting the rights of the stockholder. 

Classification of Capital A corporation may issue several classes of Stock capital stock, each embodying different terms and carrying different privileges. There are several different bases of differentiation between classes of stocks, such as the distribution of profits, the distribution of assets in case of dissolution, and the accumulation of dividends. Several classes of stock are thus possible.

The most common classification is that of common stock and preferred stock. Common stock has no preference over any other stock of the company as to dividends or assets, but carries with it the right to participate in the management of the corporation by voting at stockholders' meetings. Preferred stock has some kind of preference over the ordinary, or common, stock. It is always preferred as to the distribution of profits. To understand the effect of this preference, it is necessary to consider briefly the method of distributing the profits of a corporation. 

The profits of a corporation may be divided yearly, semiannually, quarterly, or monthly according to the discretion of the board of directors. Whenever profits are divided among the stockholders, a dividend is said to be declared. A dividend is usually stated as a certain amount per share. To illustrate: If the capital stock of a company is $100,000 and the annual profit to be distributed is $8,000, each share of stock with a par value of $100 will receive an $8 dividend. Usually not all the profit of a fiscal period is distributed as a dividend, but some part is retained in the business to provide for unevenness in profits or losses in later years.

To make its stock attractive to investors, a corporation may issue a portion of it with preference as to dividends. This means. that, whenever profits are distributed, the preferred stockholders will receive their share first, the common stockholders receiving the balance of the distribution. Preferred stock is sometimes given a preference as to distribution of assets in case of dissolution and liquidation of the corporation. If the net assets are insufficient to

repay the investments of all stockholders, payment will be made first in total to preferred stockholders, and the balance remaining will be distributed to the common stockholders. 

Common stock is sometimes more desirable than preferred stock. This may be true if the holders of preferred stock do not share in profits in excess of the amount of their preference. To illustrate: A corporation is capitalized at $1,000,000, which is represented by 5,000 shares of common stock and 5,000 shares of 6 per cent preferred stock, the latter being barred from participation in profits above the preferred rate. The directors decide to distribute profits of $100,000 among the stockholders. Of this amount $30,000 is required to pay the dividend on the preferred stock. 

The remainder, $70,000, is distributed among the common stockholders. These stockholders therefore receive a 14 per cent dividend, which is greater than that paid to the holders of the preferred stock.

The participation of holders of preferred stock in the earnings of a corporation is ordinarily limited to the rate specified on the stock. Preferred stock that is limited to the specified rate is known as nonparticipating. If the holders of preferred stock receive an additional rate when earnings are adequate to pay the holders of common stock more than a specified rate, the preferred stock is known as participating. In other words, it participates with the common stock in the distribution of earnings in excess of the specified rate. For example, the Brown Corporation has $600,000 of stock outstanding, which is composed of 1,000 shares of 7 per cent participating preferred stock and 5,000 shares of common stock. 

Profits of $60,000 are to be distributed to the stockholders. The dividends will be paid as follows: (1) 7 per cent of the par value of the preferred stock, that is, $7,000, must be paid to the holders of the preferred stock; (2) 7 per cent of the par value of the common stock, that is, $35,000, will be paid to the holders of the common stock; (3) the remaining amount, $18,000, will provide an additional 3 per cent dividend on each class of stock, the holders of the preferred stock being entitled to $3,000 and the holders of the common stock to $15,000. The total dividend to be distributed to the holders of preferred stock will be 10 per cent, or $10,000; that to be distributed to the holders of common stock, 10 per cent, or $50,000.

[Ch. 18 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Preferred stock may also be either cumulative or noncumulative. If it is cumulative, the corporation is under an obligation to pay the full amount of dividends, at the rate stated in the certificates for preferred stock, before any dividends may be paid on common stock. If a dividend cannot be paid out of past earnings the claim will stand against future earnings. To illustrate: A owns 

1,000 shares of 6 per cent cumulative preferred stock, which would ordinarily net him an annual income of $6,000. If the earnings of the corporation should at any time fall to a point where only $4,000 of the $6,000 could be paid, the remaining $2,000 would be a claim against the profit earned during the next year. If this profit were not great enough to permit the payment of the $2,000, the amount would still stand as a claim on the earnings of any year that would be sufficient to enable the corporation to pay the $2,000. On the other hand, if A's stock were noncumulative, he would receive the $4,000 but would have no claim on future earnings.

Surplus or Deficit 

On the balance sheet the capital stock is reported at the par value or, in the case of no par value stock, at the value at which it was issued. As a result of the operations of the business, profits may have been earned or losses may have been incurred. If the corporation has undistributed profits, they may be reported under a descriptive heading such as "Undivided Profits" or "Surplus." The latter title is the more common. The amount of the undivided profits, or the surplus, is added to the capital stock to show the total proprietorship. 

If the business has incurred losses greater than the earnings, the excess of the losses is reported under the heading "Deficit." This amount is listed on the balance sheet as a deduction from the value of the capital stock.

A separate account is set up in the ledger to accumulate the increases and the decreases in proprietorship due to profits and to losses. Hence, unlike that of the sole proprietorship and the partnership, the capital investment of the owners is maintained separately from earnings and losses.

Corporate Proprietor ship on the Balance Sheet

The assets and the liabilities of a corporation are reported on the balance sheet in the same way as the assets and the liabilities of a sole proprietorship or a partnership. The proprietor-

ship section of the balance sheet, however, is different. It ordinarily shows each class of capital stock and the surplus or the deficit. For example, the proprietorship section of a balance sheet for a corporation with $100,000 of 6 per cent cumulative preferred stock, $200,000 of common stock, and a surplus of $75,000 is as follows: 

PROPRIETORSHIP CAPITAL STOCK: 

Preferred Stock (6 per cent cumulative)..

Common Stock. 

Total Capital Stock. 

Surplus..... 

Total Proprietorship.. 

100,000/00 

200,000 00. 

300,000 00 

75,000 00 

375,000 00 

If the same corporation had suffered losses from operations and had accumulated a deficit of $75,000, its proprietorship would be reported on the balance sheet in the following manner: 

PROPRIETORSHIP CAPITAL STOCK: 

Preferred Stock (6 per cent cumulative). 100,000/00

Common Stock.. 

200,000/00

Total Capital Stock.. 

300,000/00 

Less Deficit... 

75,000 00

Total Proprietorship.. 

225,000 00

Values of Stock 

The value of the stock of a corporation is always spoken of in terms of shares. A share may have six different values: par value, book value, market value, liquidation value, stated value, and redemption value.

Par value. Par value is the value assigned to each share of stock when the charter is issued. It is stated on each certificate. 

Par value and face value are sometimes used as synonyms. Nopar-value stock does not have par value, but it has the other values discussed in the following paragraphs. 

Book value. The par value of the capital stock does not represent the entire proprietorship. There is usually a surplus, although there may be a deficit. The value of the capital stock plus

[Ch. 18 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

the surplus or minus the deficit equals the actual proprietorship. 

While the actual proprietorship changes, the number of shares remains fixed; hence it is clear that the value of each share, based on the proprietorship, or net worth, is different from the par, or face, value. Book value may be defined as the value of stock that is determined by dividing the net worth by the number of shares outstanding. Thus, if the balance sheet of a corporation shows a capital stock of $100,000 (1,000 shares having a par value of $100) and a surplus of $35,000, the book value of each share is $135. 

If, instead of a surplus of $35,000 in the preceding illustration, there had been a deficit of $35,000, the book value of each share would have been $65 [($100,000-$35,000)÷1,000]. 

Market value. When shares of stock are bought and sold, the price seldom, if ever, conforms to either the book value or the par value. Thus, if a stockholder in the corporation just mentioned was offering his stock for sale on the market, he probably would not receive exactly $135 for each share. If the corporation was making large profits, the stockholder could probably get more than $135, perhaps $140 or $150 a share. The value placed upon stock in the open market is called the market value.

Liquidation Value. If a corporation is dissolved, its assets must be liquidated, or converted into cash, within a limited time. 

The amounts obtained for the assets through the liquidation sale are usually less than the values at which the assets are recorded on the books. The disposal of the assets for less than the book values decreases the proprietorship and consequently the book value of the capital stock. The value of the stock under these conditions is known as the liquidation value.

Stated Value. When no-par stock is issued, provision is commonly made for a portion of the amount received from the sale of such stock to be set up as stated value. If stock is sold for $6, $7, and $8 a share at different times, the stated value might be set as $5. When this value appears in the accounts, it makes the accounting for no-par stock very similar to the accounting for par stock.

Redemption Value. When preferred stock is issued, there may be a provision permitting the corporation to redeem it, usually at a price above par. This is known as the redemption value of the stock.

Incorporation 

A corporation is "authorized by law and created by charter." Since a corporation is created by permission from governmental authority and under the supervision of a governmental unit, some legal formalities must be met before a charter will be granted. Although these legal requirements vary somewhat in the different states, their general nature can be indicated.

In the early history of the development of the corporate form, special action by a state legislature was required for the formation of each corporation. With the rapidly increasing number of applications for incorporation, this method soon grew burdensome; and in its place were substituted general laws, or enabling acts, setting forth the provisions under which corporations might be formed. Under present laws, state control over incorporations is usually delegated to the secretary of state and the state treasurer. 

Application for permission to incorporate must be submitted to the secretary of state, who acts for the state in passing favorably or unfavorably upon the legality of the proposed organization. 

The state treasurer is usually responsible for the collection of organization fees and of taxes. In some states an application for incorporation must be addressed to the Governor of the state and approved by that official. In a few states there is a commissioner of corporations or an officer bearing a similar title, who has the authority with reference to corporations that is usually vested in the secretary of state.

The process of organization varies in different states, but the typical method involves two steps: (1) obtaining subscriptions to stock and effecting a tentative organization; (2) obtaining the permission of the state to commence business operations. The law usually stipulates what the application for incorporation must contain. In many cases the state merely approves the application for incorporation. The application then becomes the charter of the corporation.

A corporation must conform to the corporate laws of the state in which it has been organized. If it extends its operations beyond the state, it is subject to the Federal laws involving interstate commerce and the laws of all states in which it carries on business. It is also subject to that general control which any state exercises over all business organizations.

[Ch. 18 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Certificate of Incor- The charter states the powers that the poration corporation possesses and the limitations. under which it must operate. As previously explained, the laws of the state in which organization is to take place specify the form of application that a proposed corporation must submit; and if this application is approved, it becomes in many cases the charter of the corporation. The provisions of law governing the form of the application for incorporation are quite important, since they must be strictly complied with to obtain the approval of the state. 

These provisions vary in different states, but in general they include all the important features peculiar to a corporation, such as the name, the purpose, the duration, the location, and the capitalization.

To show more clearly the nature of the data that may be required in an application for incorporation, the following provisions from the statutes of the state of New York are given: "Except as provided in Section 2a of this chapter, three or more persons may become a stock corporation for any lawful purpose or purposes. . . by making, subscribing, acknowledging, and filing a certificate. . . which shall state: “(1) The name of the proposed corporation. "(2) The purpose or purposes for which the corporation is to be formed. "(3) Either the amount of the capital stock and the number and par value of the shares of which it is to consist; or if the corporation is to issue shares without par value, the statements required by Section 12. "(4) If the shares are to be classified, the number of shares to be included in each class and all of the designations, preferences, privileges, and voting powers of the shares of each class, and the restrictions or qualifications thereof. "(5) The city, village, or town and the county in which the office of the corporation is to be located. "(6) The duration of the corporation. "(7) The number of its directors . . . not less than three. "(8) The names and post-office addresses of the directors... "(9) The name and post-office address of each subscriber listed on the certificate of incorporation, and a statement of the number of shares which he agrees to take... 

---

The certificate must be signed by the three or more persons who make application for the charter, and their signatures must be acknowledged by a notary public or some other designated official.

Assets 

Issuing Stock in Return When a corporation receives its charter, for Cash or Other the books of record may be opened. The incorporators, or organizers, of the corporation have, in most cases, performed some transactions prior to the granting of the charter and should have kept a record of them. These transactions are not, however, transactions performed by the corporation; therefore, no entries should be made in the records of the corporation until the charter has been obtained. 

The opening entries of a corporation are similar to those of other types of business organizations in that they record the assets that the corporation has acquired and the liabilities, if any, that the corporation is to assume. The proprietorship of the corporation at the time of formation is recorded as one item, and is not credited to accounts with the individual members of the organization, as would be done in the case of a partnership. 

The simplest case that can be taken by way of illustration is that of a corporation, the entire authorized capital stock of which has been sold for cash. The Holmes Manufacturing Company is organized on March 15 with an authorized capital stock of $800,000, consisting of 8,000 shares of common stock, each having a par value of $100. If all the capital stock is sold for cash on 

March 15, the transaction may be recorded as follows: *Mar. 15 Cash...

Capital Stock 

800,000 00 

800,000 00 

Sold 8,000 shares of capital stock at $100 a share, par value, and issued the stock.

After this entry has been posted to the ledger, the cash account will contain a debit of $800,000 and the capital stock account a credit of the same amount.

In the previous discussion it was assumed that stock was sold *For convenience, all the entries given to illustrate the opening entries of a corporation are recorded in general journal form. Some of these would ordinarily be entered in cash records.

[Ch. 18 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

for cash and that the cash was invested in the assets needed by the corporation. Occasionally stock is exchanged for assets directly; in this case a price must be assigned to the assets acquired. 

For example, if a lot is to be acquired as a site for a building to be used by the corporation, the board of directors must know the price of the lot in order to determine the number of shares to be exchanged for it. If the price is $10,000 and shares are being sold for cash at $100 par value, the number of shares to be given for the land would be 100. The entry would be:

Mar. 20 Land..

Capital Stock 

Exchanged 100 shares of capital stock at $100 a share for land as per contract No. 102. 10,000,00 

10,000 00

If payment for the stock had been made with several pieces of property, such as machinery, buildings, and land, which were to be retained for use by the corporation, the opening entry would have recorded the various assets as follows: 

Mar. 20

Buildings.

Issued 4,000 shares of capital stock (par value $100) in return for machinery, buildings, and land acquired from C. D. Evans in accordance with the contract on file. 150,000/00 155,000/00 95,000/00 

400,000 00

If a partnership is to be converted into a corporation, or if a corporation buys out a going business, prices must be assigned to the assets taken over by the corporation. If the amount of stock issued to the partners is greater than the net assets acquired, the difference is a charge to Goodwill. This practice was fully described in the chapters on partnerships. For example, if a corporation acquires a going partnership in exchange for 500 shares of its stock (par value $100), and takes over the following assets and assumes the following liabilities, the goodwill is the difference between the par value of the stock given in exchange and the net worth acquired. Calculation of goodwill is shown on page 441.

In many cases the stock given in exchange is the difference between new values placed on the assets acquired and the liabilities assumed. In such cases the assets must be set up on the corporation accounts at the new values without reference to their book values on the accounts of the partnership. Such a transaction is illustrated on page 446.

The names of the stockholders need not be mentioned in the explanation since full data will be given in the minute book, the stock certificate book, and the stockholders' ledger, which are discussed in the next chapter.

In the preceding illustrations it was assumed that all the authorized capital stock was issued. In many cases this is not true. 

For example, the charter of the B. C. Olmsted Company may authorize the issuance of stock amounting to $600,000, but only $500,000 of the stock may be sold at the time of the organization, the remaining $100,000 being retained for sale at a later time, when more funds may be needed.

It is often desirable to have the charter authorize the issuance of more stock than the quantity to be issued at the time of the formation of the corporation. The company is then relieved of the necessity of applying for a change in the charter at the time when it may wish to increase its capital. For example, if the charter of the B. C. Olmsted Company authorized an issue of only $500,000 of capital stock, but at a later time $100,000 of additional capital was needed, it would be necessary for the company to have its charter revised. Since the charter, however, authorizes the issuance of capital stock amounting to $600,000 and only

[Ch. 18 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

$500,000 has been issued, the remaining $100,000 of stock can be issued at any time without further authorization from the state. 

The consent of the majority of the stockholders, and in some cases of two thirds or three fourths, must be obtained before a charter can be changed. This requirement may add to the difficulty of obtaining an increase in the amount of authorized stock. 

The sale of $500,000 of the capital stock authorized by the charter of the B. C. Olmsted Company may be recorded as follows: June 1 Cash..

Capital Stock. 

Issued 5,000 shares of capital stock (par value $100) upon the receipt of cash in payment. 

500,000 00 

500,000 00

By this method the unissued capital stock is not recorded in the books, and the capital stock account shows only the amount of stock that has been issued. When the amount of the authorized capital stock differs from the amount of the outstanding stock, the former amount also should be shown on the balance sheet, since this information will be of importance to those who will study the report in order to determine the exact financial condition of the business. The authorized capital stock of the B. C. 

Olmsted Company may be reported in a footnote on the balance sheet in the following manner:

PROPRIETORSHIP Capital Stock Outstanding. 

Surplus.... 

Total Proprietorship.. 

500,000 00* 4,186 25 504,186 25 *Authorized Capital Stock, $600,000.00

Changing from a Part- From a legal point of view, changing from nership to a Corporation a partnership to a corporation necessitates (1) the dissolution of the partnership, which is usually effected by an agreement between the partners, and (2) the fulfillment of all the requirements imposed by the state upon the incorporators, which is evidenced by the charter granted by the state. From an accounting point of view, two steps are necessary: (1) the books of the partnership must be closed according to the partnership agreement and (2) the books of the corporation must be opened in compliance with the provisions of the charter.

To illustrate the change of a partnership to a corporation, it may be assumed that W. L. Slater and H. S. Burbank are partners. 

In order to obtain additional capital for the expansion of their enterprise, the partners decide to incorporate. The balance sheet of the partnership is:

[Ch. 18 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

If assets of a partnership are to be turned over to a new corporation at values different from their book values, the difference between the new values and the previously existing book values should be debited or credited to an account indicating the effect of such revaluation on the proprietorship of the partners. For example, if merchandise having a book value of $45,000 is to be taken over by a corporation at $40,000, the difference of $5,000 should be debited to an account such as Loss and Gain on Revaluation, in the ledger of the partnership. If land with a book value of $15,000 is to be taken over by the same corporation at $25,000, the difference of $10,000 should be credited to the account Loss and Gain on Revaluation. The balance of the loss and gain on revaluation account in the ledger of the partnership will be debited or credited to the partners' capital accounts in the profit-and-loss ratio.

CHAPTER XIX  

Recording Subscriptions The entries illustrated in the preceding to Capital Stock pages are the simplest that can be used in opening the books of a corporation. Ordinarily, before stock is actually sold, a corporation accepts subscriptions from those who are to be stockholders. The subscriber agrees to pay a certain amount at a specified time for the shares for which he subscribes. 

This amount represents a receivable of the corporation and is debited to an account entitled Subscriptions Receivable. On the other hand, the corporation has increased its proprietorship; and when payment is made by the subscriber, the corporation will issue a stock certificate to the subscriber. This increase in proprietorship is credited, at the time the subscription is received, to an account entitled Capital Stock Subscribed. The entries in both the subscriptions receivable account and the capital stock subscribed account represent phases of a transaction that has not been entirely completed. The subscription receivable will eventually, like an account receivable, be converted into cash or its equivalent; the capital stock subscribed will become capital stock outstanding. 

To illustrate: The Williams Corporation is incorporated on 

July 1, 19--, with an authorized capital stock of $600,000, composed of 6,000 shares, each having a par value of $100. A statement to this effect is made in the journal. On the same day subscriptions are received for 5,000 shares of stock at the par value. 

The asset account Subscriptions Receivable is debited for $500,000, and the proprietorship account Capital Stock Subscribed is credited for the same amount. This entry shows that the corporation has a claim against the subscribers for $500,000 and that the subscribers have a claim against the corporation for the unissued capital stock. In accordance with the terms of their contract, the subscribers make a payment of $40 on each share for which they subscribed. To record the receipt of the $200,000 from the subscribers, Cash is debited and Subscriptions Receivable is credited. 

Since the corporation has received only a 40 per cent payment on 454

Ch. 19] 455 

the stock to be issued and since $300,000, or $60 on each share, must still be collected, no stock is issued at this time; therefore, no entry is made in the capital stock subscribed account. On July 3 an additional $300,000 in cash is received to complete the payment on the subscriptions. Cash is again debited, and Subscriptions Receivable is credited. Since the stock for which subscriptions were received has been fully paid for, it may be issued. 

Its issuance converts the capital stock subscribed into outstanding capital stock. The capital stock subscribed account is therefore debited, and the capital stock account is credited. These entries, in journal form, are:

Although either of the two methods may be used in practice, the first will be used in all further illustrations. This method is simpler in that it avoids the use of the unissued capital stock account. It is also questionable whether the authorization to issue stock constitutes an accountable transaction.

Issuing Stock at a Pre- Capital stock is sometimes issued for more mium or a Discount or less than its par value. When this is done, a new account is set up to record the difference between the selling price and the par value. When stock is issued for more than its par value, it is said to be issued at a premium; when it is issued for less than par value, it is issued at a discount. If stock is issued at a premium in exchange for cash, an entry debiting Cash and crediting Capital Stock and Premium on Capital Stock must be made. For example, on July 25 The Williams Corporation sells 

500 shares of stock, which have a par value of $100, at $105 a share. 

The entry to record the sale and the issuance of the stock is: 

July 25 Cash... 52,500 00 

Capital Stock. 

50,000 00

Premium on Capital Stock. 

2,500 00 

Sold 500 shares of stock at $105 a share.

If stock is issued at a discount, the account Discount on Capital Stock is debited for the difference between the sales price and the par value. For example, if on May 1 a corporation issues at $97 a share 5,000 shares of stock with a par value of $100 a share, the following journal entry may be made to record the transaction:

Ch. 19] 

459 May 1 Cash..

Discount on Capital Stock. 

Capital Stock. 

Sold and issued 5,000 shares of stock at $97 a share, the par value being $100. 485,000/00 

15,000 00 

500,000 00

Premium and discount on capital stock should be recorded and presented on the balance sheet as a part of the investment by stockholders and not as an earning or loss of the corporation. The premium on capital stock is added to the par value of capital stock outstanding to show the total investment of the stockholders. 

Likewise, the discount on capital stock is deducted from the balance of capital stock outstanding. It is sometimes considered preferable, however, to close out the discount on capital stock against the profits of future fiscal periods. This is accomplished by a debit to Surplus and a credit to Discount on Capital Stock. 

If the subscriptions to capital stock are to be shown in the records, the subscriptions receivable account is debited for the selling price of the stock, and the capital stock subscribed account is credited for the par value. This procedure is followed whether、 the shares are sold at a premium, at a discount, or at their par value. Premium on capital stock or discount on capital stock is the difference between the amount received and the par value of the stock issued. The accounts with premium and discount on capital stock are valuation accounts affecting the capital stock account.

Organization Expenses Expenses are incurred in the organization of a corporation, such as fees paid to the state, attorney's fees, and expenses of promoting the corporation and selling stock. These expenditures, which are known as organization expenses, are not chargeable as expenses of the first fiscal period. They represent an asset because the corporation could not have been established without incurring them. Organization expenses are therefore considered an intangible asset and theoretically could be carried on the books permanently at the original cost. As the asset represented by organization expenses has no selling value, a more conservative treatment is to write it off over a period of years, ordinarily from three to five years. The amounts written off should be charged to Surplus, and not to Profit and Loss Summary, as they are not properly chargeable to any one period.

[Ch. 19 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Capital Surplus and The chief source of corporate surplus is un- 

Earned Surplus distributed earnings of the corporation.

When corporate profits are retained and not paid as dividends to stockholders, the increase in proprietorship comes from earnings and is shown by increases in the surplus account. If only earnings and dividends affected surplus, then all surplus would be earned surplus.

As is shown in the preceding paragraphs, surplus is affected by transactions other than those showing earnings and dividends. 

When stock is sold at a premium, a credit is made to the surplus account Premium on Capital Stock. Such an item is clearly not an earning of the corporation and therefore differs from the earned surplus. It is called capital surplus to distinguish it from earned surplus. Increases in proprietorship, beyond those shown in the capital stock account, from sources other than earnings are called capital surplus items.

It is considered advisable to keep separate in the ledger capital surplus accounts and earned surplus accounts. It is also more informative to keep separate on the balance sheet capital surplus and earned surplus. The increase in surplus resulting from a sale of stock at a premium is a different kind of increase from that arising from operations. Further discussion of surplus will be given in the following chapter.

Corporation Earnings. The determination of the profit or loss for a corporation is similar to that for the sole proprietorship or the partnership. It is in the distribution of earnings to owners that the corporation differs from these other two forms of business organization.

The first subtraction from the earnings of a corporation is for the Federal income tax. Until the last three or four years corporations paid approximately one eighth of their earnings as Federal income tax. More recently they have paid approximately one sixth.

As has been stated before, distribution of earnings to stockholders is at the discretion of the board of directors. If the board feels that dividends may be paid, they "declare" a dividend.

These dividends may be declared out of current earnings or out of past earnings, represented by a balance in the surplus account. 

To illustrate: Assume earnings of $60,000, an income tax of $8,000, a declaration of a preferred dividend of $12,000, and a

Ch. 19] 461 

declaration of a common dividend of $15,000. The following entry would be made to close the profit and loss summary account on 

December 31: 

Dec. 31

Profit and Loss Summary.. 

Income Tax Payable.. 

Preferred Dividend Payable No. 4. 

Common Dividend Payable No. 2. 

Surplus.... 

60,000/00 

8,000 00 

12,000 00 

15,000 00 25,000 00 If the earnings had been $30,000, and the income tax $4,000, and the board of directors wished to draw on surplus in order to pay the same amount of dividends on the common stock, the entry to close the profit and loss summary account would have been: 

Dec. 31

Profit and Loss Summary. 

Surplus... 

Income Tax Payable.. 

Preferred Dividend Payable No. 4. 

Common Dividend Payable No. 2. 

30,000/0 1,000 00 

4,000 00 12,000 00 15,000 00 This entry would reduce the balance in the surplus account, and the balance sheet for the current year would show a smaller amount for surplus than had been on the balance sheet for the preceding year. 

Ordinarily dividends are payable only from earnings of the corporation. If a corporation has accumulated a deficit from losses over a period of years, this deficit must be offset by profits before dividends may be paid. If there is cumulative preferred stock as well as common stock outstanding, no dividends can be paid on common stock until the unpaid or passed dividends that have accumulated on the preferred stock have been paid. Assume that a corporation that has $100,000 of 5 per cent cumulative preferred stock and $100,000 of common stock has a deficit of $15,000, which has accumulated over a period of four years. No dividends have, of course, been declared on the common stock during the time. Since the preferred stock is cumulative, dividends amounting to $20,000 ($5,000×4) have accumulated on it. Therefore, before any amount is available for dividends on the common stock, earnings of at least $40,000, that is, earnings sufficient to offset the deficit of $15,000 and to pay the passed dividends of $20,000 in addition to the current dividend on the preferred stock, would

[Ch. 19 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

be required. If the earnings of the current year were $40,000, the distribution would be as follows: 

To make up the deficit of the past four years.

To pay the four passed dividends on the preferred stock To pay the preferred dividend for the current year .. $15,000 20,000 5,000

Total earnings necessary before any amount becomes available for a dividend on the common stock.. $40,000

If the earnings of the current year were $60,000, the first $40,000 would be distributed as in the table above. The balance of $20,000 would be available for dividends on common stock. 

If the board decided to declare $10,000 as dividends on common stock, the balance of $10,000 would be credited to Surplus. The total credit to Surplus would be $25,000, or the sum of the deficit and the balance of the current earnings.

The payment of dividends actually involves two steps, each of which is shown by a journal entry: (a) the declaration of the dividend by the board of directors and (b) the payment of the dividend by the treasurer of the corporation. The act of the directors creates a current liability by transferring the amount of the dividend from a proprietorship equity to a creditors' claim against the corporation. Stockholders thus become creditors of their own corporation to the extent of the dividend. The payment of the dividend liability is like the payment of any other liability-a credit to Cash. The two entries below illustrate the two steps:

Dividend Payable No. 7.

The Statement of The profit earned by a corporation during 

Surplus a given fiscal period may be distributed as dividends to the stockholders, or it may be used to increase their proprietary interests. The disposition of the profit of the period may be reported in the proprietorship section of the balance sheet or at the foot of the statement of profit and loss. It is considered better accounting practice, however, to set up a separate surplus statement for this purpose. Such a statement tends to link the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss by beginning

with the surplus on the previous balance sheet, accounting for the current profits, and concluding with the surplus on the present balance sheet. A surplus statement is shown below:

Earned and Capital Surplus, December 31, 19--. 152,000 00 Records Required by The accounts of a corporation are essena Corporation tially the same as those of any other type of business organization. The few additional accounts required can be added in the general ledger. The books of original entry need not be different from those used by a sole proprietorship or a partnership. The only distinctive records required by a corporation are those used to record the corporate activities of the company. 

The records most commonly used for this purpose are: (1) the minute book, (2) the subscription book, (3) the subscribers' ledger, (4) the stock certificate book, and (5) the stockholders' ledger.

The Minute Book. The minute book is the legal record of the proceedings of all meetings of the stockholders and of the board of directors. It often includes the charter and the by-laws as well. 

This record book serves as authority for actions of the officers and furnishes data for important entries to be made by the accountant. 

In it are found decisions on matters such as the purchase and the sale of property, the investment of surplus funds, the declaration of dividends, the obtaining of additional funds through the issue

[Ch. 19 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

of bonds or additional stock, and the maintaining of reserves and sinking funds.

The Subscription Book. In most states the laws governing the creation of corporations provide that all or a substantial part of the capital stock of a corporation must be subscribed before a charter will be granted. Blanks containing the subscription contract are usually prepared, and the signatures of those who agree to subscribe for stock are placed on them. These subscription blanks may later be filed in a binder and referred to as the sub scription book. 

The entries in the subscription book may be summarized at intervals, and the totals posted through an entry in the general journal by debiting Subscriptions Receivable and crediting 

Capital Stock Subscribed. 

The Subscribers' Ledger. The subscribers' ledger contains ac counts with the individual subscribers to capital stock. An account with each subscriber is debited from the subscription book for the amount that the subscriber agreed to pay for the stock, and is credited from the cashbook with the payments made in full or on account. The subscriptions receivable account in the general ledger is also debited with the total of the subscriptions and is credited with the total of the cash received from subscribers. 

The Stock Certificate Book. The stock certificate book consists of blank stock certificates numbered serially and bound in book form with a stub for each certificate. If both preferred stock and common stock are issued, a different kind of certificate is ordinarily used for each class. Some corporations use one form for both preferred stock and common stock, but designate the certificate for preferred stock by having the word Preferred and the conditions of preference printed in ink of a different color across the face of the certificate. In some cases those provisions in the charter that refer to the different classes of stock are printed on the certificates. Then the same form can be used for both classes of stock, and only the class of stock for which the certificate is issued need be indicated.

When different forms are used for common stock and preferred stock, the certificates of each kind may be kept in separate books or in the same book. Separate books are, however, preferable. In either case the certificates of each class should have their own serial numbers.

Ch. 465 

As may be seen by reference to Illustration No. 68, each stock certificate contains the name of the corporation, the amount of the capital stock, the total number of shares, and the number of shares represented by the certificate. When stock is issued, the certificate and the accompanying stub are filled out. The stub serves as a permanent record of the number of shares of stock represented by the certificate, the name of the person to whom the stock has been issued, the date of issue, and the fact that the stock is an original issue or a reissue. 

When stock is transferred, the owner must fill out the form for an indorsement that is printed on the back of the certificate.

signature of the transferor must be witnessed, as the illustration indicates. The name of the new owner need not be filled in. If this part of the indorsement is left blank, the certificate may be passed from one purchaser to another. On the records of the company (the stub of the certificate and the account in the stockholders' ledger), however, the first transferor may still be indicated as the owner of the stock. He will remain so on the books of the corporation until the certificate has been surrendered to the secretary of the been issued to the new owner. [Ch. 19 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

on the old certificate. When a certificate is canceled, it is pasted to the stub from which it was detached at the time of issue. 

This discussion indicates that the stock certificate book is a record book, not a book of account. A relation exists, however, between this book and the account or accounts with capital stock. 

A careful check should therefore be made regularly to see that the stock certificate book is being kept correctly. From the stubs of this book the accountant obtains data for entries in the stockholders' ledger, with which the stock certificate book must therefore agree.

The Stockholders' Ledger. The stockholders' ledger, or stock book as it is sometimes called, is a subsidiary record of the capital stock outstanding. It contains a separate account for each stockholder and is controlled by the account or accounts in the general ledger that show the amount of capital stock outstanding. 

The account of each stockholder is credited with the number of shares of stock purchased by him, and is debited with the number of shares sold or otherwise transferred by him. The ownership, or proprietorship, of each stockholder is indicated, not by the par value or any other value of the shares held, but by the number of shares held, the value of which may be estimated quite readily if it is needed.

The following example illustrates the recording of stock ownership in the stockholders' ledger: C. C. Dixon, C. Ayres, and

A. J. Reynolds are the incorporators of the Reynolds Manufacturing Company, which has a capital stock of $100,000, each share having a par value of $100. Dixon owns 500 shares; Ayres, 300 shares; and Reynolds, 200 shares. The capital stock account in the general ledger has a credit balance of $100,000, which is the total amount of capital stock outstanding. A record of the capital stock issued to these stockholders is maintained in the stockholders' ledger in three accounts: one with Dixon, one with Ayres, and one with Reynolds. Five hundred shares have been credited to Dixon's account; 300 shares, to Ayres' account; and 200 shares, to Reynolds' account. The total of the balances of these three accounts is 1,000 shares. The value of these shares is equal to the credit balance of the capital stock account, $100,000 (1,000 shares at $100 each).

Ch. 19] 467 

The information in the stockholders' ledger was recorded from the stubs of the stock certificates issued to Dixon, Ayres, and Reynolds. As long as these individuals hold the same amounts of stock, the accounts in the stockholders' ledger will show the same results. If, at a later date, some of the stock is transferred from one of the shareholders to another, a change will have to be made in the accounts in the stockholders' ledger. 

For example, Dixon sells Ayres and Reynolds each 100 shares.

This transaction involves, first, the cancellation of Dixon's ownership of the 200 shares and, second, the issue of certificates to the new holders. Since Dixon's holdings are in 100-share lots, he has one certificate for 100 shares canceled to take care of Ayres' new ownership and another certificate, also for 100 shares, canceled in favor of Reynolds. These canceled stock certificates, signed by Dixon, are pasted to the original stubs, and new certificates are issued to Ayres and Reynolds. The transaction must then be entered in the stockholders' ledger. The information for the entries is obtained from the stubs of the canceled certificates and of the new certificates. Dixon's account is debited for the 

200 shares transferred, and both Ayres' account and Reynolds' account are credited for the 100 shares purchased. When the posting has been completed, the total credits still amount to 1,000 shares, or the number of shares of capital stock outstanding. This example illustrates that, while the ownership of the stock of a corporation is constantly shifting, the amount of capital stock may remain the same.

When the entries have been made in the stockholders' ledger, the accounts of Dixon, Ayres, and Reynolds appear as follows:

C. C. DIXON DETROIT, MICHIGAN 

19-- |19-- 

Feb. 1 To C. Ayres 

100 shares Jan. 

2 Original Issue 500 shares 

1 To A. J. Reynolds 100 shares

C. AYRES 

TOLEDO, OHIO 19Jan. 

2 Original Issue 

300 shares Feb. 

1 From C. C. Dixon 100 shares

[Ch. 19 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES A. J. REYNOLDS 

19-- Jan. Feb.

CLEVELAND, OHIO 

2 Original Issue 200 shares 

1 From C. C. Dixon||100 shares 

The accounts in the stockholders' ledger are similar to the capital accounts in a partnership, except that the values are in terms of shares rather than money. Another difference is that, in the records of the corporation, the capital stock is considered apart from the ownership of the individual stockholders. Hence the controlling account in the general ledger shows the value of all the outstanding capital stock, and the subsidiary ledger accounts indicate the number of shares owned by each stockholder. 

In order that the subsidiary accounts in the stockholders' ledger will agree with the controlling account in the general ledger, accurate entries, corresponding to the information kept in the stock certificate book, must be made in the stockholders' ledger. 

The number of shares represented by each certificate that has not been canceled in the stock certificate book must therefore be listed in the stockholders' ledger. The total value of these shares should equal the credit balance of the account showing the amount of capital stock outstanding.

The stockholders' ledgers of some corporations have the simple ruling of the ordinary ledger. This ruling is satisfactory if few transfers are made, but in large companies in which stock is constantly changing hands a record providing for more complete information is necessary. Furthermore, since a stockholder may "split" a certificate, or sell only a part of the shares represented by the certificate, the number of shares sold will not always correspond with the number of shares shown on the certificate. If all these details are to be recorded, additional columns will be needed in the stockholders' ledger, and a wider ledger page or card will thus be necessary.

It may be assumed that in the illustration previously cited Dixon's entire holdings are represented by one certificate for 500 shares. When Dixon sells 200 shares to Ayres and Reynolds, he is splitting his certificate. He must then surrender his certificate (No. 1) to the company. To show Dixon's remaining interest in the corporation, a new certificate representing 300 shares (No. 4) is issued to him. Certificates of 100 shares each (Nos. 5 and 6)

Ch. 19] 469 

are issued to Ayres and Reynolds to show their additional interest in the corporation. These details may be conveniently recorded in accounts similar to that shown in Illustration No. 70. This form of account provides columns for the date of issue of each stock certificate, the number of the certificate surrendered by the one making the sale, the name of the person to whom or from whom the stock was transferred, the number of the new certificate issued, the debit for shares transferred, the credit for shares obtained, and the credit balance.

ILLUSTRATION No. 70, AccOUNT IN A STOCKHOLDERS' LEDGER 

Corporate Records and To summarize:

The minute book contains a 

Accounting record of the proceedings of the meetings of the stockholders and of the board of directors. It is not a book of account, but it evidences the authority for important entries made in the books of account. 

The subscribers' ledger is a subsidiary ledger controlled by the subscriptions receivable account in the general ledger. When subscriptions are received, the blanks may be filed in a binder, which is referred to as the subscription book. The amounts of the subscriptions are posted to the individual accounts in the subscribers' ledger, and the total of the subscriptions is posted to the subscriptions receivable account in the general ledger. When payments on subscriptions are received, the individual amounts are posted to the accounts in the subscribers' ledger and the total of the receipts is posted to the subscriptions receivable account in the general ledger. When the posting has been completed, the balance of the subscriptions receivable account should equal the sum of the balances of the accounts in the subscribers' ledger.

[Ch. 19 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

The stockholders' ledger is a subsidiary record of the capital stock outstanding. When stock is issued for the first time, the individual accounts in the stockholders' ledger are credited for the number of shares issued, and the capital stock account in the general ledger is credited for the par value of the shares. When stock that has once been issued is transferred from one stockholder to another, an entry in the general ledger is not required since the total amount of capital stock outstanding has not been changed. The only entry necessary is that which is made from the stock certificate book to the individual accounts in the stockholders' ledger.

A corporation is ready to carry on its operations after a part or all of its stock has been sold, the charter has been obtained, and the preliminary data have been recorded in the books. The fact that a business is incorporated does not mean that its activities are distinctly different from those of a sole proprietorship or a partnership. No matter what its legal organization may be, an enterprise must buy to sell, or produce to sell; it must incur expenses and realize profits if it is to continue in business; and it must have these various transactions recorded in books of account in order to show its financial condition. 

The difference in legal status between the corporation and the other forms of business organization does not affect operations to a great extent. Since the existence of a corporation is the result of procuring a charter, the corporation is necessarily subject to the regulations and provisions set forth in that document. It is subject also to the national and the state laws relating to corporations. Otherwise it acts as does a sole proprietorship or a partnership.

CHAPTER XX  Corporate Surplus 

The two chief divisions of corporate surplus, (1) capital surplus and (2) earned surplus, were introduced in the previous chapter. It was stated there that, because of the difference in nature of these two types of surplus, they should be accounted for separately and should not be combined in one item on the periodic statements. The practice of showing a single surplus item on the balance sheet is still widely followed. Even when this is done, it usually represents the total of the balances of several surplus accounts in the ledger of the corporation. These surplus accounts show the special conditions that gave rise to the various surplus items. The surplus accounts of a corporation may generally be divided into two groups: capital surplus accounts and earned surplus accounts. 

Capital Surplus Accounts 

Increases in proprietorship of a corporation arising from sources other than earnings of the corporation and not represented by par or stated value of outstanding stock result in capital surplus. Such increases are best considered as additions to the permanent investment of the business, and may be credited to special surplus accounts to indicate the origins of the increases in proprietorship. Capital surplus may arise from a number of sources. The principal sources are (1) amounts paid in, (2) the revaluation of assets, (3) gifts, and (4) transactions in own stock.

Paid-In Surplus. When stock is issued at a premium, the net worth of the corporation is increased by an amount greater than the par value of the capital stock issued. This amount represents paid-in surplus, or an additional investment on the part of the owners. It may therefore be properly considered as a part of the capital surplus of the corporation. Although in some cases the account title "Capital Surplus" is used instead of the title "Pre mium on Capital Stock," the latter is preferable since the source of the proprietorship should always be indicated in a surplus account. 479

[Ch. 20 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Surplus Arising from the Revaluation of Assets. When the value of land or other fixed property has increased, and the present value of the fixed asset is to be recorded in the accounts, the value of the asset may be "written up" to correspond to the value determined by a competent outside appraiser. The credit entry is to a special surplus account, such as Surplus from Revaluation. The balance of this account may be considered a part of the capital surplus, or it may be set up in a separate classification called Reappraisal Surplus.

Surplus from Gifts. Sometimes corporations receive gifts of their own stock from stockholders, gifts of land from chambers of commerce, or gifts from other sources. When such a gift is land or similar property, it is appraised and recorded at a fair market value. Since the resulting increase in the net worth is not due to earnings of the corporation, it should be recorded as capital surplus. The source of the increase in the proprietorship should be indicated by the title of the account, for example, Surplus from 

Gift of Property. 

Surplus from Transactions in Own Stock. When a corporation purchases its own stock in the market at less than par, or sells it at a price greater than par, an increase in proprietorship results which is not due to regular operations of the business. These transactions are treated later in this chapter under the heading 

Treasury Stock. 

Earned Surplus

Accounts 

The principal source of increase in proprietorship is earnings arising from the normal operations of the corporation. These earnings are reported on statements of profit and loss. One function of accounting is to determine as accurately as possible, through the treatment of items appearing on the statement of profit and loss, the earnings of a given period. The results of the operations of the period are then summarized in the profit and loss summary account, dividends are debited to it, and the balance of this account is transferred to an account called Earned Surplus Unappropriated.

Gains from the sale of fixed assets may also be recorded as earned surplus. Other debits or credits to the earned surplus unappropriated account result from (1) the correction of errors made in previous periods and (2) the reservation of amounts for special purposes.

Ch. 20] 481 

Corrections for Previous Periods. In spite of every effort to measure accurately the profit or the loss of each given period, there almost always arises the necessity for corrections at a later time. The earned surplus unappropriated account is used in making adjustments that are required by the discovery of errors affecting the profits of previous fiscal periods. The profit or the loss that should be recorded in order to correct the error is not shown in an income or an expense account of the period in which the error has been discovered but is credited or debited to Earned Surplus Unappropriated. In this way the additional gain is credited to the accumulated profits of past periods or the loss is deducted from such profits, and the current earnings, which were in no way affected by the error, are accurately reported. 

For example, the bookkeeper of the Burbank Manufacturing

Company discovered that during the previous fiscal period the purchase of office equipment costing $1,000 was recorded by a debit to the purchases account instead of to the office equipment account. This error resulted in an overstatement of $1,000 in the cost of goods sold. The gross profit and the net profit were therefore understated by that amount. In order to correct the error, the bookkeeper made the following journal entry:

July 10 

Office Equipment. 

Earned Surplus Unappropriated. 

To correct the error of June 15, at which time the cost of office equipment purchased was charged to Pur chases instead of to Office Equipment. 

1,000|00|| 1,000,00

The correction of such errors in the books of the corporation is similar to the method used in sole proprietorships and partnerships. In the latter instances, however, corrections for previous periods are made directly in the capital accounts, whereas in cor porate accounting Earned Surplus Unappropriated is used. 

Surplus Set Aside for Special Purposes. After errors affecting the profits of past fiscal periods have been corrected, as described above, the balance of the profit and loss summary account is closed into the earned surplus unappropriated account. The board of directors is responsible for the administration of earned surplus. The board may set aside a part of the earned surplus so that it will not be available for dividends. This may be done in order that contracts with creditors can be fulfilled, that con-

[Ch. 20 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

tingent losses may be provided for, or that operating policies for the future may be carried out. 

Reservations of earned surplus are known as appropriated surplus. Whatever surplus is not appropriated is available for dividends and is known as unappropriated surplus. 

The corporation may have agreed with creditors to reserve annually out of earnings specific amounts until the obligations have been met. This reservation of earnings prevents such amounts from being paid as dividends and adds to the security furnished creditors. Reserve for Bond Sinking Fund, discussed in the following chapter, is an illustration of such an earned surplus account.

The earned surplus unappropriated account is debited and an appropriately named reserve account is credited periodically for the amount to be withheld. After the obligations have been met, the reserve account may be closed into Earned Surplus Unappropriated. The amounts previously withheld thus become available for dividends.

If a great decline in prices during the next fiscal period is expected, although the inventory is conservatively valued, a part of the accumulated earnings may be set aside by a debit to Earned 

Surplus Unappropriated and a credit to an account with a descriptive title such as Reserve for Possible Inventory Loss. 

When the operations of a corporation are to be expanded, it may be best to do so by reserving earnings rather than borrowing money. The board of directors, in carrying out such a policy, will annually reserve out of earnings predetermined amounts in order that the plans for expansion can be carried out. To illustrate, an additional factory building is to be constructed and equipped at an estimated cost of $60,000. If earnings amounting to $60,000 are to be reserved during the next four years, Earned 

Surplus Unappropriated will be debited and Reserve for Factory 

Extension will be credited annually for $15,000.

Amounts set aside from earned surplus as reserves for special purposes should not be confused with reserves for depreciation and for bad debts. A surplus reserve represents a part of the proprietorship that is set aside so that it will not be distributed as dividends. The reserve may be only temporary; in a later period it may be transferred back to unappropriated surplus and thus be made available for dividends. The equity of the stockholders is

Ch. 20] 483 

not reduced by the creation of the surplus reserve. On the other hand, the reserves for depreciation and for bad debts represent deductions from the values of the corresponding assets and at the same time result in deductions from net worth. 

Cash Dividends

A dividend is ordinarily a distribution of profits to the stockholders. Dividends may be declared annually, although the present tendency is toward quarterly or semiannual dividends. A dividend can be declared legally only by the board of directors and ordinarily only out of realized profits. The stockholders have no claim upon the profits of a corporation until a dividend has been declared. 

The policies of corporations with reference to the amount of the profits to be distributed as dividends vary widely. Corporations that are conservatively managed prefer to retain all earnings over the amount of reasonable dividends as a fund that will be available in case of economic depression or unforeseen contingencies. The funds derived from accumulated earnings are ordinarily used in expanding the business or in building up a larger working capital.

To illustrate the entries required in the declaration of a dividend and the transfer of the balance to Earned Surplus Unappropriated, the following facts are assumed: On December 31, 1939, the earned surplus unappropriated account has a credit balance of $40,000; the profit and loss summary account has a credit balance of $25,500 after providing for income tax; a dividend of $6,000 is declared on preferred stock, and a dividend of $8,000 on common stock. The entries required and the accounts after these entries have been posted are: 

Dec. 31 31

of $6 a share, payable on Jan. 17, 1940, to stockholders of record on 

Jan. 15, 1940, and Common Dividend No. 2 of $8 a share, payable on 

Jan. 18, 1940, to stockholders of record on Jan. 16, 1940.

Profit and Loss Summary.. 

Earned Surplus Unappropriated.

Earned Surplus Unappropriated.

Three dates attach themselves to each dividend declaration: (1) the date of declaration, (2) the date of "stockholders of record," and (3) the date of payment. Since corporate shares are so freely transferable, it is necessary to know which shareholders are entitled to the dividend checks. The law requires that the date of record follow the date of declaration, in order that the parties to transfers may know the dividend situation at all times. In the preferred and common dividends illustrated above, the "dates of record" were January 15 and January 16 respectively, both following the date of declaration.

When a dividend is declared by the board of directors, it becomes a liability of the company to the stockholders and must be recorded as such at once. A separate account is usually opened for each dividend declared. These accounts are numbered serially. 

Thus the ledger may contain accounts called Dividend Payable 

No. 1, Dividend Payable No. 2, etc. If a corporation has both preferred and common stock, the dividend declarations are shown on the books in separate accounts, as Preferred Dividend Pay able No. 4 and Common Dividend Payable No. 2.

Ch. 20] 485 

On the balance sheet unpaid dividends are usually reported in one total as a current liability entitled "Dividends Payable." 

If a dividend is not paid when due, a stockholder, like any other creditor of the corporation, may bring legal action against the company.

Although there is no contractual obligation on the part of a corporation to pay a dividend on cumulative preferred stock until it has been declared, it is considered good policy to show on the balance sheet the amount of cumulative preferred dividends in arrears. This may be done by adding to the balance sheet a footnote stating the amount of the dividends in arrears. 

Stock Dividends 

Dividends need not be declared payable in cash. They may be paid in various forms. 

They are sometimes paid in the stock of the corporation. Such dividends are known as stock dividends. For example, if a corporation has earned surplus of $100,000 and wishes to distribute this amount to its stockholders in the form of stock, the entries to record the declaration and the payment of this dividend are: 

Mar. 5

Earned Surplus Unappropriated. 

Stock Dividend Payable... 

100,000|00|| 

100,000 00

To record the declaration of a stock dividend. 25 

Stock Dividend Payable... 

100,000/00

Capital Stock.. 

100,000/00

To record the payment of the stock dividend.

A stock dividend has no effect on the total equity of the stockholders. The total proprietorship of the stockholders is merely divided into a greater number of parts. If, in the preceding example, a 100 per cent stock dividend were declared, the number of shares of stock outstanding after the payment of the dividend would be doubled. Each stockholder would then own twice as many shares as he had previously, but the book value of each of his shares would be only one half of the previous book value. From the point of view of the corporation, a stock dividend results in a permanent appropriation of surplus. Once the additional stock has been issued, it becomes a part of the capital investment of the stockholders and that part of the surplus is no longer available for cash dividends.

[Ch. 20 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Scrip and Commodity In addition to cash dividends and stock Dividends dividends, corporations occasionally distribute scrip dividends and commodity dividends. When a scrip dividend is declared, the stockholder receives a promise of the payment of cash at a later date. If a corporation had earnings not yet realized in cash, it might make use of the scrip dividend, having the scrip call for payment when the corporation will have realized its cash. Ordinarily, however, it is considered better practice to delay the dividend declaration until cash is available for payment.

Commodity dividends provide for the distribution by the corporation of some other asset than cash. If a corporation owns shares of stock in another corporation, it may declare a dividend payable in such shares. If it had units of merchandise which it could not sell, it might make the dividend payable in such units. 

Such dividends would be called commodity dividends. 

The Statement of The profit earned by a corporation during 

Surplus a given fiscal period may be distributed as a dividend to the stockholders, or it may be used to increase the proprietary interests of the stockholders. The disposition of the profit of the period may be reported in the proprietorship section of the balance sheet, or it may be indicated on the statement of profit and loss. For example, assume that The Kingsley Corporation earned $50,000 during 1939 and that a dividend amounting to $20,000 was declared on December 14, 1939. This information might be shown on the balance sheet in the following manner: 

PROPRIETORSHIP Capital Stock...

Dividend Declared on Dec. 14, 1939 20,000.00
