30,000 00 

Earned Surplus Unappropriated, Dec. 31, 1939 

80,000 00 

Total Proprietorship... 

330,000 00

The disposition of the profit earned during 1939 might be shown on the statement of profit and loss in the following manner:

Ch. 20] 487 

 Net Profit.. 

50,000 00

Dividend Declared on Dec. 14, 1939. 

20,000 00

Added to Earned Surplus Un appropriated.. 

30,000 00

A preferable method is to prepare a separate statement of surplus, similar to that illustrated in the preceding chapter on page 463. That surplus statement was relatively short. In many corporations, the number of items affecting surplus is larger than appeared in that illustration. Many of these items have already been discussed in this chapter. For convenience, they may be classified into two main groups: (1) those representing the correction of errors affecting the earnings of previous periods and (2) those resulting from transactions of the board of directors affecting surplus.

In a corporation the activities of two managing groups affect the accounts of the enterprise and consequently the statements. 

The officers and their subordinates, who carry on the daily transactions of the company, comprise the first group. The transactions performed by this group are recorded, summarized, and reported to the second group, the board of directors. The board of directors has certain special powers that result in accounting entries. The most important of these is the power to declare dividends. Other powers of the board of directors have to do with carrying out contractual provisions relating to sinking fund reserves, appropriating earnings for specific purposes designated by the board, and approving additional stock issues for cash or for property.

The statement of surplus provides a summary of the corrections applicable to the earnings of previous fiscal periods and of transactions performed by the board of directors. As the number of items affecting surplus becomes larger, the advantage of a separate statement increases. The statement of surplus also serves to tie together the other two corporate statements presented at the end of the fiscal period. A statement showing in detail the changes in the surplus of O. P. Judkins, Inc., is illus- 

I trated on the following page.

ILLUSTRATION No. 73, STATEMENT OF EARNED SURPLUS Method of Handling No-Par-Value Stock 

When no-par-value stock is sold, the amount received is credited to the capital stock account. Since the stock does not have a par value, neither premium nor discount on stock needs to be considered. For example, if the R. F. Monroe Corporation is authorized on June 1, 1940, to

shares*

54,000.00

When subscriptions to stock of no par value are entered in the books, they are recorded at the selling price of the stock, and the credits to Capital Stock Subscribed are entered at the same amount.

Under the laws of some states the directors of a corporation may specify that a portion of the payment for stock of no par value is to be credited to Capital Stock and the remainder to 

Capital Surplus. The portion to be credited to Capital Stock is known as the stated value. In the previous illustration the stated value might have been $20 a share. The entry would then have been: June 1 Cash...

Capital Stock. 

Capital Surplus. 

Sold 2,000 shares of no-par-value stock at $27 a share. The stated value of the stock is $20 a 'share. 

54,000 00 

40,000 00 14,000/00

No-par-value stock that has a stated value is similar to stock that has a par value. The stated value, however, is not fixed by the charter, but is changeable at the discretion of the directors. 

In the previous illustration the directors made the stated value $20 a share. On the occasion of another issue they might change

[Ch. 20 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

it to $30 a share. Such discretion in altering the value of stock does not exist when stock has a par value. 

Treasury Stock 

When a corporation reacquires some of its own stock from its stockholders either by purchase or gift, such stock is known as treasury stock. Since treasury stock has been issued for value by the corporation, it can be sold at any price without the usual limitations on discount. 

Treasury stock should be distinguished from unissued stock, since the latter has never been issued by the corporation and therefore has no accountable value.

Outstanding capital stock may be donated to the corporation by existing stockholders in order that working capital can be obtained through its sale. This practice is especially prevalent in those corporations that have issued all their stock in payment for property. The following illustration will show the method of recording such transactions involving treasury stock. 

The stockholders of a corporation donate 600 shares of stock having a par value of $100 a share. The entry to record the receipt of the stock by the corporation on February 20 is: 

Feb. 20

Treasury Stock. 

Donated Surplus.. 

Received a donation of 600 shares of stock, par value $100, from stockholders. 

60,000 00 

60,000 00 oook 

If all the stock is sold at par on March 25, the following entry will be made: 

Mar. 25 Cash... 60,000,00

Treasury Stock. 

60,00 10,000 00 

Sold 600 shares of treasury stock at par.

After these entries have been posted, the donated surplus account will have a balance of $60,000, which is the profit derived from the donation and sale of the treasury stock. This profit may be considered as part of the capital surplus of the corporation. 

Frequently the stock that has been donated to a corporation cannot be sold at par. If, in the case given above, the stock were sold at $90 a share, the entry would be as follows:

Ch. 20] 491 

Mar. 25 Cash.. 54,000 0 

Donated Surplus. 

6,000 00

Treasury Stock.. 

60,000 00 

Sold 600 shares of treasury stock, par value $100, at $90 a share. 

The donated surplus account would then have a balance of $54,000, which would be the increase in proprietorship resulting from the gift.

If only 300 shares had been sold at $90 a share, the entry would have been as follows: 

Mar. 25 Cash...

Donated Surplus.. 

Treasury Stock.. 

27,000 00 3,000/00 30,000 00 

Sold 300 shares of treasury stock, par value $100, at $90 a share. 

If donated stock is sold at a premium, the donated surplus. account is credited for the amount of the premium in order to show the increased value of the gift to the corporation. 

More frequently a corporation purchases its own stock. In this case the treasury stock account is debited for the par value of the stock purchased and credited for the par value of the stock sold. 

Any difference between the par value of the stock and the purchase price is debited to an account called Premium on Treasury 

Stock Purchased or credited to an account called Discount on

Treasury Stock Purchased. These are considered capital surplus accounts. When the stock is sold, the par value is credited to the treasury stock account, and any difference between the par value and the selling price is debited to Discount on Treasury Stock 

Sold or credited to Premium on Treasury Stock Sold.

For example, a corporation purchases 500 shares of its own stock (par value $100) for $40,000. The entry to record this purchase on April 15 is: 

Apr. 15

Treasury Stock.. 

50,000 00 Cash....

Discount on Treas. Stock Purchased. 

Purchased 500 shares of stock, par value $100, from the stockholders 40,000 00 10,000/00 at $80 a share.

[Ch. 20 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

If the stock is sold later, on May 10, for $55,000, the entry to record the transaction is: 

May 10 Cash..

Treasury Stock.. 

Premium on Treasury Stock Sold.. 

Sold 500 shares of treasury stock, purchased on April 15, at $110 a share. 55,000 00 50,000/00 

5,000 00 #HI When the treasury stock that has been acquired has no par value, Treasury Stock may be debited for either the original selling price (the amount received for the particular lot of stock at the time it was issued) or an amount based on the average value of all the stock issued, this value being determined by dividing the amount received from sales of the stock by the total number of shares issued. The difference between the value assigned to the treasury stock and the purchase price is debited to 

Premium on Treasury Stock Purchased or credited to Discount on

Treasury Stock Purchased. When the no-par-value stock is sold, Treasury Stock is credited for the value previously determined and any difference between that value and the selling price is debited to Discount on Treasury Stock Sold or credited to Pre mium on Treasury Stock Sold. 

The treasury stock account is a valuation account affecting the stock issued account. The difference between stock issued and treasury stock should show stock outstanding. By valuing treasury stock at par or at the average issuing price in the case of no par, the three stock items mentioned above are valued at the same unit price. For example: Assume that Baer & Bain, Inc., issued 10,000 shares of no-par common at $10 per share and later 10,000 shares additional at $12 per share. It later acquired 1,000 shares by purchase at $15 per share. The journal entry for the purchase of the treasury stock would be: 

May 10

Treasury Stock... 

Premium on Treasury Stock Purchased 

Cash... 

Purchased 1,000 shares of no-par treasury stock at $15 a share. 11,000/00 

4,000 00 

15,000 00

The value of $11 per share applied to the treasury stock in the preceding entry is obtained as follows:

Ch. 20] 493 

10,000 shares at $10. $100,000 

10,000 shares at $12. 120,000 $220,000 

20,000 shares.

The average issuing price is $11 per share. On the balance sheet of the corporation the items of capital stock would appear 

Capital Stock (20,000 shares issued). 

Less Treasury Stock (1,000 shares). $220,000 11,000 

Capital Stock Outstanding (19,000 shares)..... $209,000

The unit price of issued, treasury, and outstanding stock is uniform, $11 per share.

Treasury stock is considered by some corporations to be an asset in the form of a temporary investment, and is reported as such on the balance sheet. This is particularly true when the stock has been purchased for resale. For example, a corporation may purchase its own stock on the open market for the purpose of selling it later to employees on the installment plan. It is considered more desirable, however, to treat treasury stock as a minus proprietorship item, or, in other words, as a deduction from the capital stock outstanding.

Income Tax and Cor- Of the three forms of business organiza porate Statements tions, only the corporation has accounts. that are affected by income tax legislation. Since the payment of income tax is made in the year following that for which the tax is assessed, the balance sheet of a corporation will show a current liability for this tax until payment has been made. Among the current liabilities on such a balance sheet, there is commonly an item that may be termed Provision for Income Tax, Reserve for Income Tax, or Income Tax Payable. Since the tax is payable within a year, it is classified as a current liability. 

Income tax differs from other taxes in that, by provision of the law, it is not an expense of the business. The law is worded in such a way that income tax represents a participation by the taxing authority in the profits of the corporation. The government is the first participant in the distribution of earnings. For example, if the earnings of a corporation for one year are $100,000

[Ch. 20 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

and the income tax rate on corporations is 162 per cent, the amount of income tax payable during the following year will be $16,500. The balance of $83,500 will then be available for dividends and other appropriations of surplus, and as unappropriated surplus. The entry to record the income tax liability in this case will be: 

Dec. 31

Profit and Loss Summary. 

Provision for Income Tax. 

To record the income tax liability.

16,500 0 50000 16,500 00 It should be noted that the effect of income tax is similar to that of property taxes in that the tax reduces the amount of profit available for distribution to the owners. On the statement of profit and loss, however, the income tax is not treated in the same way as property taxes. Because of the way in which it is calculated (that is, as a percentage of net income), and because of the interpretation placed on it by the law, income tax is reported on the statement of profit and loss as a deduction from net income rather than as an operating expense. The method of reporting income tax on the statement of profit and loss is shown below. 

Total Net Income before Provision for Income Tax.

Less Provision for Income Tax. 

Net Income.. 

100,000,00 16,500 00 83,500 00

CHAPTER XXI CORPORATION SECURITIES 

Corporation Capital and When money is invested in a corporation, Corporation Earnings the corporation is expected to earn profits out of which dividends will be paid to the investors. In previous chapters corporation capital was shown to be contributed largely by stockholders, who participated in corporate profits through dividend declaration. Corporations might issue two kinds of stock, preferred and common, the preferred with a prior but limited claim on earnings and the common with a secondary but unlimited claim on the balance of earnings. If the corporation were highly profitable, the common stock benefited; if it were only moderately profitable, the common stock took the smaller return. 

Because of the ease of transferring stock and the availability of earnings through dividends, corporations have found investors ready to exchange their money for shares of stock. Other corporations have absorbed the property of competitors in exchange for shares of their own stock. The great business of investment banking and the huge volume of trading on the stock exchanges give evidence of the present-day interest in corporation stocks. 

The corporation has become the great capital assembling unit of modern business.

Since some investors prefer a guaranteed income in the form of interest and an assurance of the return of the investment at some date in the future, many corporations have sought this form of capital through the issuance of corporate bonds. Such capital is, for the corporation, borrowed capital and involves periodic interest payments together with ultimate return of the principal. Because interest payments are more certain, and since creditors have prior claim on corporation assets, the cost of borrowed capital is less than the expected return on preferred and common stock. Interest on bonds is an expense of the corporation and must be paid before income tax and dividends. 

To illustrate the effect of obtaining capital by the methods discussed above, assume that a beginning corporation needs 503

[Ch. 21 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

$4,000,000 in cash to provide the capital necessary to carry on operations. If the capital is obtained through the sale of 40,000 shares of $100 par common stock and the average annual earnings for the next ten years are $480,000 subject to an income tax of 16% per cent, the return to the investors in common stock would be as follows: 

Earnings..

Less Provision for Income Tax. 

Available for Stockholders.. 

$480,000.00 80,000.00 $400,000.00

If all the earnings available were paid as dividends, the dividend rate on common stock would be 10 per cent. 

If, on the other hand, half the capital is obtained through the sale of 20,000 shares of $100 par, 6% preferred stock, the return to investors in common stock, would, with similar earnings, be as follows: 

Earnings..

Less Provision for Income Tax. 

Balance..

Dividends on Preferred Stock. 

$480,000.00 80,000.00 $400,000.00 120,000.00 $280,000.00

Available for Common Stock Dividends... 

If all the earnings available were paid as dividends, the dividend rate on common stock would be 14 per cent. Since half the capital was furnished at 6 per cent, and since the average earnings after provision for income tax was 10 per cent, the common stockholders gain by the difference between average earnings and preferred stock dividends.

Or, in case half the capital is obtained through the sale of $2,000,000 of 42% bonds, one half of the remainder through the sale of 6% preferred stock, with earnings of $480,000, the return to investors in common stock would be as follows: Earnings..

Less Interest on Bonds.. 

Balance.... 

Less Provision for Income Tax (16%% of $390,000). Balance..

Dividends on Preferred Stock.. 

Available for Common Stock Dividends... 

$480,000.00 90,000.00 $390,000.00 65,000.00 $325,000.00 60,000.00 $265,000.00

CORPORATION SECURITIES 

Since there is only $1,000,000 of common stock outstanding in this case, the dividend rate on common would be 26½ per cent if all the earnings were declared as dividends.

These illustrations show the advantage to common stockholders of having corporation capital obtained through bonds and preferred stocks, if the corporation earns on an average more than the cost of bonds or the return on preferred. But, if the average earnings are less than the interest rate on bonds or the return on preferred, the common stock carries the risk of loss of return. In the previous example, if the earnings had been $240,000 instead of $480,000, and half the capital had been furnished by 6% preferred stock, then after the payment of the income tax, the balance of $200,000 would have gone $120,000 to preferred and $80,000 to common. The rate on common would have been 4 per cent. Or, if bonds and preferred stock had been used, as above, the return to investors in common would have been as follows: Earnings

Fixed or Long-Term 

Liabilities

If a corporation seeks additional funds through the issue of bonds, the transaction gives rise to fixed or long-term liabilities. The fixed liabilities of most frequent occurrence are mortgages payable and bonds payable. During recent years another fixed liability has appeared with increasing frequency on the balance sheets of corporations. This liability is long-term notes. The accounts necessary to record these fixed liabilities will be discussed briefly. 

Mortgages Payable

The most simple form of long-term borrowing is that effected by means of the mortgage. A mortgage gives the creditor a preferred claim on some particular asset. If the note that accompanies the mortgage is not paid at the specified time, the creditor has, through this preferred claim, the right to bring an action that may result in the sale of the asset to satisfy the debt. Mortgages may be classified

[Ch. 21 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

as real-estate mortgages and chattel mortgages. A mortgage of the former class gives the creditor a preferred claim on real estate as security for a debt, evidenced by an accompanying note; whereas a chattel mortgage gives him a preferred claim on personal property for the same purpose.

The mortgage note is not used extensively by large corporations as a means of obtaining money. This fact is evidenced by the infrequent occurrence of mortgages payable on the balance sheets of such enterprises. The mortgage is used more frequently by sole proprietors, partnerships, and small corporations as a means of obtaining funds with which to purchase real estate. 

The purpose of the mortgage payable account is to show the amount owed on long-term notes that are secured by a mortgage. 

The mortgage payable account is credited with the face value of the notes issued by the business and secured by a mortgage, and debited with payments made on these notes. The balance of the account is the face value of the notes secured by the mortgage and still outstanding against the business. It is reported as a fixed liability on the balance sheet. 

Bonds Payable 

A bond is a promise under seal to pay a definite sum of money at a stated time and to pay interest at a stipulated rate. Certain property is usually pledged as security for the payment of both the principal and the interest, but bonds may be issued without being secured by a particular piece of property, or by a mortgage. 

The purpose of the bonds payable account is to show the face value of the bonds outstanding against the corporation. 

This account is credited with the face value of the bonds issued, and debited with the face value of all bonds redeemed or otherwise retired. When a corporation has several issues of bonds outstanding, a separate account for each issue is maintained. On the balance sheet the amount of each issue may be reported as a separate item; but more frequently only one item of bonds payable is listed, this amount representing the balance of all issues outstanding. This is shown as a fixed liability. 

The title of the bonds payable account should give the life of the bonds, the date they come due, the interest rate, and how often interest is paid. If a corporation has only two or three issues, each may very well be shown separately on the balance sheet.

Ch. 21] 507 

CORPORATION SECURITIES 

But if it has many issues, many of them small, they may be combined into one item on the balance sheet and the detail given in a supporting schedule.

Bonds are classified in various ways. As to the mode of interest payment, they may be registered or coupon bonds. If registered, the interest check is made out to the registered party; if coupon, the coupon is clipped and sent in for payment. As to security, bonds may be secured, as explained above, or unsecured. 

As to payment of principal, they may call for payment of a definite amount at a definite date in the future, or they may provide for payment at a time determined by lot, or they may be convertible into some other form of security at the option of the corporation or the holder. 

Bond Premium and Discount

Bonds are sometimes sold for more than their face value. If a business has unusually high credit or offers an exceptionally high rate of interest, buyers may be willing to pay more than the face value for its bonds. For example, on January 2, 1940, Bland & Company sell $100,000 worth of bonds, which are due in five years and bear 6 per cent interest, at 105. The transaction is recorded as follows: 

Jan. 2 Cash...

Bonds Payable. 

Premium on Bonds Payable... 

105,000 00 100,000/00 5,000/00 

Sold $100,000 worth of bonds, due in five years and bearing 6 per cent interest, at 105.

The investors paid this premium because they were willing to lend money to the corporation at less than the contract rate. 

In other words, the prevailing interest rate is less than the contract rate for this particular issue of bonds. The adjustment to the actual yield of the bonds so that the investors will receive only the prevailing rate of return is effected by having the investors pay in advance for the privilege of receiving a return in excess of the prevailing rate. Bland & Company have incurred two liabilities: one to pay the face value of the bonds ($100,000) in five years, and the other to pay interest at a higher rate than the prevailing rate.

Since the bond premium has been received because the rate of interest to be paid is higher than the prevailing rate, it may be

[Ch. 21 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

considered as a liability. At the time of each interest payment the corporation will pay to the bondholders interest at the contract rate. Not all of this payment, however, will represent actual interest cost to the corporation, as a portion of the premium already received may be considered as an offset against the interest payment.

In the case of Bland & Company the bonds will mature in five years. Hence the premium may be divided into five equal parts to offset the interest payments for the five years. The entries made at the end of each year to record the payment of interest and the offset of the premium against interest cost will be as follows: 

Dec. 31 Interest Cost.. Cash. 

Paid interest at 6 per cent on the bonds outstanding. 6,000,00 

6,000 00 31 

Premium on Bonds Payable*. 

1,000 00 

Interest Cost.. 1,000 00 

To write off one fifth of the premium received on the sale of bonds.

A single compound entry might be made instead of the two entries given above. 

Dec. 31 Interest Cost.

Premium on Bonds Payable. 

Cash...

To record the payment of interest at 6 per cent on the bonds outstanding, and the amount of the premium that offsets this payment. 5,000/00 1,000 00 

6,000 00

The balance of the premium on bonds payable account, which represents the amount of the bond premium unamortized, may be reported on the balance sheet as a liability immediately after the bonds payable.

Bonds may also be sold at a discount. For example, on 

January 2, 1940, Bland & Company sell five-year 4 per cent bonds with a face value of $100,000 at 95. This transaction is recorded 

*In the illustrations in this chapter bond premium and bond discount are divided equally over the period for which the bonds are issued. Exact amounts that take into consideration compound interest may be obtained from published tables.

CORPORATION SECURITIES 

Jan. 2 Cash...

Discount on Bonds Payable. 

Bonds Payable.. 

95,000 00 5,000 100 000 00 

Sold $100,000 worth of five-year, 4 per cent bonds at 95.

The discount on the bonds represents a deferred charge to interest cost. The corporation has contracted to repay at maturity an amount greater than it received in cash. In return, however, it will pay at the end of each year interest at a lower rate than the prevailing one. The discount may be considered as analogous to interest paid in advance when a note is discounted. 

Although, at the time of each interest payment, the corporation will pay interest at only the contract rate, the real interest cost will be more. In order that the proper interest cost will be recorded, a share of the bond discount must be distributed to each period. The entry made at the end of each year to record the payment of interest and the amount of bond discount pertaining to the payment may be as follows: 

Dec. 31 Interest Cost.. Cash...

Discount on Bonds Payable. 

To record the payment of interest at 4 per cent on the bonds outstanding, and the amount of the discount pertaining to this payment. 

5,000 00 

4,000 00 1,000 00 Br The balance of the discount on bonds payable account is usually reported on the balance sheet as a deferred charge to expense. It is considered more logical, however, to treat it as a valuation account affecting bonds payable. 

Nature of Bond

The nature of bond premium can be illus- 

Premium and Bond trated by emphasizing the movement of Discount illustrated cash incident to the bond issue. When a premium is received on bonds issued, the movement of cash inward is that much greater than the movement of cash outward when the bonds are paid. The interest payments during the life of the bonds must absorb this difference. 

For example: Hoyt Inc. issues $100,000 of 10-year, 7% bonds at $110. The corporation therefore receives $110,000 in cash at

[Ch. 21 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

the time the bonds are issued. During the life of the bonds the corporation will pay $7,000 annually as interest on the bonds, or a total of $70,000. When the bonds come due at the end of the tenth year, the corporation pays the bondholders $100,000. Considering the above transactions and their relation to cash, the account for cash might be set up as follows:

CASH 

Cash received from sale of 

7% bonds 110,000/00 

Cash paid for interest during 10 years. 

Principal paid.. 

70,000 00 100,000/00

The result is a net decrease in cash over the life of the bonds amounting to $60,000, which is the cost of the bond deal. Distributed over ten years, this gives an annual cost of $6,000, or 6 per cent, on the value of bonds outstanding. Hence, such a transaction is spoken of as a 7 per cent bond in a 6 per cent market, and the additional amount received at the time the bonds are sold is called premium on bonds payable.

If the corporation issued 10-year, 5% bonds, it should receive in a 6% market $90,000 for the bonds. Such a sale would involve a discount on bonds payable. The account involving cash received from the sale of the bonds and paid as interest and principal would appear as follows:

CASH 

Cash received from sale of 

5% bonds.. 90,000/00 

Cash paid for interest during 10 years. 

Principal paid... 50,000/00 

100,000 00

The net cash difference here is also $60,000, or $6,000 a year, which amounts to 6 per cent on the amount issued. 

Sinking Fund and When bonds are issued, it is frequently Sinking Fund Reserve with the provision that a definite amount is to be set aside each year to provide for their retirement at maturity. The amounts set aside form a sinking fund. The total amount of this fund should, upon the maturity of the bonds, equal the face value of the bonds. The fund may be in cash, although it consists more commonly of investments in marketable securities. 

The control of this fund may be in the hands of a third party, known as a trustee.

Ch. 21] 511 

CORPORATION SECURITIES 

When an amount is transferred to the sinking fund, an account called Bond Sinking Fund is debited and the cash account is credited. For example, the Royal Manufacturing Company issues 20-year bonds amounting to $100,000, with the provision that $5,000 is to be set aside in each of the 20 years to provide a sinking fund for the retirement of the bonds at maturity. The entry to record the annual transfer of an amount to this fund is: 

Dec. 131

Bond Sinking Fund.. 

Cash... 

To transfer to the sinking fund for bonds an amount equal to one twentieth of the face value of the bonds. 5,000/00 5,000/00

The balance of the bond sinking fund account may be reported as an asset on the balance sheet. 

Not uncommonly there is the provision that a reserve for the bond sinking fund shall be established out of profits. This reserve is established to reduce the amount of earned surplus available for dividends and thus to make greater the security back of the bonds. An entry must therefore be made to transfer from the surplus account to a new account, called Reserve for Bond Sinking Fund, a part of the profits equal to the amount transferred to the bond sinking fund. For example, if, in the case of the Royal 

Manufacturing Company, a reserve for the bond sinking fund is established from profits, the following entry, in addition to that shown above, must be made each year: Dec.

Earned Surplus Unappropriated. 

Reserve for Bond Sinking Fund.. 

To transfer a part of the earned surplus to the reserve for the bond sinking fund. 

5,000 00 5,000/00

During the life of the bonds the bond sinking fund and the reserve for the bond sinking fund increase each year; hence at the end of the 20 years each is equal to $100,000, the face value of the bonds. The bond sinking fund is then used to pay the bonds. 

This transaction is recorded as follows: 

2 Bonds Payable. 

Jan. | | 

Bond Sinking Fund.. 

Paid the bonds outstanding. 

100,000 100,000 00

[Ch. 21 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

This entry closes the bond sinking fund and the bonds payable accounts, but the reserve for bond sinking fund account has a credit balance of $100,000. This reserve represents profits of previous years that were set aside so that they could not be distributed as dividends. It will be observed that the reserve for the bond sinking fund has no direct relation to the bond sinking fund itself but that its use is simply a means of insuring an increasing security back of the bonds. After the bonds have been paid, the reserve is no longer necessary. It can therefore be trans ferred to Earned Surplus Unappropriated, and this part of the profits can be distributed as dividends or used in any other way that the board of directors may choose. The entry to transfer the reserve to the earned surplus unappropriated account is: 

Jan. 2

Reserve for Bond Sinking Fund.. 

Earned Surplus Unappropriated. 

To transfer the reserve to the earned surplus unappropriated account. 100,000/00 100,000,00

Long-Term Notes 

Corporations often issue notes for periods that are relatively shorter than the life of the average bond, but that are longer than the time of the ordinary note given to a bank or a trade creditor. The time of these notes is usually from two to five years from the date of issue. The purpose of issuing such notes is usually to obtain fixed capital at a time when the investment market is not considered favorable for an issue of long-term bonds. At the same time, however, the corporation usually expects to issue bonds to take the place of the notes when interest rates are more favorable to securities of longer duration. These notes are very similar to bonds except in the length of time for which they are issued, the similarity being particularly noticeable in the manner in which both are issued and marketed. Long-term notes are usually not secured by mortgages, but, on the contrary, depend for their salability on the earning power and the general credit of the company. 

The account maintained with an issue of long-term notes is. similar to that maintained with any issue of bonds. The title of the account should indicate the nature of the issue. The balance is reported as a fixed liability on the balance sheet.

Ch. 21] 513 

CORPORATION SECURITIES 

Stocks and Bonds Held Securities-that is, stocks and bonds-that as Assets appear on the balance sheets of the issuing corporations as proprietorship and fixed liabilities are assets on the balance sheets of the businesses that own them. Corporations may invest their surplus funds in securities of other corporations because they expect to receive dividends or interest on such securities. These assets are reported on the balance sheet under the heading "Investments." This classification is distinct from those of current assets and fixed assets. 

When the stock of another corporation is acquired, it is recorded at the total cost, which includes the price of the stock, the commission paid the broker, and taxes or other expenses incident to acquisition. A dividend received on such stock is treated as income of the period in which the dividend was declared. The stock remains in the ledger at the cost value until it is sold. The present market value of the stock may, however, be shown on the balance sheet in a footnote or in brackets after the title of the account. Because market values fluctuate constantly, it is considered better practice not to change the value of the asset in the ledger. 

For example, assume that the Atlantic Investment Corporation purchased 1,000 shares of General Manufacturing Co. 7% preferred stock at $125 % per share, that the commission on the purchase was $250, and that taxes and other costs amounted to $62.75. The journal entry would be: 

Jan. 6 General Mfg. Co., 7% Preferred Stock. Cash... 

To record the purchase cost of 1,000 shares of General Manufacturing Co. 

Purchase price at $125% $125,375.00 

Commission on purchase 

Taxes and other costs...

250.00 62.75 $125,687.75 125,687|75 125,687 75 The amount debited to the investment account is the total of all charges incident to acquiring the stock. 

When the bonds of another corporation are acquired, they must be treated differently. The holder of a bond has a claim for a definite amount that is to be paid upon the maturity of the bond and also a claim for interest, which is to be paid at stated intervals. 

Since the face value of the bond is the amount to be realized at maturity, the bond must be recorded at this amount in the ac-

[Ch. 21 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

counts. This value must be recorded even though the amount actually paid for the bond may be more or less than the face value. 

Bonds are sometimes purchased at their face value. When purchased at an amount greater than the face value, they are said to be bought at a premium; when purchased at an amount less than the face value, at a discount. Since, at the time of maturity, they must appear in the accounts at their face value, the premium or the discount must be recorded separately. 

If a bond for $1,000, payable 10 years hence, is purchased at $1,100, it will produce only $1,000 at maturity. The purchaser is willing, however, to pay $1,100 because the rate of interest on the bond is greater than the prevailing rate. In this case it is desirable to spread the premium over the life of the bond as a reduction in the interest actually received on the bond. purchase of the bond is therefore recorded as follows: 

Jan. 2 Bonds Receivable (X 7%). 

Premium on Bonds Receivable. 

Cash... 

To record purchase of 7% bonds at $110.

The 1,000/00 100 00 1,100 00 When the annual interest is received, the following entry is made: 

Dec. 131 Cash. 

Interest Earned.

Premium on Bonds Receivable. 

To record receipt of interest and amortize the premium of $100 over the 10-year life of the bond. 70/00 6000 1000 By this means the true earning on the investment is recorded each year. At the end of 10 years the premium on the bond will have been written off completely. The bond will appear in the ledger at its face value, which is the amount that will be received in payment of it. On the balance sheets made during the life of the bond, the face value of the bond and the balance of the premium account will be reported under the heading "Investments."

When bonds are purchased at a discount, exactly the reverse condition to that just illustrated exists. If a bond for $1,000, payable 10 years hence, is purchased for $900, it will produce $1,000 at maturity. The issuing company is willing, however, to pay, at the maturity of the bond, more than it has received because the bond provides for interest at a lower rate than the current rate. In this case the investor should spread the discount

Ch. 21] 515 

CORPORATION SECURITIES 

over the life of the bond as an addition to the interest actually received on the bond. The purchase of the bond is recorded by debiting Bonds Receivable for the face value ($1,000), crediting Discount on Bonds Receivable for the discount ($100), and crediting Cash for the purchase price ($900). When the interest on the bond is received annually, Cash is debited and Interest Earned is credited. At the same time Discount on Bonds Receivable is debited for $10, and Interest Earned is credited for the same amount. At the end of 10 years the discount on the bond will have been written off completely. The bond will appear in the ledger at its face value, which is the amount that will be received in payment of it. On the balance sheets made during the life of the bond, the balance of the discount account may be reported as a deferred credit to income, or as a subtraction from the face value of the bond.

At the time bonds are purchased, there may be accrued interest that is not yet due, and the purchaser may be charged for this. The amount accrued may be recorded as a debit to the interest earned account. When interest is received at the end of the first year, the amount accrued at the time of purchase is thereby deducted from the amount received, leaving the remainder as the amount earned.

The market price of bonds held as a permanent investment is not recognized in the accounts. Since the bonds are not to be sold, the market has no effect on their value to the firm. On the balance sheet the market price may be shown in a footnote or in brackets after the title of the account. 

When bonds are purchased only as a temporary investment and are to be sold before maturity, they should be entered in the books at cost. This procedure is similar to that followed when stock is purchased for the same purpose. In this case the purchaser of bonds may be compared to the purchaser of stock, no maturity claim being anticipated by either purchaser. 

Investment Trusts

Corporations formed to buy shares of stock in other corporations, for the purpose of pooling the dividend returns, are called investment trusts. By pooling the dividend returns, is meant the receiving of various dividend payments from various corporations, and out of those receipts declaring an investment trust dividend to the stockholders of the investment trust. To illustrate: The Atlantic Investment

[Ch. 21 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Trust has invested its capital in the stocks of Corporations A, B, C, D, E, and F. In the year 1939 the dividend rates on these stocks are 9%, 2%, 6%, 8%, 4%, and 0%. The dividends received by the Atlantic Investment Trust constitute the income from which it may pay dividends. The rate it can pay will tend to average the high and low rates paid by Corporations A to F. The investment trust, by a distribution of its stock holdings, can average the return for its own shareholders.

The service of averaging uneven dividend returns of industrial, public utility, and other stocks appeals particularly to the small investor. It relieves him of the risk of low dividends, or none at all, which he would have to carry if he invested directly. The investment trust is often large enough to employ experts to advise on the investment of funds. If restricted to "pooling" of dividends, it would render a useful service to small investors. 

The Balance Sheet of a Accounts that are characteristic of the cor- 

Corporation porate form of organization have been discussed in this and the three preceding chapters. A balance sheet containing the balances of such accounts is given in Illustration No. 74 on page 517. This balance sheet shows the financial condition of the General Trading Corporation at the end of the second year of operation. The significance of each item reported on the statement should be readily apparent. Several factors are, however, worthy of special note. (1) Subscriptions to 500 shares of 7 per cent cumulative preferred stock with a par value of $100 were received at $112, payment by the subscribers to be made in four equal installments. The original amount of the subscriptions receivable on this stock was therefore $56,000, the par value of the preferred stock ($50,000) plus the premium ($6,000). The balance sheet indicates that the first payment of 25 per cent on the subscriptions has been made, for the balance of the subscriptions receivable account is reported at $42,000. This item is listed among the current assets. The balance of Preferred Stock Subscribed, the account that shows the amount of the stock subscribed for but not yet issued, is $50,000, the par value of the stock. This amount is added to the value of the preferred stock outstanding. The premium on the preferred stock, $6,000, is shown under "Capital Surplus." (2) Organization expense, which was originally $5,000, is reported at $3,000, two fifths of the expense incident to incorporation

and organization having been written off during the two years by a charge to Earned Surplus Unappropriated. Organization expense is presented under the heading "Intangible Assets." (3) Dividends on common stock are payable semiannually; those on preferred stock, quarterly. The account Common-Stock 

Dividend Payable No. 4 indicates the fourth dividend declared on common stock since incorporation, and the account Preferred- 

Stock Dividend Payable No. 8 indicates the eighth dividend de clared on preferred stock. On December 24, 1939, a dividend of 

25 cents a share was declared payable on January 12, 1940, to common stockholders of record on December 29, 1939. Since 

18,000 shares of common stock were outstanding on that date (20,000 shares of stock issued, minus 2,000 shares of treasury stock), the dividend is $4,500. At the same time a dividend of 

134 per cent, or $1,750, was declared on the preferred stock outstanding. Both dividends are listed on the balance sheet as current liabilities. (4) On January 2, 1939, bonds bearing 7 per cent interest and payable on December 31, 1948, were issued. The bonds have a face value of $100,000 but were issued at 110. The bonds payable are therefore reported as a fixed liability at their face value of $100,000. The premium of $10,000 received on the issue was reduced by one tenth on December 31, 1939, since it is to be amortized over the 10-year life of the bonds. The balance of the premium is also shown as a fixed liability. 

The agreement of the bondholders with the corporation provides that $10,000 in cash is to be deposited annually with a trustee to provide a sinking fund for the retirement of the bonds and that $10,000 is also to be transferred annually, before any dividends are declared, from Earned Surplus Unappropriated in order to provide a reserve for the bond sinking fund. That the first provision has been complied with can be seen by reference to the balance of the bond sinking fund account, which is listed as $10,000 under "Investments." Compliance with the second provision is evidenced by the balance of the reserve for bond sinking fund account, which is reported at $10,000 under the heading "Earned Surplus Appropriated." (5) The common stock has no par value and is therefore reported at the amount received for it. The total of $196,000 on the balance sheet represents sales as follows:

The corporation reacquired in December, 1939, 2,000 shares of the common stock at $9 a share, or $18,000. This stock was reacquired because of a contract to sell the president 5,000 shares of common stock in 1941 at $8.50 a share. The treasury stock is shown on the balance sheet at an amount based on the value of the common stock that has been sold. This amount is $19,600. ($196,000 divided by 20,000 equals $9.80, the average value of a share of common stock; $9.80 times 2,000 equals $19,600, the value of the treasury stock.) The difference between the amount paid ($18,000) and the value assigned to the treasury stock ($19,600) is $1,600, the discount on the treasury stock. On the balance sheet the value of the treasury stock is deducted from the amount of the common stock sold. The discount on the treasury stock is shown as part of the capital surplus of the corporation. (6) The stock of Howard Meyers and Son, Inc., which was purchased by the General Trading Corporation, is reported at its cost under the caption "Investments." Stock that the General

Trading Corporation issued and then purchased was not, however, reported as an asset but as a deduction from the amount of common stock sold. (7) The net income of the General Trading Corporation for the year ending on December 31, 1939, was $48,000. The first charge against this income was $6,000 for income tax. An entry was therefore made charging Profit and Loss Summary and cred iting Income Tax Payable. The following tabulation indicates the disposition of the net income for 1939:

(8) Plans were made in 1939 to expend $60,000 in 1943 on plant extension. The balance of the account Reserve for Plant 

Extension ($15,000) represents one fourth of this amount, which was appropriated from the earnings of 1939. This item is listed under the caption "Earned Surplus Appropriated.' 

Surplus is divided into three classes on the balance sheet of the General Trading Corporation: capital surplus, earned surplus appropriated, and earned surplus unappropriated. The last item represents surplus that can be distributed as dividends or used in any other way that the board of directors may choose. 

1. Each of two corporations with about equal credit ratings issues bonds amounting to $100,000. The A Corporation offers bonds with 6 per cent interest and sells them above par. The B Corporation offers bonds with 5 per cent interest and sells them below par. (a) Does the A Corporation gain by selling its bonds above par? (b) Does the B Corporation lose by selling its bonds below par? (c) How will each of the corporations determine the annual interest cost on the bonds? 

2. The Curry Corporation purchases ten 20-year, 52 per cent bonds of the Lewis Manufacturing Company. It plans to retain these bonds until maturity. The bonds have a par value of $10,000, but they are purchased for $9,000. Give the entries made in the books of the Curry Corporation (a) when the bonds are purchased, (b) when the yearly interest is received, and (c) when payment of the bonds is received. 

3. The Curry Corporation also purchases ten 20-year, 6½ per cent bonds of the Baker Publishing Company. It plans to retain these until maturity. The bonds have a par value of $10,000, but they are purchased for $11,000. Give the entries made in the records of the Curry Corporation (a) when the bonds are purchased, (b) when the yearly interest is received, and (c) when payment of the bonds is received. 

4. Give the entries made in the records of the Lewis Manufacturing Company (a) when the bonds mentioned in Problem No. 2 are issued, (b) when the yearly interest is paid, and (c) when the bonds are paid.

(a) What will be the balances of the bond sinking fund and the reserve accounts at the end of 10 years? (b) How will they be reported on the balance sheet prepared at that time? (c) What entries will be necessary when the bonds are paid?

The income tax rate was 18%. There was appropriated a sinking fund reserve of $15,000 for plant extension. Dividends declared and paid during 1939 amounted to $16,000. Preliminary Instructions. The purpose of this practice set is to provide material that will give practice in the application of the principles discussed in preceding chapters. The set represents a retail clothing business conducted during the month of December by a partnership. The business is incorporated on January 2 of the following year. With the exception of transactions affecting the proprietorship accounts, the transactions are the same as those of a business conducted by a sole proprietorship. Although the transactions are typical of those completed by a retail clothing business, the purpose of the set is not to illustrate the methods and the records of a particular type of business, but rather the general principles followed by all businesses.

Controlling accounts with accounts receivable and accounts payable are maintained in the general ledger. The individual accounts of customers and creditors are kept in separate subsidiary ledgers.

The books of original entry consist of a general journal; a purchases journal; a cashbook, the receipts side of which has columns headed General Cr., Sales Cr. (for cash sales), Accounts Receivable

Cr., Net Cash Dr., and the payments side, columns headed General 

Dr., Accounts Payable Dr., Purchases Discount Cr., Net Cash Cr.; 

and a petty cash book with columns for miscellaneous selling expense, miscellaneous general expense, and charges to sundry accounts. 

A sales journal is not required for a record of credit sales because the charges to customer's accounts are posted from the duplicate sales tickets directly to the subsidiary ledger accounts. The individual amounts are to be posted at the same time that individual items are posted from the other books of original entry. The student will record, at one posting, only the credit sales that have been completed on or before the date of posting. At the end of each month the credit sales for the month, as shown by the sales tickets, should be totaled, and a general journal entry should be made to credit the sales account and to charge the accounts receivable controlling account. 

At the end of the month the posting is to be proved by a trial balance, and schedules of the subsidiary ledgers are to be prepared. 

In addition, a ten-column work sheet, a balance sheet, and a statement of profit and loss are to be prepared; the accounts are to be adjusted and closed; a post-closing trial balance is to be taken; and reversing entries are to be recorded as of the first day of the next month.

In actual practice, cash would be proved, a deposit made, and the individual accounts posted on each day. Because of the compara- 530

Ch. 21] 531 

tively small number of transactions in the set, however, this work will be completed only at the end of each week and each month. 

Opening Entries. J. L. Powers and P. K. McKee are partners in a retail clothing business that is known as Powers and McKee. At the end of each month each partner withdraws $200 as his salary. 

After the partners' salaries have been deducted from the net income for the month, the balance of the net income is distributed as follows: 

7/12 to J. L. Powers and 5/12 to P. K. McKee. Any withdrawals of merchandise made by a partner are to be charged to an account with the partner in the accounts receivable ledger. At the end of the month the balance of this account is to be closed into the partner's personal account.

Jan. 2. J. L. Powers and P. K. McKee incorporated their retail clothing store under the name of Powers and McKee, Incorporated. 

The charter authorized the issuance of 100 shares of 6% preferred stock, par value $100, and 300 shares of common stock, par value $50. 

In payment of their interests in the business Powers received 30 shares of preferred and 90 shares of common; McKee received 30 shares of preferred and 50 shares of common. The difference between their capital accounts and the par value of the stock issued to them was taken care of by a payment in cash. 

Jan. 2. Sold 30 shares of preferred stock at $110. Received cash and issued the stock. 

Jan. 2. Sold 40 shares of common stock at par. Received cash and issued the stock. 

Jan. 2. Purchased for $10,000 from the Peterson Realty Co. the land and the building in which the business is located. Issued a check for $5,000 and signed a 6% mortgage note for the balance. 

The amount of the mortgage is to be paid at the rate of $500 a year, and interest at 6% is to be paid semiannually, at the end of June and of December. 

The value of the building is estimated at $6,000; and that of the land, at $4,000. (Record the transaction in the general journal; also enter the amount of cash in the cashbook.)

On the statement of profit and loss, classify the expenses into four groups: selling expenses, delivery expenses, building expenses, and general expenses. In the classification building expenses, place all expenses that pertain to the building.

Record all reversing entries that should be made at the beginning of the new fiscal period. A reversing entry need not be made for accrued property taxes.

CHAPTER XXII  

Description of Manu- Thus far, consideration has been given to facturing Operations the accounting procedures used by enterprises that are concerned principally with buying and selling merchandise. The merchandise that these enterprises sell must originate with other enterprises, the principal activity of which is manufacturing. The manufacturer employs labor and uses machinery in converting raw materials into finished goods. His chief activity is changing the form of the commodity, but he also must sell what he manufactures. It is in changing the form of the commodity, in converting raw materials into finished goods, that the manufacturer differs from the merchandiser. 

The commodities purchased by a manufacturer are his raw materials, but they were finished goods to the manufacturer who sold them. Flour is raw material to the baker but finished goods to the miller. Steel plate is raw material to the automobile manufacturer but finished goods to the steel producer. The designation raw materials or finished goods has significance only in connection with a specific manufacturer and his relation to the commodity. 

The extent of business activity in manufacturing is apparent to any one who contemplates the vast variety of changes in form I that take place in producing the commodities and the services used by society. Manufacturing provides work for a large percentage of the employed, uses a large share of the capital equipment of the country, and makes possible a substantial portion of the profit shown on statements of profit and loss. 

The activities of manufacturing are clearly distinguished from those of selling and require separate treatment in the accounts. 

The manufacturer, in producing finished goods from raw materials, incurs some expenses that are not incurred by the merchandiser. 

Expenses incident to the use of machinery loom large on the manufacturing statement. The number of workers employed in the manufacturing enterprise is usually greater than the number employed in the mercantile establishment. An elaborate and extended pay roll characterizes the manufacturing plant. The 543

[Ch. 22 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

machinery that the manufacturer uses requires power, which he must produce himself or purchase from power companies. Specialized machinery also requires special shops for maintenance and repair. The materials that the manufacturer uses must be acquired, stored, and controlled. Hence the manufacturing function usually calls for an organization of men and machinery that is complicated and specialized. 

This more extensive organization naturally adds many items to the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss. New assets and liabilities are valued in new balance sheet accounts, and new operating activities are measured in new expense accounts, which appear on the statement of profit and loss. The general ledger is expanded to take care of these additional accounts, and new subsidiary ledgers account for much of the manufacturing detail. The treatment of these accounts, together with the recording and the summarizing of transactions affecting the accounts, constitutes the subject matter of this chapter and the two following chapters. 

Balance Sheet Accounts Each of the balance sheet classifications contains additional accounts as a result of the manufacturing function. Under "Current Assets" inventory accounts are added; under "Fixed Assets" new accounts for various kinds of machinery and equipment are shown; under "Intangible Assets" accounts with such assets as patents and copyrights may be reported; and under "Deferred Charges" accounts with supplies and prepaid expenses that are characteristic of the factory are shown. The liability accounts of a manufacturing enterprise are similar to those of a trading enterprise. The balance sheet may, however, show accrued liabilities, such as the accrued factory pay roll, that are not common to a trading enterprise. 

There is also greater likelihood that bonds secured by mortgages on fixed assets, such as the manufacturing plant, will appear on the report of a manufacturing enterprise.

The single inventory of merchandise found on the balance sheet of a merchandising business is displaced by three inventories representing goods in the state in which they were acquired, goods in the process of manufacture, and goods in the state in which they are to be sold. These inventories are called respectively raw materials, goods in process, and finished goods. The costs of oper-

Ch. 22] 545 

Eac 2 ating the factory, including labor and the various manufacturing expenses, are added to the cost of the raw materials in determining the cost of goods in process and finished goods. Unlike the selling process, which adds no value to the merchandise inventory, the manufacturing process increases the value of both the goods in process and the finished goods inventories.

Because of the extensive use of machinery in manufacturing, the percentage of capital invested in fixed assets tends to be larger than that of the trading concern. The building or buildings housing this machinery must be adapted to that purpose and are therefore usually owned by the manufacturer. The number of accounts with machinery and equipment is sufficient to warrant the use of a machinery and equipment subsidiary ledger. 

The subsidiary ledger carrying an account with each indier vidual piece of machinery is called the plant ledger. In connection with the heading of each account, space is provided for recording of useful information regarding the particular machine. In this space are commonly indicated the technical title and description of the machine, the size and weight, the name of the vendor from whom f the machine was purchased, the rated output, and other useful res information. Beneath the heading the items that made up the total cost of the machine, such as the price paid the vendor, the transportation cost, and the installation cost, are listed. Space is der also provided for accumulating the periodic increase in the reserve for depreciation applicable to the machine and for recording the ever diminishing book value of the machine. Such a record of ed each machine is thought to give better control over the machinery used and to provide a more accurate determination of the periodic h depreciation charges. den ring acer pear The manufacturer may have exclusive rights known as patents, which possess value during their life. Patents are granted by the Federal Government in order to encourage the invention of mechanical devices. On the balance sheet patents are reported at cost less amortization. The life of a patent is seventeen years. 

The useful life of the patent may often be less because of the subsequent invention of new and improved devices. The estimated annual decrease in the value of the patent becomes a yearly manufacturing expense. Thus a patent acquired at a cost of $50,000 and having an estimated useful life of ten years would be annually reduced in value $5,000. This amount would be charged to the manufacturing cost of each year.

[Ch. 22 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Copyrights, which are exclusive rights to publish written material, are granted for a period of twenty-eight years but often have a useful life of a much shorter period. Because of the uncertainty regarding their useful life, these intangible assets are often written off the books over a short period of time by charges to operations.

Manufacturing activities add a number of new items to the deferred charges. Factory supplies on hand that were purchased to maintain and service machinery, unexpired insurance of various kinds, largely due to the use of machinery, and unused services that assist in manufacturing represent assets commonly found on the balance sheets of manufacturing enterprises. They also entail additional adjusting entries to be made at the end of each accounting period.

Capital Charges and Quite often, especially in accounting for 

Revenue Charges manufacturing enterprises, it becomes difficult to distinguish between expenditures that add to the value of fixed assets and those that are chargeable as current expenses. All expenditures incurred up to the time at which a fixed asset is ready for use may be considered a part of the cost of the asset, and may therefore be debited to the asset account. For example, the following items may be considered as the original cost of a machine purchased for use in a factory: (1) the purchase price as indicated by the purchase invoice, (2) the freight and drayage paid for the transportation of the machine from the place of business of the seller to the factory of the purchaser, (3) the cost of unpacking and setting up the machine, (4) the cost of installation (including the cost of constructing a metal or a concrete base), (5) the cost of testing to determine the usefulness of the machine, and (6) the cost of any changes or alterations necessary before the machine is in proper condition for operation. Similarly, in the original cost of a building may be included all charges involved in the construction. These may include, in addition to the charges for the supplies and labor used in erecting the building, the cost of tearing down the old building, of excavating for the foundation and the basement, of plans and supervision, and all similar charges incurred in the completion of the building. 

After the fixed asset is put into service, only those expenditures that increase its life or efficiency may be added to its value. 

A machine with an estimated life of four years may be overhauled,

Ch. 22] 547 

et or worn parts may be replaced, with the result that the life of the machine may be increased to five years. The cost of the overhauling or the replacement, to the extent of the value added through the increased life of the machine, may be added to the original cost by charging the asset account. Such expenditures are said to be charges to capital. 

As an asset continues in use, some expenditures are necessary to maintain it in an efficient operating condition. A building requires repairs and paint, and taxes and insurance premiums must be paid. Machinery requires repairs from time to time, as well as supplies necessary for its operation, such as oil and waste; taxes and insurance premiums must also be paid. Expenditures of this type, representing a part of the necessary cost of using the asset during the current period, should be charged as part of the current operating expenses of the business. These expenditures are said to be charges to revenue since they are deductible from the revenue of the period.

Unless care is used, capital expenditures and revenue expenditures may be confused. For example, an expenditure which, if its effects were carefully analyzed, would be found to be necessary in the maintenance of a particular asset, may be considered an addition to the value of the asset. On the other hand, a real addition to capital may be mistaken for an operating expense. The distinction between capital expenditures and revenue expenditures is an important one, since the reporting of manufacturing costs, the determination of the profit for each period, and the presentation of the proprietorship from time to time depend on the accuracy with which this distinction is made. 

Statement of Profit and The statement of profit and loss of a manu- 

Loss Accounts facturing enterprise contains groups of items representing selling expenses and general and administrative expenses similar to those of a trading enterprise. In addition, it contains an entirely new group of items representing purely manufacturing activities. This group includes the cost of raw materials put into production; the cost of labor used directly in converting the raw materials into finished goods; and the expenses that arise from labor not used directly and from all the facilities employed in the process. For convenience in analysis, this threefold group is known as materials, direct labor, and overhead. Materials represent the delivered cost of raw materials that are used in producing

[Ch. 22 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

the finished article. Direct labor represents the wages of the workmen who devote their time to converting the raw materials into goods in process and finished goods. Overhead represents all the other expenses of operating the factory that are not included in the first two items. 

The overhead items represent perhaps the most important group in present-day manufacturing. With increasing use of machinery, much of the factory pay roll is related to the care and upkeep of the machinery rather than being applied directly to raw materials. Overhead includes not only taxes, insurance, depreciation, and maintenance incident to the use of machinery but also all expenses of housing the machinery and furnishing satisfactory working conditions for employees. Whatever expenses other than direct materials and labor are involved in the manufacturing process are included in the overhead group. 

The Cost of Goods Since manufacturing functions differ from Manufactured Schedule trading functions, it is customary to separate the statement of profit and loss accounts having to do with manufacturing from those having to do with trading. The manufacturing group is frequently placed in a separate schedule to avoid the presentation of an extended statement of profit and loss. This schedule gives the cost of the goods manufactured during the latest period. It is therefore comparable to that section of the statement of profit and loss of a merchandising enterprise that shows the cost of the goods purchased. When the schedule is used, the statement of profit and loss of the manufacturing concern is similar to that of a trading company except for a reference to the supporting schedule showing the cost of the goods manufactured. Furthermore, the cost of goods purchased is displaced by the cost of goods manufactured, which is taken from the schedule. 

Other schedules may be used to simplify the principal accounting statements by removing detail from them. In large enterprises having many accounts, it is customary to have a number of schedules supporting items on the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss. For example, the cash balance may be supported by a schedule giving the balances on deposit in several banks; the accounts receivable, by a schedule showing the names of the customers and the balances owed; the sales, by a schedule showing

Ch. 22]. 

549 

sales by territories or by commodities. In fact, there is scarcely an item on such statements that may not reasonably be exhibited in greater detail on some schedule. Schedules make the periodic information more flexible and adaptable to the use of the various individuals who have occasion to refer to it. 

In the schedule showing the cost of the goods manufactured, the cost of the raw materials put into the manufacturing process is determined by means of the beginning inventory, the purchases, the freight on the raw materials purchased, the returns and allowdances, and the ending inventory. The labor cost of those factory employees who worked on the raw materials is added to the cost of the raw materials. This labor cost is obtained from an analysis of the factory pay roll. The pay roll ordinarily includes also the wages of other factory workers, such as janitors and machine repair men. This expense is an element of the factory overhead and is therefore listed with the manufacturing expenses. Manufacturing expenses constitute the third item of costs on the schedule and are ordinarily represented by a group of accounts. The most common manufacturing expense accounts are: 

Factory Supervision Factory Clerical Labor Factory General Labor 1 Foremen's Salaries Compensation Insurance Patents Expense Taxes Fire Insurance Repairs on Buildings Repairs on Machinery Depreciation of Buildings Depreciation of Machinery Depreciation of Patterns 

Heat, Light, and Power

Royalties Factory Supplies Used 

Manufacturing expenses are commonly referred to as overhead. They represent expenses incidental to the manufacturing process and include the cost of facilities furnished the workmen to assist them in converting the raw materials into finished goods. 

The cost of the manufacturing operations of any given period is made up of the cost of raw materials consumed, the wages of labor used on the raw materials, and all other expenses incidental to the operation of the factory. With the increased use of machinery this last group represents an ever larger percentage of the total manufacturing costs.

The Period Covered by the Statement of Profit and Loss 

Manufacturing operations frequently make advisable the choice of a fiscal year that differs from the calendar year. It is not uncommon for manufacturing concerns to choose as their fiscal year any period of twelve months that best fits in with the recurring cycle of their manufacturing activities.

[Ch. 22 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

An attempt is usually made to make the close of the fiscal year come at a relatively inactive period. In such a period inventories will be smaller and the opportunities for making the necessary counts are better. For example, if 1940 models of automobiles are introduced in October and the fiscal year ends December 31, 1939, the statement of profit and loss for the year ended December 31, 1939, will be confusing because it covers the end of the 1939 model year and the beginning of the 1940 model year. 

When the fiscal year differs from the calendar year for the reasons mentioned above, it is called the natural business year. 

The use of the natural business year is widely advocated by the profession of accountancy. Its use tends to make the interpretation of statements less complicated and more informative. 

For control of manufacturing operations, it is advisable to have a much shorter period than a year. It is customary therefore to have monthly interim reports for the use of management. Such monthly reports of manufacturing operations serve to keep under closer control the rather widespread activities. When summaries are used for comparative purposes, it is highly desirable to have operating periods of equal length. Because months have unequal numbers of working days, accountants tend to favor the use of thirteen fiscal periods of four weeks each.

Adjusting and Closing 

Manufacturing 

Accounts 

The adjusting of manufacturing accounts is similar to the adjusting of trading accounts. When inventories of supplies, such as factory supplies, exist, and when expenses, such as insurance, have been prepaid, the assets are set up as deferred charges and only the amounts actually consumed are charged to manufacturing. When unrecorded liabilities, such as accrued wages of factory workers and accrued taxes, exist, the liabilities and the related expenses should be provided for by adjusting entries. 

When the accounts of a manufacturing enterprise are to be closed, all the accounts pertaining to the manufacturing process, which are ordinarily presented in the schedule of the cost of goods manufactured, are closed first. It is advisable to close these accounts into an account called Manufacturing Summary. The balance of this account is the cost of the goods manufactured, which corresponds to the cost of the purchases and freight in of a trading enterprise. The balance of the manufacturing summary account is closed into the profit and loss summary account.

The

Ch. 22] 551 

items appearing on the statement of profit and loss are then closed, as in the case of a trading enterprise, in the order in which they appear on the report.

The relation of the manufacturing summary account to the profit and loss summary account is shown below:

Beginning Inventories Materials Purchased Labor Manufacturing Expenses MANUFACTURING SUMMARY Ending Inventories To Profit and Loss Summary PROFIT AND LOSS SUMMARY From Manufacturing Summary 

The items appearing in the manufacturing summary account would be the same as those found on the schedule of Cost of Goods Manufactured. Those appearing in the profit and loss summary account would correspond to the items on the Statement of Profit and Loss.

The Work Sheet of a 

Manufacturing Enterprise An additional pair of columns is added to the work sheet of a manufacturing enterprise in order to segregate the accounts used in determining the cost of the goods manufactured. All items that enter into the cost of the goods manufactured, or that are deductions from the cost of the goods manufactured, are entered in the Manufacturing Schedule columns. The difference between the totals of the Manufacturing Schedule columns is the cost of the goods manufactured. This amount is transferred to the Statement of Profit and Loss columns.

The use of the Adjustments, and the Adjusted Trial Balance columns is similar to the work sheet illustrated in Chapter XII. 

The only difference is the addition of new items having to do with manufacturing operations.

The treatment of these adjustments on the working sheet, except those for the goods in process and the raw materials inventories, is similar to that explained in Chapter XII. The goods in process inventory account and the raw materials inventory account are adjusted through the manufacturing summary account since, when the books are closed, they will be closed through that account. The raw materials inventory at the beginning of the fiscal period has become a part of the cost of goods manufactured. 

Raw Materials Inventory is therefore credited for the value of the beginning inventory, $30,000, and Manufacturing Summary is debited for the same amount. The raw materials inventory

C. Ch. 22] 553 

10 at the end of the period must be recorded as an asset and must be shown as a deduction from the cost of goods manufactured. Raw 

Materials Inventory is therefore debited for $36,850, and Manufacturing Summary is credited for the same amount. In a like manner, the inventory of goods in process at the beginning of the period must be transferred to the manufacturing summary account, and the goods in process at the end of the period must be deducted from the cost of goods in process and must be shown as an asset. 

The first transfer is accomplished by crediting Goods in Process 

Inventory and by debiting Manufacturing Summary for $15,000.

The second transfer is accomplished by debiting Goods in Process Inventory and by crediting Manufacturing Summary for $20,000. 

The finished goods inventory account is adjusted through the profit and loss summary account in the same manner as the merchandise inventory of a mercantile business. The finished goods inventory at the beginning of the period is transferred to the profit and loss summary account by crediting Finished Goods Inventory for $20,000 and by debiting Profit and Loss Summary for the same amount. The finished goods inventory at the end of the period is recorded by debiting Finished Goods Inventory for $34,000 and by crediting Profit and Loss Summary for the same amount.

After the Adjustments columns have been totaled to prove the accuracy of the adjusting entries, the items are carried across into the Adjusted Trial Balance columns. This pair of columns is totaled to prove the accuracy of the adjusted amounts. The adjusted amounts are then carried across the work sheet into the proper columns. All items representing the cost of goods manufactured are extended into the columns headed "Manufacturing Schedule." These items include the raw materials purchases, the freight and cartage in, the purchases returns and allowances, all manufacturing expenses, and the costs and deductions from costs arising from the inventories of raw materials and goods in process at the beginning and the end of the fiscal period. The balance of each item, except the items written on a line with "Manufacturing Summary," is extended into the proper column. The items appearing opposite "Manufacturing Summary" represent the inventories of raw materials and goods in process at the beginning and the end of the fiscal period.
