ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

The difference between the totals of the Manufacturing Schedule columns represents the cost of goods manufactured. This amount is carried forward into the Debit column under the heading "Statement of Profit and Loss." The balances of the income accounts are extended into the Credit column under the heading "Statement of Profit and Loss," and the balances of all expense accounts that do not represent manufacturing expenses are extended into the Debit column. The amounts of the assets, the liabilities, and the proprietorship are carried forward into the proper columns in the same manner as they are in the preparation of a work sheet for a trading concern. The columns of the work sheet are then totaled and ruled. 

The statement of profit and loss of The Franklin Manufacturing Company is shown in Illustration No. 76. All the data required for this statement, including the cost of goods manufactured, have been obtained from the Statement of Profit and Loss columns of the work sheet. The cost of goods manufactured is reported as a single amount, and reference is made to the schedule that shows how the amount was determined. 

The cost of goods manufactured schedule is shown in Illustration No. 77. This schedule contains the same amounts as those shown in the Manufacturing Schedule columns of the working sheet. The amounts are arranged, however, so that the desired information can be obtained readily. The right-hand amount column contains the beginning goods in process inventory. To this amount is added the total of the elements of manufacturing cost for the period, and from this sum the ending goods in process inventory is subtracted to obtain the cost of the goods completed during the year. The amount column second from the right contains the three elements of cost: the materials used, the direct labor, and the total of the manufacturing expenses. 

The cost of raw materials consumed is made up of the begin. ning inventory of raw materials, the purchases made during the period, the freight and cartage on the purchases, less the purchases returns and allowances and the ending inventory. The manufacturing expenses, totaling $60,600, consist of all expenses incidental to operating the factory except labor applied directly to the conversion of raw materials into finished goods.

period there are unfinished goods, represented by the beginning goods in process inventory. During the period materials, labor, and manufacturing expenses are added to this inventory. At the end of the period the goods in process are inventoried. This amount is subtracted from the sum of the beginning goods in process inventory and the materials, labor, and manufacturing expenses. The result is the cost of the goods manufactured.

the report; and the deferred charges include factory supplies. The liabilities include a current liability, wages payable, and a fixed liability, first-mortgage 7 per cent bonds payable. 

After the financial reports have been prepared, the adjusting and closing entries are recorded in the general journal. The Adjustments columns of the work sheet are used as the basis for these entries. Since all manufacturing costs are to be summarized in an account called Manufacturing Summary, the adjustments for raw materials inventory and the goods in process inventory are posted to this account. The adjustments for the finished goods inventory are posted to the profit and loss summary account. 

After the adjustments have been recorded, the closing entries are made. All manufacturing items are transferred to the manufacturing summary account. The balance of the manufacturing summary account is then a debit that represents the cost of the goods manufactured. This balance is transferred to the profit and loss summary account, and all remaining income and expense items are closed into the same account. The balance of the profit and loss summary account then represents the profit or the loss for the period, and is transferred to Surplus. The manufacturing summary account of The Franklin Manufacturing Company is:

ILLUSTRATION No. 79, MANUFACTURING SUMMARY ACCOUNT 

Valuation of the Inventories of a Manufac- The valuation of the inventory of raw materials at the end of a fiscal period does not differ from the practice followed in trading concerns. Cost or market prices are available and are used. In turing Enterprise

Ch. 22] 561 

the valuation of the goods in process and the finished goods inventories, however, market prices either are not available, or, if they are, are unsuitable because they represent selling prices. In either case it is necessary to ascertain the cost of the items in these . inventories. This computation involves the use of estimates based on information derived largely from the accounting records. 

In estimating the cost of finished goods, and goods in process, it has become customary to consider the cost as being composed of three elements: raw materials, direct labor, and overhead. The raw materials and the direct labor are embodied in the goods being produced and represent direct costs. All other costs, which are not directly identifiable with goods being produced and which apply to the factory as a whole, are called indirect costs and are reported under the heading "Manufacturing Expenses" on the cost of goods manufactured schedule. Included with manufacturing expenses are the cost of indirect labor and that of factory supplies used. 

Factory supplies may be considered indirect materials since they represent materials used to facilitate production within the factory. 

The total of the manufacturing expenses, including indirect materials and indirect labor, comprises the item overhead. In valuing the finished goods, it is possible to determine the direct costs; but some basis of allocating to the finished goods the proper indirect costs, or overhead, must be used. Factory overhead is commonly expressed as a percentage of the direct labor cost. The relation of the elements of cost to the finished goods, based on the costs of The Franklin Manufacturing Company, are shown in the following table: 

MANUFACTURING SUMMARY DIRECT COSTS OVERHEAD

Manufacturing Expenses.. 

$60,600.00

=80% $75,750.00

The cost of any item in the finished goods or the goods in process inventory is thus seen to be made up of the three elements mentioned previously. For example, the cost per unit of Commodity A in the finished goods inventory results from the following combination: 

Direct Materials. 

Direct Labor... 

Overhead (80% of Direct Labor).

Perpetual, or Book, Inventories $ 5.50 2.50 2.00 

Total Estimated Cost Per Unit..... $10.00 The accounting procedures described in this chapter require the taking of physical inventories of raw materials, goods in process, and finished goods whenever a balance sheet and a statement of profit and loss are prepared. To take these inventories monthly would require a great deal of time and labor and would interfere with manufacturing operations. As a substitute for physical inventories, perpetual or book inventories are being increasingly used.

When book inventories are kept in a manufacturing plant, accounts are set up with raw materials, goods in process, and

Ch. 22] 563 

finished goods, and entries debiting and crediting these accounts are made currently. For example, the raw materials account is debited for each receipt of raw materials and credited for each withdrawal of raw materials for manufacturing. The balance of the account gives the amount of raw materials that should be on hand. It is therefore not necessary to take a physical inventory of raw materials to determine the balance for statement purposes. 

It is necessary only to check occasionally the balance of the account with the quantity on hand. 

When book inventories of all three manufacturing inventory items are kept, the system of accounting is known as cost accounting. A description of cost accounting as it is found in manufacturing or trading concerns is presented in the following two chapters. 

Under a cost accounting system, it is possible to prepare monthly balance sheets and statements of profit and loss with accompanying schedules of cost of goods manufactured, without the cessation of operations that is necessary when physical inventories have to be taken.

1. The Strom Company manufactures watches; the Sayler Company, ice cream. At the end of a fiscal period which company would normally have the larger amount of goods in process in proportion to the total annual sales? 

2. The two companies mentioned in Problem No. 1 are located in Chicago, Illinois. Do their fiscal periods necessarily coincide? 

Explain.

Taxes and assessments during the ten years amounted to $2,500, of which amount $1,200 occurred prior to the erection of the building. The estimated life of the building is twenty years with a salvage value of $1,000. What correcting entry or entries should be made?

CHAPTER XXIII COST ACCOUNTING-JOB ORDER 

Cost Accounting and Cost accounting is commonly distinguished 

General Accounting from general accounting. It is often considered as belonging to a separate realm or as representing a distinct field of accounting procedure. This is scarcely true. The difference is one of emphasis. Earlier chapters have presented the accounts used in determining the cost of goods sold. In the preceding chapter a group of new accounts were assembled to determine the cost of goods manufactured. The selecting and grouping of accounts identifiable with some process or product is cost accounting. 

It is the relating of a portion of the total expenses to a defined part of the business, known as a cost unit. Cost accounting is actually unit accounting.

Whenever the general accountant breaks down the life of a business enterprise into periods of a year or of a month, he is engaged in cost accounting. He is determining the costs applicable to that period as a unit. When he goes further and finds the cost of sales as separate and distinct from operating expenses, he is again engaged in cost accounting. And when he analyzes expenses to show separately the cost of selling and the cost of general or administrative operations, he establishes still further cost units. 

The statements of profit and loss already presented contain elements of the cost accounting process. 

Whatever operations are performed in business, costs are involved. The cost of delivery, the cost of granting credit, the cost of buying or producing power, all these represent significant managerial problems. Cost figures are always helpful to the management in determining policies of business administration. 

In manufacturing, the unit to which costs are related is the job order, or production order. A job order calls for the manufacture of a specified number of units of finished goods. A portion of the total manufacturing expenses must be assigned to that particular job in order to find the cost of the units. Records are provided to show the materials and labor costs on each job and to apportion to each job a part of the total manufacturing ex- 573

[Ch. 23 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

penses. When the job is completed, the total cost is divided by the number of units finished in order to determine the cost of each unit of the finished goods. This procedure demands a careful analysis of the cost or expense elements entering into each job. 

The elements of cost in a completed job are shown in the following tabulation: 

JOB No. 147 (1,000 units)

Materials Used.. 

Labor.. 

Manufacturing Expense Applied. 

Total Cost..... 

Cost per unit, 512c $146 00 246 00 123 00 515 00 The purpose of this chapter and of the following chapter is to present the cost emphasis as it is found in accounting practice. 

It will be illustrated first in the manufacturing enterprise, in which it has been developed most extensively. Later, similar procedures will be applied to other types of enterprises. 

Purpose of Cost Accounting 

Cost accounting is particularly helpful to management. It deals primarily with internal conditions, and with these management is principally concerned. It assists in the following ways: 

(1) Determination of Operating Efficiency. The cost account ing reports, by showing increasing or decreasing unit costs, will indicate whether the efficiency of the plant management is increasing or decreasing. They thereby aid in placing upon the plant management a definite responsibility for the efficient production of the goods. If the business has more than one plant manufacturing the same product, the cost records will show a comparison of the relative efficiency of the different plants. 

(2) Obtaining of Greater Accuracy in Stating Balance Sheet Values and Operating Results. The previous chapter presented the use of cost data in valuing inventories of goods in process and finished goods. These data were based on estimated rather than actual costs. Actual data and organized procedure insure accuracy and result in more satisfactory accounting statements. Cost accounting establishes a procedure for the valuation of inventories that provides the check of double entry for accuracy.

Ch. 23] 575 

COST ACCOUNTING ― JOB ORDER 

Ee Ou ES 

(3) Establishment and Maintenance of Proper Sales Prices. 

Although prices are set primarily by competition, there is a relationship between them and costs. Cost accounting is a very definite aid in the establishment and maintenance of sales prices. 

If the cost of specific units manufactured is not shown, the management does not know what a fair sales price should be. If the cost is known and the margin of profit is too low, the management can alter the design of the product if necessary or can, in some other way, attempt to reduce the production cost. 

(4) Control of Future Expenditures and Planning of Future 

Operations. The compilation of cost data gives the management facts upon which it can base estimates for use in the future planning of production. By the use of accurate data on past costs, the management can ascertain those products that it should seek to sell in large quantities and those that it should discontinue or sell in as small quantities as possible. After the quantity of goods to be produced has been ascertained, cost information is necessary to ascertain the cash required to finance the manufacturing program. Such data are essential in the accurate planning of all the manufacturing activities. 

Flow of Costs in Per- When a manufacturing plant is operated petual Inventories under a cost system, asset accounts are set up to record its three inventories. These accounts are debited for all additions and credited for all subtractions. Every expenditure connected with the operation of the factory eventually becomes a charge to an inventory account. Not only the materials and the goods on hand at the end of the accounting period are shown by the inventory accounts, but also the volume of activity during the period.

Manufacturing expenditures become parts of the goods in process inventory because that inventory represents raw materials as they are being converted into finished goods. In manufacturing it is the conversion of raw materials into finished goods that causes manufacturing expenditures. The costs represented by these expenditures move through the goods in process inventory and become incorporated in the cost of the finished goods. The flow of these costs is shown briefly at the top of page 576. 

All manufacturing expenditures, as was suggested in the last chapter, may be classed under the three heads: Raw Materials,

[Ch. 23 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES Raw Materials 

Goods in Process 

Pay Roll 

Overhead 

Finished Goods 

Direct Labor, Overhead. These three elements of cost move through the goods in process account and ultimately become a part of the cost of finished goods. 

Cost Accounting in 

Manufacturing The need for estimating various costs in order to determine the value of goods in process and finished goods inventories was discussed in the previous chapter. Estimates were made of the materials, labor, and overhead charges found in the inventories at the close of the period. 

The calculation of such estimates may result in many errors. 

The incorporation of the three inventory accounts into the current accounting system, however, provides the accuracy of double entry and tends to eliminate errors.

The inventory accounts are affected not only by transactions that are completed between the manufacturing enterprise and other businesses, but also by transactions that are completed between different departments or units of the enterprise. The effects of these internal transactions are reflected in the accounts just as if the transactions were completed with other enterprises. An order on the storeroom for raw materials results in a credit to the raw materials account and a debit to the goods in process account. 

Such a transaction is shown below in journal form. 

Goods in Process. 

Raw Materials. 

12.35 12.35 

A report of time spent on a production order by a workman results in a credit to the factory pay roll account and a debit to the goods in process account, as shown below:

Goods in Process. 

Pay Roll.. 2.45 2.45

Ch. 23] 577 

COST ACCOUNTING JOB ORDER 

​ea When goods are completed and moved from the factory into the finished goods stockroom, Goods in Process is credited and Finished Goods is debited. None of these transactions is completed with any outside business, yet under the perpetual inventory system they are accounted for in cost accounts. 

The recognition of the accountability of internal transactions is a distinguishing characteristic of cost accounting. Since the two bargaining parties who usually establish accountable values are absent, it becomes necessary to use the precise cost as the basis of the accounting entry. The principles and methods fol- 

I lowed in setting up such costs constitute the subject matter of cost accounting in manufacturing. A consideration of the treatment of internal transactions affecting each of the perpetual in ventory accounts follows. 

Raw Materials Account Raw materials are recorded in an asset account, which is treated in much the same way as the cash account; that is, it is debited for additions and credited for withdrawals. When materials are purchased, they are charged at invoice price plus transportation cost to the raw materials account. When they are withdrawn from the storeroom for use in the process of manufacture, their value is credited to Raw Materials and charged to Goods in Process or to one of the manufacturing expense accounts. Raw materials that will become a part of finished goods are charged to the goods in process account; raw materials that will be used to assist in some process of manufacture are charged to a particular manufacturing expense account. These charges result from internal transactions and necessitate the calculation of cost price as the basis for the accounting entries. This price is ordinarily the actual price paid for the materials plus the transportation cost. The following journal entry accounts for the use of materials: 

Goods in Process Factory Overhead Raw Materials. 

1,250.00 250.00 1,500.00

Since the raw materials account represents many kinds of units rather than one, it is set up as a controlling account with a subsidiary ledger. This ledger is called the stores ledger, or raw materials ledger, and contains an account with each kind of material in the storeroom. Postings are made currently to the raw

[Ch. 23 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

materials accounts in the subsidiary ledger, and the balances of these accounts are compared at intervals with the actual amounts of the various kinds of materials on hand. This comparison and the accompanying adjustments that may be necessary eliminate, in most companies, an annual shutdown for the purpose of taking at one time complete physical inventories.

The additions to raw materials are shown on materials-received reports, which are made out by the receiving clerk. The materials listed on such a report are priced by a clerk in the purchasing department. The report is then sent to the balance of stores clerk, who records the amount on the debit side of a raw materials account in the stores ledger. Withdrawals are shown on materials requisition slips. The amount of the materials listed on such a report is posted to the credit side of a raw materials account in the stores ledger. The materials-received reports and materials requisition slips are summarized once a month, and the totals are posted respectively to the debit and the credit sides of the raw materials controlling account. The relations between materialsreceived reports, materials requisition slips, and their summaries are comparable to the relations between sales invoices, checks received, and the sales and cash receipts journals. The raw materials and the accounts receivable controlling accounts provide totals at the end of the month; the stores ledger and the customers' ledger provide detail currently.

The relation of the controlling account Raw Materials to the stores ledger is shown in the following diagram: 

STORES LEDGER RAW MATERIALS

Just as each materials requisition is the basis for a credit to an account in the stores ledger, it is at the same time the basis for a debit to a job order account or to a factory overhead account.

Whatever materials are withdrawn from the storeroom must be charged to production. This is discussed further in the following paragraphs.

Goods in Process Account 

Earlier in this chapter it was stated that 

Raw Materials is credited and Goods in 

Process is debited when raw materials are withdrawn from the #storeroom and put into production. The goods in process account shows, on its debit side, the cost of all elements put into manufacturing and, on its credit side, the cost of all elements leaving manufacturing, principally as finished goods. Charges for direct materials and direct labor, which are costs identifiable with particular jobs in process, as well as charges for overhead, are made to Goods in Process. 

Since the goods in process account represents many kinds of units rather than one, it also must be a controlling account for a subsidiary ledger. This ledger is known as the production order ledger, or cost ledger. In the cost ledger an account with each factory production order is established, and to it are charged all materials and labor costs identifiable with that order. To the production order account is also charged a portion of the unidentifiable expenses that represent factory overhead. The accounts in the subsidiary ledger are posted currently. Such an account may be closed and removed from the cost ledger when the order with which it correlates has been completed. A summary of the completed production orders provides the information for the entry at the end of the month, in which the goods in process account is credited and the finished goods account is debited. 

The relation of the controlling account Goods in Process to the cost ledger is shown in the following diagram:

Labor cost is treated in a manner similar to materials cost. 

In place of the materials requisition slip as the basis for a charge to an account with a particular production order, the time ticket, or time report, is used. On the daily time ticket the time of each workman in the factory is charged either to a specific production order or to indirect labor. When the time tickets are summarized, the goods in process and the indirect labor accounts are debited and the pay roll account is credited. The pay roll account is debited and the cash account is credited when the periodic payment of wages is made. The time tickets from which the pay roll is prepared serve as media for current postings to the accounts with production orders. The total of the debit postings to these accounts must agree with the direct labor charge to the controlling account 

Goods in Process. 

In order that the goods in process account will show all manufacturing costs, those costs not identifiable with particular production orders must be grouped and applied to Goods in Process as a percentage of one or more of the known items. In the previous chapter the factory overhead, consisting of indirect materials, indirect labor, and other manufacturing expenses, was ascertained and expressed as a percentage of the direct labor cost. The various

[Ch. 23 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

kinds of indirect manufacturing costs are ordinarily recorded in separate accounts. At the end of the month these accounts are closed into the factory overhead account in order that the various indirect costs can be charged to production orders as a single element of manufacturing cost.

Since the percentage of actual overhead to actual labor cost cannot be obtained until the end of the period, and since it is desirable to know the total cost of a production order when that order has been completed, it is customary to estimate the total manufacturing expenses for the forthcoming period and to predetermine a rate that will approximately absorb the actual overhead for the period. Whenever orders are completed, the respective accounts in the cost ledger are charged with overhead at the predetermined rate. Also, at the end of the accounting period when the value of the goods in process inventory is needed, the production orders that have not been completed must be charged with overhead. The latter procedure is illustrated on page 580 in the account with Job No. 4, which was uncompleted at the end of the month. In order to show the full value of the goods in process inventory, it was necessary to add overhead to the other elements of cost in this job. The amount of overhead, $375, was obtained by multiplying $500, the direct labor cost, by the overhead rate, 75 per cent. At the end of the month the amounts of overhead that have been charged to completed and uncompleted jobs should agree with the debit amount of overhead in the goods in process account. This debit is determined by the application of the overhead rate to the direct labor cost in the controlling account at the end of the month. 

To illustrate the treatment of overhead, it may be assumed that the total manufacturing expenses for the forthcoming period are estimated at $75,000 and that the total direct labor cost is estimated at $100,000. From these amounts an overhead rate of 

75 per cent is established. All jobs are therefore charged for overhead equal to 75 per cent of the direct labor cost charged to the jobs. At the end of the period it is found that the total direct labor cost was $90,000 and that the overhead charges were $70,000. 

The actual overhead rate should have been 77-7/9 per cent ($70,000 divided by $90,000). The jobs were consequently charged for less than the actual indirect charges. The entries in the accounts appeared as follows:

Ch. 23] 583 

COST ACCOUNTING - — JOB ORDER 

It 

​PAY ROLL GOODS IN PROCESS 

90,000

FACTORY OVERHEAD 

(75% of 90,000) 90,000 67,500 70,000 67,500

SU The fact that the factory overhead account has a debit bal- Iance is also proof that the rate was too low and that the jobs have consequently been charged for less than they should have been. att In such a case overhead is said to be underabsorbed. When the factory overhead account has a credit balance, it is evident that the rate was too high and that the jobs were therefore charged for 12 more than they should have been. Overhead is then said to be overabsorbed. Over a period of a year these balances should tend to offset each other. If the rate is consistently too low or too high, however, it should be increased or decreased so that the goods et produced will be charged as closely as possible for the actual overhead. The difference between the actual overhead and the amount ercharged at the predetermined rate is usually added as a lump sum to the cost of sales or is subtracted from it. No attempt is usually made to correct the overhead costs already charged to each order. per When a production order has been completed, the costs represented by the materials, labor, and overhead charged to it are summarized in the account in the cost ledger. An entry is then made in a special journal known as a finished goods journal. At the end of the accounting period the total of these entries is posted to the debit of Finished Goods and the credit of Goods in Process. 

The balance of the goods in process account then represents the total costs charged to uncompleted orders, or, in other words, the cost value of the goods in process inventory. 

Finished Goods Account 

In the preceding paragraph it was stated that, at the end of an accounting period, the finished goods account is debited and the goods in process account is credited for the total costs applicable to all production orders completed during the period. Hence Finished Goods is debited for the cost of all goods transferred from the factory to the stockroom. It is also credited for the cost of all goods shipped.

[Ch. 23 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

When goods are sold, shipping orders are commonly prepared, and from these are formulated entries debiting Cost of Sales and credit ing Finished Goods. At the same time Accounts Receivable is debited and Sales is credited for the selling price. Each sale thus requires two entries: in the first the finished goods are recorded at the cost price; in the second, at the selling price.

Like the two previous inventory accounts, the finished goods account is a controlling account. The subsidiary ledger that it controls is known as the stock ledger, or finished goods ledger. In the stock ledger an account is kept with each kind of manufactured commodity. On the debit side of each account in the stock ledger there are columns for the number of units of the commodity manufactured, the cost of a unit, and the cost of the completed order. On the credit side there are columns for the number of units shipped, the cost of a unit, and the total cost of the units shipped.

When finished goods are to be recorded in a stock ledger account, the cost of a single unit must be determined. The total cost of the order for the commodity, as well as the number of units completed, is given on a cost sheet in the cost ledger. When the total cost of the order is divided by the number of units produced, the result is the cost of a unit. When a shipment is made, the cost of the goods sold is determined by multiplying the cost of a single unit, which is given on the debit side of the account, by the number of units shipped. 

Often the debit side of a stock ledger account will show that the same commodity was produced on several occasions at different costs. In this case the unit cost that is listed first on the debit side is applied to the first shipment of the commodity. When the number of units shipped exceeds the number manufactured at the first unit cost, the second unit cost is used until that lot of the commodity has been exhausted. This pricing procedure is perhaps the one most widely used in crediting accounts in the stock ledger and is known as the "first-in, first-out" method. The same method is also used in crediting accounts in the raw materials ledger. The application of this method in connection with finished goods is illustrated by Commodity B in the following diagram. 

This diagram shows the relation of the controlling account Finished 

Goods to the stock ledger.

The Factory Ledger 

In some cases it is convenient to have all accounts with factory inventories and factory operations set up in a ledger or ledgers entirely separate from the general ledger. This is true particularly when the factory is located at some distance from the main office. The details of factory operations are of interest principally to factory executives and need to be easily available. An account called Factory Ledger is therefore set up in the general ledger, and all transactions having to do with the factory are summarized in it. Purchases of raw materials, pay-roll payments, current factory expenses, and

[Ch. 23 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

monthly charges such as depreciation, insurance, and taxes on the factory are recorded as debits to this account. The corresponding credits are made to Vouchers Payable, Reserve for Depreciation, Prepaid Insurance, and Taxes Payable. Goods shipped from the factory on orders from the main office are recorded at cost as credits to this account and debits to Cost of Sales. The debit balance of the account represents the net investment in factory inventories.

The reciprocal nature of the entries is illustrated in each of the above entries. Whenever Factory Ledger is debited on the general books, General Ledger is credited on the factory books, and vice versa. All factory transactions are recorded in a single account in the general ledger.

The details of factory operations are recorded in the factory ledger. In this ledger are accounts with raw materials, pay roll, overhead, goods in process, and finished goods. In order that this ledger will be self-balancing, an account called General Ledger, which is the reciprocal of the factory ledger account in the general ledger, is set up. When statements are prepared in the main office, the balances of accounts in both ledgers, except the balances

When a factory ledger is maintained, it is customary to keep also a factory journal or journals, from which postings are made to the factory ledger. These journals may serve as cumulative records of the information entered on factory forms evidencing internal transactions. 00 The flow of costs through factory ledger accounts, based on the foregoing transactions, is illustrated by the chart on page 592. 

The chart shows the directness with which the two elements raw materials and direct labor enter into the cost of goods in process; it also indicates the indirect way in which the element factory overhead becomes a part of the cost of goods in process. Raw materials amounting to $50,000 and direct labor amounting to $50,000 have been charged directly to Goods in Process. The detail of each of these charges is shown in the cost ledger, controlled by the goods in process account. The number of jobs ordinarily controlled by the goods in process account is, however, many times the number used here. ·

Overhead has been charged to Goods in Process at 80 per cent of the total direct labor cost, and to each job account in the subsidiary ledger at the same percentage of the direct labor cost of that job. The total charge to Goods in Process for overhead is $1,000 less than the actual overhead; hence, for October, there was underabsorbed overhead of $1,000. In September the same rate had been used, but the charge to Goods in Process more than equaled the total overhead; hence there was overabsorbed overhead of $400. The balance of the factory overhead account at the end of October was therefore $600. The estimated rate, while not entirely accurate, was sufficiently so for the purpose of finding the cost of separate jobs and also the value of the two inventories of goods in process and finished goods. All manufacturing expenses except the small balance in the factory overhead account have passed through the goods in process account. A balance in the factory overhead account is in reality a deferred charge or a deferred credit to the manufacturing activities of succeeding periods and is carried forward from month to month. 

The chart does not show the accounts in the subsidiary ledgers controlled by the raw materials account and the finished goods account. Such subsidiary ledgers are illustrated on pages 578 and 585. The entries recording the internal transactions in the accounts shown on the chart and in the three subsidiary ledgers, only one of which is included in the chart, provide a complete record of manufacturing costs. By means of successive debit and credit entries, costs have progressed from the raw materials stage to the finished goods state. The entry recording the selling price would not have been changed. 

The accounting result of recording the internal transactions having to do with manufacturing is to show directly the cost of goods sold, adjusted by the amount of overabsorbed or underabsorbed overhead, and to leave in the raw materials, goods in

[Ch. 23 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

process, and finished goods accounts the amounts of the inventories at the end of the accounting period. Physical inventories serve only to check the accuracy of the balances in the raw materials, goods in process, and finished goods accounts. This condition is to be contrasted with that in general accounting, where the amount of the inventory is unknown until a physical count is made. There is, in general accounting, no book record that can be used in checking the accuracy of the physical inventory. 

The use of perpetual inventory accounts for raw materials, goods in process, and finished goods distinguishes cost accounting from general accounting. The accounts with raw materials, goods in process, and finished goods, and the accounts in the subsidiary ledgers that are controlled by these three accounts, provide information that proves extremely helpful in controlling the activities of an industrial enterprise. 

The termination of the flow of costs in a single account entitled Cost of Sales facilitates the preparation of a statement of profit and loss at frequent intervals, perhaps each month. The simplicity of such a statement is apparent from the following illustration: 

THE MILLER MANUFACTURING CO.

The statement can be prepared by direct reference to the sales, the cost of sales, and the expense accounts. It is not necessary to take physical inventories or to compute the cost of goods sold. The statement of profit and loss can thus be prepared accurately, rapidly, and with a minimum of computation and detail. 

If more detailed information is required, the cost of the sales can be analyzed by reference to the finished goods and the goods in process accounts. This procedure, however, is generally not necessary.

Ch. 23] 595 

COST ACCOUNTING -JOB ORDER 

The cost of the sales is adjusted on the statement by adding or deducting the underabsorbed or the overabsorbed overhead resulting from inaccuracy in estimating the overhead rate. When the accounts are closed, the difference between the actual overhead and the estimated overhead is closed into the cost of sales account.

Process Cost 

Accounting 

CHAPTER XXIV  

In the previous chapter cost accounting was defined as the selecting and grouping of accounts identifiable with some product or process. In describing and illustrating a job-order cost system, manufacturing expense accounts were identified with the product directly. In this chapter the same manufacturing expenses will be identified first with a manufacturing process and only secondarily with the product. This indirect method of finding the cost of products manufactured is known as process cost accounting.

In many industries it is not possible to identify the raw materials with specific job orders as they are converted into finished goods. In the manufacture of ink, paint, soap, paper, and other similar products, the raw materials processed form a homogeneous mass, not identifiable with production orders. In this case the elements of cost must first be identified with processes and then with finished goods. The cost of a gallon of paint is the cost of the process divided by the number of gallons turned out by the process. The cost of a cake of soap is the cost of the process divided by the number of cakes turned out by the process. 

The items charged to the process account are similar to the items charged to the goods in process account under the job order system. They include the elements of cost: materials, labor, and overhead. A process account is illustrated below:

PROCESS A Materials.. 

4,000/00 Labor.. 3,000/00 Overhead.. 

2,000 00 

Units completed-10,000. 

Cost per unit 9,000 9,000/00 €90c 

9,000 00 10,000 

9,000 00

The finished goods of Process A may become the raw materials of 

Process B. 

602

Ch. 24] 603 

As manufacturing becomes more and more standardized, and the units of output, day after day, tend to be alike, the significance of job-order costs disappears and greater utility is found in knowing the costs of processes from day to day. Such processes characterize not only manufacturing operations but selling operations as well. Retailing and wholesaling consist of a series of processes, which taken together produce sales. The cost of almost any activity may be divided by the units of output, and a process cost per unit obtained. Process cost accounting is therefore perhaps more universally applicable than job-order cost accounting. It is the purpose of this chapter to describe process cost accounting in manufacturing and in other business fields as well. 

Job-Order and Process In job-order cost accounting the three Costs Distinguished elements of cost-materials, labor, and overhead-were charged directly to job orders. All production consisted of job orders, and all costs ultimately were identified with some job. As illustrated above, in process cost accounting the elements of cost are charged to processes and the cost of goods produced in a given period is obtained by dividing the cost of the process by the number of units produced. Since all goods produced by a process are like units, it is no longer useful to classify production into job orders. Process costs give average costs. 

If there were but one process in a factory, the cost accounting would be very simple. The elements of manufacturing would be charged to the single process account, and the process unit cost would be determined by dividing the total by the number of units produced. The accounting procedure would be exactly similar to that used in Chapter XXII in finding the cost of goods manufactured. If the finished goods produced in that factory had been like goods, the manufacturing summary account would have been the process account.

Ordinarily, however, there are several processes in a factory. 

It therefore becomes necessary to account for each process separately, just as it was necessary in the preceding chapter to account for each job order separately. If there were as many processes as there were job orders, it would be advisable to set up a separate process ledger similar to the cost ledger. But, since the number of processes is more limited, process cost accounts commonly appear in the factory ledger, and there is no subsidiary process ledger.

[Ch. 24 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Process costs result in averaging the costs of given operations as they apply to goods manufactured. Job-order costs differentiate the costs applicable to goods manufactured. Job-order systems involve more detail and give more specific information than process systems. Since process costs provide only one cost figure for each cost period, they require much less detailed work. 

In a factory where a number of processes exist, it is customary to have additional departments that do not process the raw materials directly but assist the processing departments. For example, if processing uses power machinery, the power to run the machinery may be furnished by a power department. If the product involves chemical engineering, the formulas used may be controlled by a chemical engineering department. Such departments are known as service departments.

Service Departments Where service departments exist, the fac and Process Costs tory is considered not as a unit but as a collection of departments. The service departments facilitate the processing departments in producing finished goods. They include such departments as the factory office, the building department, the power plant, and the repair shop. 

The existence of the departments in a factory gives rise to additional internal transactions. The services rendered by a service department result in internal transactions between that department and the processes that receive the benefit of the service. 

With these internal transactions, as with those discussed in the previous chapter, the basis of the amount involved in the transaction is the cost of the service rendered. For example, if the total charges to the power department for the period of a month are $3,000 and this department provides power service to two processes, the debits to the two processes will be based on the $3,000, the cost of the service rendered. If this department rendered 

150,000 kilowatt hours of service, then the charge would be made at the rate of two cents a kilowatt hour. If Process A used 100,000 kw. hours and Process B 50,000 kw. hours, the service department account and its charges to A and B would appear as shown at the top of page 605. 

In a departmentalized factory, the service department costs are charged periodically to the process accounts. The period usually chosen is a month. At the end of the month the process

On the left side of this account are shown all the elements entering into the process, including the cost of raw materials and the service department costs. Since the materials cost $4,000, the amount added to the materials in Process C is $6,000 ($10,000 — $4,000). This amount, $6,000, is called the processing cost. Since 

10,000 units have been processed, the cost of a completed unit is $1, of which amount 40 cents represents the material cost and 60 cents the processing cost. This cost of $1 per unit, the finished goods cost in Process C, becomes the material cost in Process D if processing is continued in a new process. 

Inventories of Partially In the preceding illustrations, it was as- 

Processed Materials sumed that the goods were completely processed at the end of the accounting period. In such cases the

• [Ch. 24 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

determination of unit costs is a simple problem in division. Frequently, however, materials in process do exist at the end of the period, and all of the charges to the process account should not be included in the cost of the finished units. A portion of the processing cost belongs to the partly processed materials. 

When partly processed materials do exist and it is necessary to determine an inventory value for them, a distinction must be made between materials charged to the process and the other debits to the account, which constitute the processing cost. In the previous illustration the cost of processed units was $1 per unit, made up of two elements raw materials, 40 cents, and processing cost, 60 cents. For purposes of illustration, assume that instead of 10,000 units, only 7,000 had been completed and that the balance of 3,000 were only one third completed, on the average, at the end of the month. The unit material cost would be the same as before and would be obtained by dividing the materials cost, $4,000, by the number of units put into process, 10,000. The unit processing cost would be larger than before because a smaller number of units had been processed. The number processed would be obtained by adding the 7,000 completed units to the fractional part of the 3,000 units only one third completed. Processing for the period would be 7,000+(½ of 3,000), or 8,000 units. The term equivalent units is applied to the 8,000 because it does not give the number processed but only the equivalent volume of processing. 

The processing charges, consisting of labor, overhead, and service costs, are divided by the number of equivalent units processed to obtain the processing cost applicable to the completed units. This amounts to $6,000÷8,000, or 75 cents per unit. The cost value of the completed units is then made up as follows: 

Materials costs 

Processing costs 

7,000 X $.40 $2,800 7,000 X .75 5,250 

7,000 X $1.15 $8,050 The cost value of the partially processed units is obtained as 

Material costs 

3,000 X $.40 

$1,200 .75 Processing costs 3,000 X .25 750 

3,000 X $.65 = $1,950

Ch. 24] 607 

This balance of 3,000 partially processed units is the beginning inventory of Process C for the next accounting period. If the processing costs for that period increase from 75 cents per equivalent unit to 90 cents per équivalent unit, the processing costs of the 3,000 partially processed units would be a combination of 1% of 75 cents and 2% of 90 cents or, in other words, % of the last period's processing costs plus 2/3 of this period's processing costs. Of the goods completed during this second period, the first 3,000 would have a unit processing cost of 85 cents, and the rest of the product a unit processing cost of 90 cents. 

The preceding illustration shows the emphasis that is placed on each accounting period's processing. The processing costs for that period are kept distinct from the processing costs of the preceding period. The materials costs of the new period are also kept separate. The production of each period is treated in the accounts as if it were processed separately. 

The length of the period depends on the situation in each factory. Process costs may be calculated for the production of a few hours, for the total number of units put into process, for the number of units completed, and for the extent of completion of those still in process. In a continuous process, the extent of completion is usually one half. As stated in the previous section, the period chosen is usually the month. 

Processes Producing The result of a process may be two or more 

Two Products products in place of one, as assumed in previous illustrations. In such a case it becomes necessary to apportion among the resulting products the processing costs of the period. To accomplish this apportionment, some basis must be chosen. This basis may be according to the number of units of each product processed. In some cases the number of units of the products may be weighted to give effect to factors applicable to the products. For example, meats are bought by packers "on the hoof" at a price per pound. The finished goods, the various cuts of meat sold by the packers, have widely diverging prices. 

It is assumed that these prices were merged in the price paid "on the hoof." Based on such assumption, the products resulting from the process share in the total processing costs according to weight and price combined. The output in quantity of pounds is weighted by the market price of the finished goods.

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES

If one of the two products has so little value that it can be classed as a by-product, any return on it may be treated as a credit to the processing account. No attempt is made to calculate profit or costs on the by-product. For example, if Process A resulted in two products, X and M, and the net return from M was only $200, that amount could be credited to the cost of producing Product X.

Process Cost Accounting At the beginning of the chapter it was in Distribution stated that retailing and wholesaling consist of a series of processes that taken together produce sales. These processes of distribution are sometimes called functions of distribution. One writer on the subject includes two functions or processes: (1) demand creation and (2) physical supply; still another, four processes: (1) advertising, (2) selling, (3) delivering, (4) collecting. Whatever the analysis, each process or function involves expense items, the sum of which gives the cost of that function.

Assume for purpose of illustration that selling consists of two processes, (a) advertising and (b) personal selling. If in a given period the advertising costs are $200,000 and the personal selling costs are $1,300,000 for 100,000 units, the analysis of selling processes may be expressed as follows:

Advertising 

Personal selling

100,000 13 per unit $1,500,000 100,000 = $15 per unit

If the advertising is tripled, with a resulting decrease in personal selling of $300,000 and an increase in units sold of 60 per cent, an analysis of unit selling process costs would show: 

Advertising $ 600,000 ÷ 160,000 $ 3.75 per unit 

Personal selling

1,000,000 160,000 

6.25 per unit $1,600,000 160,000 $10.00 per unit

It is important for the management of any mercantile business to know what proportions of the various functions produce the sale of a unit of a product at the least cost. 

The services rendered by mercantile stores may well be accounted for separately. A knowledge of these costs from period to period will assist management in meeting its many and varied problems. For example, delivery service, if rendered, may be purchased from parcel delivery companies or provided by the store's own trucks. A knowledge of relative costs will assist in the determination of the better policy. While cost is not the only factor in such a choice, it is nevertheless an important factor and should not be omitted.

It has been said that it costs more to sell the world's goods than to produce them. Efforts are constantly being made to reduce the price margin between the producer and the consumer. 

If services are made available to the consumer that he does not need or wish, the margin may be narrowed by the elimination of such service costs. Thus, today, the margin on foodstuffs has been reduced through the elimination of delivery costs, credit costs, and the use of self-service. Careful studies of the costs of mercantile processes should point the way toward a more efficient system of distribution. 

Process Cost Accounting Other fields of business have use for in Other Fields knowledge of process costs. In railroad transportation, it is common practice to calculate, for freight services, the cost per ton mile; for passenger service, the cost per passenger mile. In motor truck operation, calculations are made periodically to determine the cost per mile and the cost per ton mile. In banks, costs are assembled and unit cost prices are determined in order to learn the relative profitableness of various depositor's accounts.

Outside the field of business, increasing use is being made of the principles of process costs. In educational institutions the

Ch. 24] 615 

cost per student and the cost per student hour are found from period to period. In hospitals the cost per patient and the cost per patient day have administrative uses. In all types of institutions cost studies and cost calculations are helpful to the administration and to those responsible for the institutional policies. 

The costs of services rendered by government are also receiving consideration. Much of the effort of experts in municipal accounting is pointed toward the discovery of various municipal costs. For example, tax payers are interested in knowing how the cost of fire protection or the cost of police protection in their cities compares with similar costs in other cities. 

E Process Costs and Busi- To each and every business operation some ness Operations cost is attached. To know the amount of this cost is to increase the intelligence applied to the administra- - tion of business operation. 

Cost accounting goes beyond the first, or original, expense. 

The cost of coal itself may not be so important as the cost of heating or the cost of horse power. The fact that delivery expenses in general have decreased from $3,000 to $2,500 may not be so significant as the fact that the cost per ton mile has increased from 10 cents to 12 cents. It is not the original outlay that is so important but rather the use made of that original outlay. Such use is expressed in the costs obtained by cost accounting. The results obtained from the expenditure are incorporated in the unit cost figures, and therein lie the utility and the promise of cost accounting.

CHAPTER XXV DEPARTMENTAL ACCOUNTING Departmental Income 

When a business sells several classes of commodities or services, the income from each class may be accounted for separately. It is then possible to know which classes are profitable and which classes are unprofitable. When management is so informed, it can develop policies much more intelligently. 

The total volume of sales may be broken down into classifications, each classification representing a particular type of commodity or service sold. To determine the gross profit or net profit for each sales classification, it is necessary to break down each item on the profit and loss statement that enters into gross or net profit. The result is a departmentalization of business operations and the accounting for the business as if it were a group of separate units instead of a single unit. Since the purpose is to departmentalize income, the increase in accounts is principally in profit and loss statement accounts.

Income and Costs by 

Departments and Branches

The purpose of the next two chapters is to illustrate the further development of cost analysis in the mercantile field. The departmental classification of commodities sold, together with related expenses, represents an extension of cost accounting into the business of merchandising. Just as the factory becomes a collection of departments in order to distribute costs more accurately, so sales activities are departmentalized in order that information for accounting control may be more specific. Similarly, accounts that depict as units the activities of branches, though these branches may be owned and controlled singly, require groupings of expenses similar in character to those discussed in the two previous chapters.

Need for Departmental In a small business the owner is concerned 

Revenue Accounts primarily with the net profit obtained as a result of trading in all classes of merchandise carried by the busi- 621

[Ch. 25 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

ness. In a large business, however, it is often desirable to classify the commodities bought and sold into two or more groups and to determine the profit made from each group. To illustrate: If a business deals in both hardware and furniture, it may be advisable to maintain accounts from which the profit made from each class of merchandise can be determined readily. The owner of the business can then judge the profitableness of the department handling each class of merchandise and can better plan for the future. If each department has a separate manager, the efficiency of the manager can also be better judged from the profit made by his department. 

There may be other reasons why the management of a business should have information regarding the results of trading in each group of commodities. The reasons given above, however, are sufficient to indicate that in a large business a classification of revenue accounts according to departments may be needed. Departmental Analysis The records of a departmentalized business in Accounts and Books should be so planned that they will supply of Original Entry all the information desired about each department. In a small business, such as a retail store in which the various classes of merchandise are handled together and are sold by the same sales force, the departmental records for each department will show only the gross profit on sales. The expenses will not be divided according to departments but will be grouped together.

The accounts of a business that is divided into departments should furnish information about the sales, the purchases, and the inventories of each department. If a more complete summary for each department is desirable, they should also provide a departmental analysis of sales returns and allowances, freight and drayage on both incoming and outgoing shipments, and purchases returns and allowances. If the accounts are to show this departmental analysis of trading operations, the books of original entry must be designed to provide the same analysis. This provision can best be made by the introduction of additional columns into the books of original entry. In order that the use of these special columns may be shown, the books of original entry for a business operating hardware and furniture departments will be illustrated.

Ch. 25] 623 

DEPARTMENTAL ACCOUNTING 

The Purchases Journal In the purchases journal shown in Illustration No. 82, the purchases of the firm of Barker and Barker have been recorded. This record is the same as the purchases journal that has been used and illustrated previously except for the two additional amount columns entitled 

Hardware Purchases Dr. and Furniture Purchases Dr. The amount of each purchase has been entered first in the Accounts 

Payable Cr. column and then in the Hardware Purchases Dr. or the Furniture Purchases Dr. column according to whether the purchase was for the hardware department or the furniture department.

The credits to the accounts of the vendors were posted currently. At the end of the month the three amount columns were totaled and the journal was ruled. The totals were posted to the accounts indicated in the columnar headings. If a bookkeeper using this type of purchases journal does not care to write the names of the accounts to which the totals are to be posted, he may indicate the posting by writing directly below the total the number of the page in the ledger to which the posting has been made. This method is illustrated in the purchases returns and allowances journal in Illustration No. 83 on page 624.

DEPARTMENTAL ACCOUNTING 

In a retail business there are many cash sales. These are usually recorded as daily totals. If a departmental analysis of sales is made, separate columns for the cash sales of the various departments may be provided in the cash receipts record, and the total of each column may be posted at the end of the fiscal period to the credit of the departmental sales account. If a busi- 

Iness sells largely on account, however, and makes relatively few cash sales, it may not wish to have in its cash receipts record a - separate column for the cash sales of each department. In this case the need for these special columns in the cash receipts record may be avoided by providing one special column for cash sales in -the sales journal.

For example, the credit and cash sales of a wholesale firm dealing in hardware and furniture are recorded in a sales journal having a special column entitled "Cash Dr." The journal in 

Illustration No. 85 shows the sales that were made during April 

E by this firm. Each sale on account was recorded in the same manner as it was in the journal shown in Illustration No. 84. The cash sales were recorded as totals, however, in the Cash Dr. column and in the column corresponding to the department in which these sales were made. The totals of the cash sales were also recorded in the cash receipts journal in the manner shown in Illustration 

No. 86 on page 626.

ILLUSTRATION No. 85, SALES JOURNAL WITH A COLUMN FOR CASH SALES 

Since the entries for cash sales in the sales journal were not to be posted to a separate account, they were checked in the Ledger Page column. The total of the Cash Dr. column was also checked in order to prevent double posting from the sales journal and the cash receipts journal. As a result the amounts of cash

[Ch. 25

sales were posted from the sales journal only as a part of the totals of the Hardware Sales Cr. and the Furniture Sales Cr. 

columns. In the cash receipts journal a check mark was placed in the Ledger Page column, opposite each entry for cash sales, to indicate that the entry was not to be posted. As a result the amounts of cash sales were posted from this book only as a part of the total of the Net Cash Dr. column. The equality of the debits and the credits was maintained in posting the columnar totals from both books because the credits to the hardware sales and the furniture sales accounts were equal to the debit to Accounts Receivable, posted from the sales journal, and the amount of cash sales posted from the cash receipts journal as a part of the total of the Net Cash Dr. column.

ILLUSTRATION No. 87, SALES RETURNS AND ALLOWANCES JOURNAL SHOWING DEPARTMENTAL ANALYSIS

Ch. 25] 627 

DEPARTMENTAL ACCOUNTING 

-Freight, Express, and Freight, express, and drayage on incoming

Drayage shipments of merchandise represent a part of the cost of the merchandise sold and must therefore be added to the cost price of the merchandise purchased. Freight, express, and drayage on outgoing shipments of merchandise represent the cost of delivering goods to customers. The drayage cost is almost invariably borne by the seller, but the freight or express charges are usually paid by the purchaser. 

If a business is departmentalized, the freight and drayage in should be departmentalized in order to obtain accurately departmental cost of sales. If this cost can be charged directly to the various departments, more accurate figures result. If it is not possible to charge it directly, it should be assigned on some satisfactory basis. If commodities in the several departments are of = similar bulk, the basis of purchases would be acceptable for such assignment.

Since freight and drayage outward is usually classified as a selling expense, it will not be necessary to departmentalize it, unless net profit by departments is to be obtained. If so, the basis for charging it to the several departments may follow that used for charging in-freight and drayage to departments. 

Operating Expenses 

A small departmentalized business may use only the departmental trading accounts that have been discussed in the preceding paragraphs. From these accounts the gross profit on the sales of each department can be determined. A large business will probably wish to have the expenses analyzed according to departments so that the net profit for each department can be determined. 

Whenever possible, the expenses of a department may be charged to the departmental expense accounts as the payments are made. For example, if a business having two departments pays salaries each week, the salaries may be charged to separate departmental accounts, such as Salaries of Sales Clerks, Department A and Salaries of Sales Clerks, Department B. 

Many expenses can be divided more conveniently at the end of the fiscal period than at the time they are paid or recorded. 

For example, the management may decide that rent is to be distributed to the various departments according to a fixed ratio. 

When the ratio is established, both the amount and the desirability of the space used by each department are taken into con-

[Ch. 25 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

sideration. Space in a prominent position on the first floor may be considered as being worth more than space on upper floors or in less prominent positions on the same floor. 

If the rent is paid monthly, it may be distributed to the departments at the time each payment is made and the departmental rent accounts may be charged directly. If the books are closed only once a quarter or once a year, however, it probably would be more convenient to charge all rent payments to a rent account and then to distribute the rent to the various departments at the close of the fiscal period. 

Apportionment of

Operating Expenses to Departments

When departmentalized statements of profit and loss showing net operating profit by departments are to be prepared, it becomes necessary to charge these expenses to the various departments. As in the case of process costs, discussed in the previous chapter, these departments naturally fall into two main divisions: (1) those departments that make sales or sell services and (2) those departments that assist or serve the selling departments. 

The departments in the second class are again called service departments. Their expenses should be charged against the selling departments in accordance with the cost of the service rendered these departments. For example, a departmentalized retail store may maintain a central office, with files, desks, and office employees, which serves the various selling departments. If the service rendered by this central office is proportionate to sales made, then its costs should be apportioned among the several selling departments according to sales. Or, a parking lot for the convenience of customers may be maintained at considerable cost. 

Customers make purchases in the various departments of the store. 

The cost of the parking lot should be apportioned to the various selling departments on some equitable basis. 

If there are a number of selling departments and a number of service departments, a columnar distribution sheet provides an effective accounting device for this apportionment. Such an expense distribution sheet is shown in Illustration No. 88 on page 629. 

This distribution sheet provides for the collection and distribution of the expenses of two service departments, (1) purchasing and (2) general office, to three selling departments designated A, B, and C. 

It also indicates in Column 2 the basis of distribution or apportionment. The Total column should agree with the sum of the

Ch. 25] 629 

DEPARTMENTAL ACCOUNTING 

12 selling department columns since all costs are eventually charged against one or another of the selling departments.

ILLUSTRATION NO. 92, STATEMENT OF PROFIT AND Loss OF A DEPARTMENTALIZED BUSINESS

[Ch. 25 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Adjusting and Closing the Accounts of a Departmentalized

Business The departmentalization of a business results in no essentially new problems in the adjusting and closing of the ledger. For each department, separate inventory, sales, sales returns and allowances, purchases, freight in, and purchases returns and allowances accounts are required. If the reports show only the gross profit of each department, the merchandise accounts for each department are closed into a trading summary account for that department. The balances of these departmental trading summary accounts are then closed into a profit and loss summary account for the enterprise as a whole. The expense accounts are closed into the latter account just as they are when a business is not departmentalized. The financial income and the financial expense accounts are also closed in the usual manner. If the net profit or the net loss of each department is determined, departmental profit and loss summary accounts are used, and only the net profits or the net losses of the various departments are closed into a general profit and loss summary account. 

1. Distinguish between departmentalization as it applies to manufacturing enterprises and as it applies to trading enterprises.
