CHAPTER XXVI BRANCH ACCOUNTING 

Branch Operations and In the attempt to increase volume of sales, Income many business concerns have established branch headquarters. Through operation at various points, the needs of consumers are more effectively met and sales are built up. Whenever sales occur at different locations, there is need for branch accounting of income and expense. Only in this way can the central management be informed of the nature of activities at the several branches.

The development of branch activity by large business corporations has been one of the outstanding movements in modern business. The corporation that sells merchandise in a hundred or a thousand stores over a state or over the entire country is a wellknown feature of present-day merchandising. The chain store, operating under a central management, but each with a branch manager in charge locally, has become an accepted characteristic of the business of today.

For efficient management, it is necessary to know the profitableness of each branch. The earning capacity of various locations under the several managers is an important part of branch administration. In order to justify its existence, a branch should show a profit. If branch accounting shows that a branch is showing a loss or a less than average profit, that branch deserves the serious consideration of the central management. 

In this chapter it will be possible to deal only with a central office and one or two branches. The fundamental considerations, however, are not materially changed when there are many branches. An understanding of the transactions of a home office and its branch should contribute to an understanding of this current economic phenomenon.

Organization for 

Branch Control 

The organization for the control of the branch depends upon the nature of the operations carried on by the branch. Any of the conditions described in the following paragraphs may exist: 643

[Ch. 26 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

(1) Some businesses establish branches that are to be maintained only as sales agencies. The chief function of such a branch is to take sales orders and to forward them to the general office for approval. If the order is approved as to credit, terms, and prices by the general office, that office makes shipment to the customer. The customer is invoiced from the general office, and collection is made by that office; consequently the sales record and the accounts receivable are kept at the general office. Under these conditions it is necessary to have only representatives of the sales department at the branch. A branch manager has supervision of the salesmen at the branch. The manager, in addition to acting as supervisor of the salesmen, spends a considerable part of his time in a sales capacity. He is under the control of the head of the sales department and reports to him. The only executive of the company who has any control over the branch, other than the sales manager, is the treasurer, who exercises supervision over the petty cash fund maintained at the branch. (2) The branch, though not maintaining an inventory, may invoice customers and make collections. In this case the branch must have a representative of the credit department who is able to judge the credit standings of the customers. The branch manager may act in this capacity, or he may delegate this duty to an assistant. In either case the one acting in this capacity is under the supervision of the general credit manager. If the branch makes collections, it exercises custody of the cash funds under the supervision and control of the treasurer. (3) The branch may maintain an inventory from which shipments are made to customers. It may maintain a sufficient inventory to fill all orders, or it may keep in stock only certain classes of merchandise and may forward orders for other merchandise to the general office. It may receive all of its inventory through the general office, or it may be authorized to purchase all or part of the merchandise from outside vendors. When an inventory is maintained, it is under the control of the merchandise manager or some other officer who is responsible for the proper maintenance of the inventory and for obtaining a satisfactory turnover. If purchases are made from outside vendors, these purchases are subject to the control of the general purchasing agent or the merchandise manager.

Ch. 26] 645 

BRANCH ACCOUNTING 

This discussion has dealt with branches of mercantile businesses. A business may have two or more factories that are used in the production of its commodity. These factories are not branches in the strict sense of the term, but are rather separate - units of the business. Each factory is usually under the control of a works manager or superintendent, who reports to the head of the production department or to the president. With this exception the control exercised over a factory by the general officers of a business is very similar to that exercised over a sales branch. 

Methods of Branch Accounting 

The following classification of branch accounting methods is not intended to be arbitrary. In any specific business, modifications will be found, and in many cases part of the accounting system will be centralized at the general office and other parts will be centralized at the - branch. Generally, however, the methods of accounting for branches are classified as follows:

First Method. No accounting records except those of a memorandum nature are kept at the branch. The books of original entry and the ledger records of the branch transactions are maintained at the general office. If only a few branches are maintained and the transactions performed by them are not too numerous, I the transactions may be entered in the general records of the home office. For example, the sales of the branches and of the general office may be entered in the same sales record; all cash transactions may be entered in one cash record; likewise, all purchases may be entered in one purchases record; and all miscellaneous transactions, in one journal. In this case only one ledger will be kept. If the branches are numerous and their transactions are large in number, separate records of original entry and a separate ledger are maintained for each branch. Reports that serve as the basis of entries in these books are sent to the general office by the branches.

In nearly all cases a branch is given a working fund, which is sufficient to meet its needs for a limited period of time. The branch reports its disbursements to the general office when it needs additional funds, and the general office sends it a check for the amount of the disbursements. The amount of this check brings the fund back to the original amount. In short, the working fund is maintained in the same manner as a petty cash fund.

[Ch. 26 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

The request for additional funds by the branch is accompanied by an original copy of the branch cash-disbursements record with the paid vouchers attached. The general office files the loose-leaf sheets in a binder and uses them as its record of the cash disbursements of the branch.

Second Method. The branch makes entries for all transactions that it performs, and forwards to the general office summaries of these entries. For example, the branch records all sales made by it, and at the end of the month sends to the general office a report showing the total to be debited to Accounts Receivable and credited to Sales. Similar reports are made for purchases, cash receipts, and cash disbursements. The general journal entries for the month are reported individually. The branch makes the periodic summaries on journal vouchers. The general office files the vouchers in loose-leaf binders and uses them as mediums of posting either to an individual ledger maintained for the branch or directly to the general ledger. 

Third Method. The branch maintains books of original entry and also a ledger. In short, it has a complete accounting system. 

In the ledger is a controlling account with the general office, which takes the place of the customary proprietorship accounts. 

In the ledger of the general office is a similar controlling account with the branch. Periodic reports, usually prepared monthly, are made to the general office; and from these reports entries are made in the records of the general office to adjust the controlling account with the branch. This method of branch accounting will be illustrated in the remaining pages of the chapter. 

Reciprocal Accounts in The general office controlling account with Branch Accounting a branch is an asset account, for it represents the ownership or investment in the branch. However, this is not true if shipments of merchandise from the central warehouse to the branch are priced at the selling price because in that case the controlling account includes an element of unrealized profit. 

There is no profit on such shipments until the goods are sold by the branch. In the illustrations given it is assumed that shipments are made at cost and in that case the controlling account with the branch is an asset account. 

This account is known as Branch Ledger and is debited for cash or other assets that the general office sends to the branch.

Ch. 26] 

647 

BRANCH ACCOUNTING 

Whenever a profit is reported by the branch, the general office records this in its books by a debit to the branch ledger account and a credit to a suitable income or proprietorship account. This method may be used, for any profit made by the branch is really a profit to the company and represents a corresponding increase in the ownership interest in the branch. Cash or other assets received from the branch or a loss reported by the branch is recorded as a credit to the account Branch Ledger, for the reduction in the net. assets of the branch represents a decrease in the ownership interest in the branch.

The branch keeps a reciprocal account known as General Ledger. This account really represents a liability of the branch to the general office. The receipt of cash or other assets from the general office is recorded as a credit to the general ledger account. 

When a profit is made by the branch, the general ledger account is credited, for a profit increases the liability to the general office. 

Whenever the branch sends cash or other assets to the general office or incurs a loss, the general ledger account is debited since the liability of the branch to the general office is decreased. 

The reciprocal nature of branch and general office accounts differs from that of factory ledger and general ledger described in the cost accounting chapter in that the branch may have transactions with outside business units. In the case of the cost accounting described in Chapter XXIII, all the transactions of the factory were with the general office. In the case of the branch, however, transactions not involving the general office as the second party will be presented. It is, therefore, not possible to say that the branch ledger and the general ledger are wholly reciprocal. Only certain specified accounts, such as Branch Ledger and General Ledger, are reciprocal.

The balances of the branch ledger account of the general office and the general ledger account of the branch will be equal if the procedure just outlined is followed by the general office and the branch.

Illustration of Account- The accounting procedure of a general ing for a Branch office and a branch does not differ from the usual accounting procedure except for the introduction of the branch ledger and general ledger accounts. To illustrate branch accounting, the following summary of the transactions performed by a branch is given:

[Ch. 26ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES

4. It paid operating expenses amounting to $2,500. 

5. It collected $3,000 on accounts receivable. 

6. It sent to the general office $10,000 in cash. 

The merchandise inventory of the branch at the end of the month amounted to $12,000.

Of the previous six transactions only the first and the last involve transactions between the general office and the branch. 

The other four transactions involve only the branch as one party and outside business units as the second parties.

The consolidation of the statements of several branches with that of the general office would be accomplished in a similar manner to that illustrated above with a single branch. The only difference would be a lateral expansion of the work sheet. Instead of one column for Branch Ledger, there would be as many columns as there were branches. Separate lines at the left would have to be left for Branch Ledger A, Branch Ledger B, etc.; as well as for 

Shipments A, Shipments B, etc. Work sheets for the consolidation of more than two operating units are illustrated in the following chapter.

Valuation of the Mer chandise Inventory of a Branch

The merchandise inventory reported on the balance sheet of the branch is usually valued at the same prices at which goods are billed by the general office to the branch. These prices are frequently higher than cost because the consigned merchandise is valued at selling prices. In all cases in which this inventory valuation is not on the basis of cost or market value, whichever is the lower, a reserve sufficient to reduce the inventory to its proper valuation is set up on the books of the general office. Upon the consolidation of the balance sheet of the branch with that of the general office, this reserve is treated as a deduction from the branch

[Ch. 26 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

inventory, and the branch inventory is reported at its net value in the consolidated balance sheet. 

When shipments to branches are billed at prices higher than cost, adjustments of the branch statement of profit and loss figures must be made if the true branch profit is to be known. It is simpler to follow the practice illustrated in this chapter and price shipments at cost, thus avoiding price adjustments in inventories and shipments accounts.

Accounting for Many The preceding illustrations concern the re- Branches lations of one branch to the general office. 

The principles would not differ if there were hundreds of branches, as there are today in many chain-store organizations. While there are unity of ownership and a central management, there must exist at the various branches separate managements having autonomy and directing operations within certain prescribed limits. The operating accounts must, if they are to be most useful, represent the operations of each of the branches. Sales and the expenses incident to sales must be identified with the several branches in which activities are carried on. 

At the end of each period the several branch statements need to be combined into consolidated statements for the enterprise as a whole. The consolidation of many statements follows the same general plan that is illustrated in this chapter, though each additional branch adds just that much to the detail to be incorporated in the consolidated statement. 

Departmental and branch accounting is only one illustration of the use of analytical accounts to provide detailed reviews of activities that are later to be consolidated into a single statement covering the enterprise as a unit. As the size of the business unit increases, the need for reporting currently the income, the costs, and the expenses of the parts of which the whole is composed increases to the same extent. From the point of view of the administrative control of operations through accounts, such departmental and branch analyses are extremely helpful. Analyses of Internal With the ever-increasing size of business Operations units, the need for internal accounting is likewise growing. It is necessary to account separately for the various parts of which these large units are made up. This ac-

Ch. 26] 655 

BRANCH ACCOUNTING 

counting involves first a breakdown of the accounts, departmentally or by branches, followed by a consolidation to show the unit as a whole.

In the large five-and-ten-cent store chains, for example, there is first a breakdown by commodities sold and second a breakdown by branches selling these commodities. For efficient administration of these large mercantile establishments, it is necessary to have the departmental analysis and the branch analysis. 

More and more attention will be directed in the future to accounting for small units under a central administration.

CHAPTER XXVII CONSOLIDATED STATEMENTS Analytic and Synthetic In Chapters XXIII to XXVI accounting 

Accounting Procedures procedures that break down total amounts into related parts have been discussed. The costs of a job order, of a process, or of operating a department or a branch are derived from an analysis of income and of cost or expense accounts. In this chapter accounting procedures that put together and report as units the properties, liabilities, ownership interests, and operating results of related units will be discussed. The former procedures are analytic; the latter are synthetic. 

Both types of procedures are useful to accountants in preparing statements for the various kinds of business organizations. 

When an enterprise is large, detailed analytical information prepared from the accounts assists the management in exercising intelligent control. When separate enterprises operate as a single economic or business unit, combined statements depict more accurately the financial position and the results of operations. When detailed information is called for, the accountant must be able to particularize; when a comprehensive survey is demanded, he must be able to summarize.

Trend Toward Business The past five decades have witnessed a 

Combination constant and determined trend towards business combination. This has taken place primarily in the effort of business owners to gain greater profits. Large-scale economies, the possible reduction of competition and the possibilities for better control of prices, and the extension of specialization in production have offered opportunities for greater earnings. Single ownership and unified control of combined corporate units have been obtained successfully in most cases by merger, by consolidation, or by the establishment of a holding company. 

Merger. A merger occurs when one corporation directly acquires the assets and assumes the liabilities of another. Payment for the net assets acquired may be made in cash, or capital stock of the acquiring corporation may be issued to the stockholders of 661

[Ch. 27 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

the acquired corporation. The latter corporation loses its individual corporate existence through the absorption. 

Consolidation. A consolidation involves the formation of a new corporation that acquires all the assets and assumes all the liabilities of several corporations that agree to combine. All the original corporations lose their individual corporate identities when the larger unit is formed. The new enterprise may issue stock to obtain funds with which to pay the stockholders of the original organizations for their ownership rights in those companies, or it may offer stock in the new enterprise in exchange for the stock that stockholders of the original companies held before consolidation. 

Holding Company. A corporation may own a controlling interest in the outstanding stock of another corporation; yet both retain their individual corporate identities. When such a situation exists, the organization that holds all or a majority of the outstanding stock of the other corporation is known as the holding company, or parent company; and the corporation that is controlled is known as the subsidiary. An organization that owns a controlling interest in the stock of another elects a majority of the board of directors and is consequently in a position virtually to control the activities of the subsidiary. Ownership of 51 per cent of the outstanding stock makes such control possible, although in some cases a smaller percentage gives a holding company sufficient voting power to elect directors of its own choice, especially wher the remaining stockholders are scattered widely and unorganized. 

Unlike the situation in a merger or a consolidation, a corporation that buys a controlling interest in the stock of another does not acquire the assets or assume the liabilities of the latter.

There is merely a transfer of stock ownership, and both corporations retain a separate legal existence. The entry made by the corporation that has purchased the stock of another may be as follows: 

Mar. 1 

Investment in Stock of Company A...

Cash (or Capital Stock) 

To record the purchase of 5,000 shares of the stock of Company A for cash (or in exchange for a like amount of stock). 

500,000 00 500,000/00

Ch. 27] 663 

CONSOLIDATED STATEMENTS 

Statements for Com- After merger or consolidation, little diffibined Companies culty is encountered in preparing the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss for the single enterprise, since all accounts are kept in one set of books. One organization actually owns all of the assets and is responsible for all of the liabilities. When the relationship is that of a holding company and a subsidiary, both companies retain their separate corporate identities, keep individual accounting records, and prepare separate statements at the end of each fiscal period. From the point of view of the holding company, however, it is desirable that the financial condition of both this company and the subsidiary, as well as the results of the operations of both for the period, be reported by means of consolidated statements. The two companies are so intimately interrelated that they are virtually one organization. The statements of a holding company that show the combined financial condition and operating results are the consolidated balance sheet and the consolidated statement of profit and loss. A number of problems that arise in the preparation of these statements will be discussed in this chapter. 

Consolidated Balance Sheet Prepared Upon the Acquisition of a 

Controlling Interest in Subsidiary

The balance sheet of a holding company shows an asset account representing the investment in the stock of the subsidiary. 

The consolidated balance sheet substitutes for the investment account the actual assets and liabilities of the subsidiary. This substitution is justifiable, for the holding company actually controls the net assets of the subsidiary through the ownership of a controlling interest in the stock of the latter company. When the assets and the liabilities of the holding company are combined with those of the subsidiary, and are thus presented in the form of a consolidated balance sheet, a better picture of the financial condition of the holding company is made available to any one who is interested. A consolidated work sheet is prepared to facilitate the elimination of the investment account and the substitution of the assets and the liabilities of the subsidiary. The following illustration involves the preparation of a consolidated work sheet and a consolidated balance sheet on the date when a particular corporation acquires a controlling interest in the stock of another corporation. 

On January 1 Company H purchases 80 per cent of the outstanding stock of Company A from stockholders of the latter for

[Ch. 27 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

$60,000 in cash. The entry made to record the purchase of this stock is given here in journal form. Jan. 1

Through the use of the work sheet the investment account on the books of Company H has been replaced by the assets and the liabilities of the subsidiary. The investment account represents only an 80 per cent interest in the capital stock and the surplus of Company A. Eighty per cent of the capital stock of $50,000 and of the surplus of $20,000 of Company A is therefore eliminated by entries of $40,000 and $16,000 in the debit column under the heading "Eliminations." These eliminations from the capital account and the surplus account of the subsidiary represent only the proportionate ownership that the holding company has acquired. The balances of $10,000 and $4,000 in the capital stock and surplus accounts represent the ownership interest of the minority stockholders. These balances are extended into the

[Ch. 27 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Consolidated Balance Sheet column. The total of the two amounts is reported as net worth of outsiders on the consolidated balance sheet.

Corresponding credits of $40,000 and $16,000 to the investment account of the holding company are entered in the credit column under the heading "Eliminations." Since these two amounts total only $56,000, it is evident that, by paying $60,000 Company H has paid more than book value for the stock of Company A. The excess of $4,000 is considered payment for the intangible asset goodwill. The $4,000, which is the difference be tween the debit balance of the investment account and the credit amount representing eliminations, is therefore extended into the Consolidated Balance Sheet column as the value of the goodwill. 

If the credits in the preceding paragraph had exceeded the amount in the investment account, a credit balance would have resulted. Such a credit balance is considered a credit to Capital 

Surplus. It is sometimes called negative goodwill, but, in fact, it represents the payment of a less amount than the equity acquired as measured by the accounts of the subsidiary corporation. For example, assume that Company H had acquired the 80 per cent interest in Company A at a cost of $50,000 instead of $60,000. The two credits of $40,000 and $16,000 would have exceeded the purchase price by $6,000. This amount would have appeared on the consolidated balance sheet as Capital Surplus. 

If a holding company owns a controlling interest in more than one company, the positive and negative goodwill items are combined in one figure on the consolidated balance sheet. If the net total is a debit, then goodwill is shown; if it is a credit, then it is shown in Capital Surplus. 

After the eliminations have been made on the work sheet, the amounts of the corresponding assets and liabilities of the related companies are combined, and the totals are extended into the Consolidated Balance Sheet column. The consolidated balance sheet is then prepared. This statement is shown at the top of page 667. 

The financial condition of Company H is presented more clearly by this consolidated report than it was by the individual balance sheets shown on page 664. The two companies are represented as a single organization, which is virtually their actual relationship. The investment of the holding company, as well as that proportion of the capital stock and the surplus of the sub-

The investment is not shown on the consolidated balance sheet because it does not represent an asset when the organizations are considered as a unit; the capital stock and the surplus of Company A that were acquired by Company H are not shown on the consolidated balance sheet because such amounts do not represent liabilities when the organizations are viewed as a unit. The elimination of the investment account of the holding company and the capital and surplus accounts of the subsidiary may be compared to the elimination of the branch ledger and general ledger accounts from the consolidated balance sheet of an enterprise having a branch. The equity in the subsidiary corporation not acquired by the holding company is reported as a separate item, Minority Interest. The excess of the payment over the book value of the stock acquired by the holding company is shown as the asset goodwill. The amounts of all similar assets and liabilities have been combined. 

The Investment Account 

The investment in the subsidiary may be carried on the books of the holding company at either of two values. The holding company may carry the investment in the subsidiary at cost, or it may take up profits and losses of the subsidiary. Since the latter method is similar in principle to the method of accounting for a branch, it will be used as the basis for the discussion in this chapter. 

When a profit is made by the subsidiary, the holding company will increase its investment by an amount equal to the proportion of the profit to which it is entitled. For example, assume that in the case of Company H and its subsidiary, Company A, which were mentioned previously, the profit of Company A for the year

[Ch. 27 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

is $10,000. This amount increases the surplus of Company A to $30,000. Company H, which has an equity of 80 per cent in the subsidiary, makes the following entry in its books: 

Dec. 131 Investment in Stock of Company A... 

Income from Subsidiary Company A.. 

To take up 80 per cent of the profit of $10,000 announced by Company A. 8,000|00 | | 8,000,00

The account Income from Subsidiary Company A will be closed into the surplus account of Company H, and the $8,000 will thus become available for distribution as dividends. Whenever the subsidiary ascertains the amount of a profit and closes it into the surplus account, Company H will take up its share of the profit and thus increase the investment. This procedure is followed because the two companies are so closely related that profits of the subsidiary are in reality profits of the holding company in proportion to the stock ownership of the latter in the subsidiary. 

If the surplus account of the subsidiary, Company A, is reduced as a result of a loss, Company H will debit an account such as 

Loss from Subsidiary Company A and credit the investment account for 80 per cent of the loss, or for that part which is proportionate to the ownership of the stock of the subsidiary company. 

If Company A announces a dividend of $4,000 when it determines its profit for the year, the holding company will make the following entry to record its share of this dividend: 

Dec. 31 Dividends Receivable, Company A..

Investment in Stock of Company A. 

To record 80 per cent of the dividend of $4,000 announced by Company A. 3,200 00 || | 3,200/00 This entry will merely convert a part of the asset represented by the investment into a current asset, dividend receivable. The amount of the investment will thus be decreased, for the subsidiary, in setting up the account Dividends Payable, has decreased its surplus by the amount of the dividend declared. When the dividend is paid by Company A on January 10, the holding company will make the following entry:

Dividends Receivable, Company A.. 

Received cash from Company A in payment of the dividend declared on 

Dec. 31, 19--. 3,200 00 3,200 00

Intercompany Eliminations In the case of a holding company and a subsidiary, a number of items other than the investment and the capital interest in the subsidiary may have to be eliminated in the preparation of the consolidated balance sheet at the end of the fiscal period. Some of the most common items that must be eliminated to show the true financial condition of the interrelated companies are as follows:

Advances. One member of the related group may obtain necessary funds by borrowing them from another member. Such an advance will appear as an asset on the books of the lending company and as a liability on the books of the borrower. Since this is only an intercompany item, it may be eliminated, for its presence on the consolidated balance sheet would carry no meaning and would result merely in inflating the assets and the liabilities. 

Accounts Receivable. The amount receivable that is due one company for goods sold to an affiliated member is eliminated against the corresponding amount payable by the company that made the purchase.

Notes Receivable. When one company receives a note as an acknowledgement of the indebtedness of another member of the affiliated group, the amount recorded as a note receivable by the former is offset against the amount recorded as a note payable by the latter.

Ch. 27] 671 

CONSOLIDATED STATEMENTS 

Bonds. When one company has purchased bonds of another, the investment of the former is offset against the liability of the company that issued the bonds.

Intercompany items such as interest receivable and interest payable or dividends receivable and dividends payable are also eliminated in the preparation of the consolidated balance sheet. 

Care must be taken, however, to eliminate only such items as are intercompany in character; any amount due from outsiders or owed to them must be shown on the consolidated balance sheet. 

Intercompany Profit on Sales of merchandise by one company to an 

Inventory affiliated organization are made just as they would be to outsiders. Hence part of the profit earned by the selling company from the total sales made during the period will have been realized on sales made to affiliated companies. At the end of the fiscal period, some of the merchandise that was purchased by an affiliated company may still be on hand. In the books of this company the inventory will be valued at cost, which includes the margin of profit made by the seller. From the point of view of the consolidated organization, the profit included in the value of this inventory has not been realized and must therefore be eliminated.

To illustrate: Company B owns a 100 per cent interest in 

Company C. Merchandise valued at $5,000, which was purchased from Company C, is included in the inventory of Company B. 

This merchandise cost Company C $4,000; hence it was sold to 

Company B at a profit of $1,000. Company B will take up the entire profit announced by Company C, for it has a 100 per cent equity in the latter enterprise. The profit earned by Company C, however, includes the $1,000 profit that was made on sales to Company B and that is now included in the value of the inventory of the latter company. Since this profit has not actually been realized by the consolidated organization, it is necessary to deduct $1,000 from the surplus account of Company B and to credit a like amount to an account called Reserve for Intercompany Profit on 

Inventory. The latter account represents a valuation reserve, and on the consolidated balance sheet the amount is treated as a leduction from the merchandise inventory. 

A problem slightly more difficult is encountered when the holding company does not own all the stock of the subsidiary.

[Ch. 27 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

For example: Company H owns an 80 per cent interest in Company A. Merchandise that cost Company A $150,000 is sold to 

Company H for $200,000. At the end of the year Company H has on hand merchandise purchased for $20,000 from Company A. In taking up 80 per cent of the profit that Company A has earned during the year, Company H will acquire 80 per cent of the profit of $50,000 that was made by Company A on sales to Company H. The surplus of Company H will therefore be increased $40,000 as a result of the profit on intercompany sales. 

Since merchandise worth $20,000, or equal to one tenth of the total amount sold by Company A, remains as an inventory on the books of Company H, one tenth of the $40,000 does not represent actual profit. Four thousand dollars is therefore deducted from the surplus account of Company H, and a reserve for intercompany profit on inventory is established for a like amount. 

The consolidated balance sheet that is prepared after these eliminations and adjustments have been made will show accurately the financial condition of the interrelated companies. 

The manner of preparing the consolidated balance sheet when eliminations and adjustments such as these must be made is illustrated on succeeding pages.

Consolidated Statement A consolidated statement of profit and loss of Profit and Loss is presented together with a consolidated balance sheet if the results of operations and the financial condition of the affiliated group as a single unit are desired. In the preparation of the consolidated statement of profit and loss, a work sheet may be used to facilitate the elimination of intercompany items.

Sales made by one company are purchases on the records of the buyer. Since the purpose of the consolidated statement of profit and loss is to show the results of activity between interrelated companies and the outside world, intercompany sales and purchases must be offset against each other and thus eliminated. 

If this were not done, the sales as well as the purchases would be overstated because they would include intercompany transactions. 

Other intercompany items on the individual statements of the holding company and the subsidiaries are similarly eliminated. 

Interest paid by one company will be reported as an expense on the statement of that company and as income on the statement

Ch. 27] 673 

CONSOLIDATED STATEMENTS 

of the company that received the interest. This item must be eliminated. Amounts paid and received as rent between interrelated companies, or for other services, must also be eliminated. 

Any part of the merchandise inventory at the end of the period that represents merchandise purchased from an affiliated company must be reduced to its actual cost.

The surplus of $115,000, shown on the consolidated balance sheet, represents the surplus of $32,800 reported on the consolidated balance sheet of January 1, 19--, plus $82,200, the holding company's share of the net income for the year. The determination of the total net income of the holding company for the year is shown below the work sheet for the consolidated statement of profit and loss (page 676).

The consolidated balance sheet portrays the financial position of a group of interrelated companies as if but one company existed.

Ch. 27] 679 

CONSOLIDATED STATEMENTS 

The intercompany assets, liabilities, and ownership interests are eliminated. The report therefore shows the financial position of the group as a unit in relation to the outside world. 

In a similar manner the consolidated statement of profit and loss shows the results of the operations of the group as a unit. 

Any items representing intercompany operations are eliminated, and the results of only those transactions that were performed with the outside world are shown.

ILLUSTRATION No. 102, CONSOLIDATED BALANCE SHEET FOR A HOLDING COMPANY AND ITS SUBSIDIARIES 

Such statements show more accurately the exact financial position and the results of operations than do separate statements for the individual members of the group. As long as the control of the several companies is vested in one holding company, intercompany equities, claims, and operations are not a significant part of the larger economic unit. With the central control it is the financial position and the financial progress of the entire group of related corporations that is significant. 

Consolidated statements, however, do not supplant statements for the individual companies. Since each company is a legal unit with all the responsibilities and obligations that are attached to such a unit, it is necessary to have statements for each company, especially to determine its relation to creditors and to determine the position of minority stockholders. Consolidated statements are therefore supplementary to the statements for the individual companies.

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

1. (a) When are consolidated statements prepared? (b) What is the chief problem involved in their preparation? (c) What accounting device is best adapted to their preparation? 

2. The General Steel Company merges with the Acme Steel Cor poration and the United Steel Company, Inc. The General Coal 

Company acquires controlling interests in the Acme Coal Corporation and the United Coal Company, Inc. How will these combinations affect the balance sheets and the statements of profit and loss of the various companies? 

3. What effect has a holding company on the participation in profits by the minority shareholders in its subsidiary? 

4. Company H owns 60 per cent of the stock of Company A and all the stock of Company B. What part of the stock of Company 

A and of Company B is reported on the consolidated balance sheet? Explain. 

5. The stock of Company C stands at $400,000 on the balance sheet of Company H. Company C has $400,000 of stock outstanding and a surplus of $100,000. Company H owns a 60 per cent interest in Company C. What is the goodwill figure for the consolidated balance sheet? 

6. The Grube Company owns 75 per cent of the stock of the King 

Company, a subsidiary. Give the journal entries that would be made by the Grube Company for the following: 

Dec. 31. The King Company reports a profit of $20,000. 

Jan. 15. The King Company declares a $16,000 dividend. 

Feb. 1. The Grube Company received its dividend check. 

7. Company F owns 70 per cent of the stock of Company G, of which there is $200,000 outstanding. In addition, Company G has a surplus of $50,000. If the consolidated balance sheet of the companies shows goodwill of $10,000, which arises from Company F's ownership of Company G, then at what figure is Company F carrying its investment in Company G?

A's "Investment in Y" account is $120,000, and the capital stock and surplus of Company Y are $100,000 and $40,000 respectively,

10. Company B owns $300,000 of the capital stock of Company C, which has outstanding stock with a par value of $500,000. At the end of one year Company B has an inventory of $100,000 that was acquired from Company C. This merchandise cost the latter company $75,000. Company B was $150,000. At what amount should the inventory be valued on the consolidated balance sheet if the holding company owns 60 per cent of the stock of Company A and all the stock of Company B? 

12. Company A owns 80 per cent of Company B. Company B purchased during the year $50,000 in merchandise that cost Company A $40,000. At the end of the year Company B has $10,000 of this merchandise in its inventory. Make in journal form the adjustment that would be necessary on the consolidated balance sheet. 

13. On January 1, 1939, Holding Company H bought 60 per cent of the stock of Company A; this investment is recorded on the books of Company H on December 31, 1939, as $300,000. During the year Company A announced a net profit of $80,000 and declared dividends of $20,000. At what price did Company H record the investment on January 1, 1940?

Accounting for Estimated Future Trans actions 

CHAPTER XXVIII BUDGETS 

In previous chapters the accountant has been considered a historian who deals with the effects of past transactions. He has been primarily concerned with the recording and summarizing of actual happenings. His accounting has had nothing to do with the plans of management until those plans were crystallized into actual business transactions. 

Business transactions are normally the result of plans made by the various executives who constitute the management. Before any sales are made, the sales manager plans their making. 

Before any goods are produced, the factory manager plans their production. Before any funds are raised through a bond issue, the details of the raising are in the mind of the financial officer. All these plans have a single objective, to improve the financial position of the company. 

It is advisable to attempt to measure the financial effect of the plans on the business. In this measuring the accountant may render management a service by ceasing for the moment to be a historian and by concerning himself with the transactions planned by the managing group. He assembles these plans for future transactions into an estimated statement of profit and loss and an estimated balance sheet. He reports the effect of the co-ordinated plans before any action takes place. This preliminary report puts the plans down in black and white for permanent reference. And, in so doing, a conservatism is added to the executives' plans for the coming fiscal period.

This dealing with estimated future transactions is called budgeting. If the plans deal with all the transactions of the ensuing period, they are known as a master budget. If they deal only with transactions affecting cash, they are called a cash budget. If they involve only income and expense transactions, they are called an operating budget. The master budget will form the principal subject matter of this chapter. 706

Ch. 28] 707 

BUDGETS The Budget Period 

In accounting projected into the future, as well as in accounting dealing with the past, a period must be set up. The period to be covered by planning ordinarily equals the period covered by reports on the past. Hence it happens that the most commonly used budget period is the year. 

Just as the accounting year is broken down into quarters or months, so is the budget year divided into shorter intervals. The annual budget with a "break-down" into months is the type of - budget commonly found. The planning of the next year's operations is the prevailing budget procedure. 

In some planning, the period is longer than a year. If the plans involve the purchase of fixed assets that will last several years, and a corresponding permanent increase in output, the budget period for such plans must necessarily be longer. Public utilities, such as the telephone company, budget five or ten years ahead. Automobile companies budget a year at a time because of yearly models offered consumers.

Planning and Doing in In the development of scientific business Business Management management the separation of planning and doing has been a marked feature. Complete plans are drawn up before actual operations are begun. The proposed course of action is first put down on paper and approved by all of the executives concerned.

This planning inevitably involves future transactions. The sales manager plans to increase sales. To provide the goods to be sold, the factory superintendent plans to expand the plant and to increase employment. The financial officer plans to extend # additional credit and to seek new sources of capital. All of these plans anticipate the actual transactions of the new period. 

If these planned transactions are organized in accounts and set up in a balance sheet and a statement of profit and loss, a more complete picture of the effect of the plans on the business will be available. The cost of expanded operations may be so large that net profit is reduced. In such a case contraction rather than expansion may be more desirable. The net effect of all the proposed activities should be carefully considered by the management before such plans are put into action. 

In budgeting, planned transactions are accounted for. If sales are to be increased 25 per cent from last year's $600,000, an

[Ch. 28 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

entry may be made in the budget working papers, debiting accounts receivable and crediting sales for $750,000. If new machinery costing $150,000 must be purchased to provide for the increase in sales, and bonds payable at 6% must be issued in part payment, the following entry would be made: 11 

Machinery. 

Bonds Payable. 

Cash.. 

150,000 00 100,000/00 

50,000 00

In addition, provision must be made for additional depreciation on machinery and for interest on the bond issue. All of the planned transactions are thus accounted for, and the net effect is shown in an estimated balance sheet and an estimated statement of profit and loss. Such accounting activity is an important part of budgeting. 

If the resulting statements appear favorable and desirable, the planned operations should be distributed among the months of the new year. Actual transactions month by month should be compared with the estimated transactions. This comparison of real transactions with estimated transactions period by period is one of the valuable features of budgeting. 

Essentials of Budgetary In a small company planning is usually 

Control done in an informal way by means of conferences between the chief executive and his assistants. When a company becomes sufficiently large so that the chief executive must delegate a considerable amount of responsibility to his assistants, planning activities by such informal means becomes increasingly difficult. Experience indicates that more desirable results are obtained if each subordinate is required to submit to his superior a formal statement of what he plans to accomplish and what will probably be the cost of performing the activities for which he is responsible. The preparation of such statements by the executives of a company, and the co-ordination of these statements into a well-balanced program for the business as a whole, result in budgetary control.

The procedure by which budgetary control is obtained must, of necessity, vary from business to business. Rarely can any two enterprises follow exactly the same procedure. In all cases, however, the essential features of budgetary control are:

Ch. 28] 709 

BUDGETS 

1. The statement of the plans, in the form of estimates, for all the departments of the business for a certain period of time. 

To be really useful, these estimates should be drawn up by means of co-operative effort. 

2. The co-ordination of these estimates into a well-balanced program for the business as a whole. 

3. The preparation of reports showing a comparison between. the actual and the estimated performances; and the revision of the original plans when these reports show that such a revision is necessary. 

1. Departmental Estimates. Each department should prepare an estimate of its activities for the budget period. The method of stating these activities depends on the nature of the operations of the department. The sales department should usually show its estimated sales in both volume and value, and it should also show its estimates of the cost of making these sales. The production department should state the volume of production that it plans for the period and the amount of materials, labor, and manufacturing expenses that it estimates will be necessary to carry out this program. The purchasing department, the office manager's department, and similar departments should indicate their estimated expenditures for the period. Finally, the financial department, using all the other estimates as a basis, should estimate the cash receipts and the cash disbursements for the period. 

Accurate estimates of future operations are admittedly difficult to prepare. Nevertheless plans must be made for the future; and the more difficult it is to plan, the more urgent it is that plans be made. Even though the departmental estimates are not entirely accurate, they are better than no estimates at all. There must be some chart for the future even if it is in rough outline. 

2. Co-ordination of Estimates. Because of the interdependence of business departments, some departments will need the estimates of other departments in making their own estimates. The sales, production, and finance departments must work on the same fundamental plan. They must agree on the quantity and kinds of goods to be produced and sold.

In a small business this co-ordination may be accomplished by having the executives discuss their plans informally with one another. In larger concerns, better results are obtained if a definite procedure is established for the co-ordination of the plans into a summarized budget for the business as a whole. In this case the

[Ch. 28 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

co-ordination can be accomplished by having the departmental heads transmit their estimates to an executive in charge of budgetary procedure. This executive combines the estimates into a master budget and submits this budget, together with the supporting estimates, to a budget committee composed of all the principal executives with the president as chairman. 

This committee considers the proposed budget and makes such revisions as it thinks necessary. The departmental heads, as members of the budget committee, are given an opportunity to defend their original estimates. After the budget has been approved by the budget committee, it may be submitted to the board of directors for final consideration. The budgetary procedure should be so organized that the executives will receive their approved budgets a sufficient time before the beginning of the budget period to enable them to make appropriate plans for the enforcement of the budgets. 

3. Budget Reports. Each department should make plans to carry out the program outlined in the budget. For example, the advertising department makes contracts for advertising space; the sales department sets quotas for its salesmen; the production department sets up schedules of production. Records should be established to show the performance of each department in a form that can be compared with the budget of the department. This means that the classifications in the budgets should correspond with the classifications in the accounts. In fact, a budget should be regarded as a statement of future accounts made in terms of units of responsibility.

Periodic reports comparing the estimated and the actual performance for the period should be made. These should go to the heads of the departments, the budget committee, and the board of directors. After considering these reports, the budget committee and the board of directors should make such revisions as they think necessary. The budget reports are a very essential part of budgetary control. If each executive knows that a comparison of his estimate and his actual performance is to be made, he will exercise greater care in both the preparation and the enforcement of his budget. These reports should be designed to show the following: (a) actual this month, (b) budget this month, and (c) increase or decrease. Such a report is shown in Illustration No. 103 on page 711.

ILLUSTRATION No. 103, BUDGET REPORT

[Ch. 28 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Budgetary Control Not All business firms practice budgetary con a New Idea trol to a greater or less degree, although many of them do not realize the fact. When rates of depreciation are established on fixed assets, a budget on the life of these assets is made; when standard dividend rates are approved by the board of directors, a budget on the earning capacity of the organization is established; when the salaries of employees are agreed upon, a budget on the earning capacity of the employees is made. All these budgets are changed from time to time as experience shows the need for correction in the original estimates. 

It may be found that the assets are depreciating more rapidly than was originally estimated; that the earning capacity of the business is greater or less than was originally believed; that the employees are more or less efficient than was originally supposed. 

In the same manner estimates of sales, production, finances, and expense should be made in the light of the best information available; and these estimates should be changed whenever more complete or more accurate information so dictates. 

Businessmen are coming to realize that the co-ordination of departmental activities is necessary for the maintenance of a unified business policy and that this co-ordination can be accomplished best by having the plans of each department submitted in writing for the consideration of the management. The budget system provides a systematic method of gathering information from the past and the present, of formulating plans for the future on the basis of this information, and of subsequently reporting how these plans have been carried out. 

Procedure for Master The master budget is usually the product Budget of the budget committee. It co-ordinates the departmental budgets and organizes them into a single budget for the business as a whole. In the master budget are found all of the estimated transactions for the coming year. When these transactions are accounted for, the estimated financial position at the end of the coming year, together with the estimated financial progress during the year, will be available. It is the function of the accountant to express the effect of the budgeted transactions in these two reports.

In the construction of these reports, the statement of profit and loss and balance sheet of the past year are points of reference.

Ch. 28] 713 

BUDGETS 

The amounts of proposed activities are expressed as fractional parts or percentages of the amounts for the year just past. Because of his knowledge of these past statements and his ability to account for transactions, the accountant is peculiarly fitted to serve the budget committee in the preparation of the accounting statements in the master budget.

The amount of sales is perhaps the most significant single figure in the statement of profit and loss aside from net profit. 

The estimated sales of the new period is the logical beginning point. 

If the business outlook is unfavorable, the sales manager may estimate a 25 per cent decline in sales. If the unit price of the commodity sold is reduced 10 per cent, he may believe it possible to maintain the volume of sales. The determination of the best sales policy for the new year is a problem involving not only the sales manager but also all the members of the executive group. 

For example, if the sales of 1938 consisted of 10,000 units of A at $30 and 20,000 units of B at $15 with a total volume of sales of $600,000, the sales manager might estimate a decline of 25 per I cent in the volume if prices remain the same. This would give 

7,500 units of A at $30 and 15,000 units of B at $15, or $450,000. 

If A units were reduced in price to $27.50 and B units to $12.50, he might estimate that sales volume could be maintained. This would give 10,000 units of A at $27.50 and 20,000 units of B at $12.50 or $525,000, a decline of only 1212 per cent in sales volume. 

At the budget meeting it would be necessary to determine the better policy, an important factor in which is the net profit resulting. Because of fixed charges, such as depreciation on machinery, taxes, and insurance on fixed assets, the costs of manufacture per unit would doubtless be increased if volume of output were reduced. If volume were maintained, reduced costs of variable charges such as materials and labor might compensate for the reduced selling prices.

If, in place of contraction, the outlook promises increased volume and the sales manager estimates an increase of 25 per cent at the existing prices, the effect of such increase in output on production policies and costs must be considered. The increased volume in sales may make advisable the installation of new machinery that would reduce labor costs. The payment for the new machinery may require that additional stock be sold or that bonds be issued. It may require expansion of sales force and increased advertising appropriation.

[Ch. 28 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

The transactions incident to the proposed plans should be set up in "T" accounts on working paper. First, there should be entered the balances as shown by the balance sheet at the end of the past fiscal period. The transactions in the master budget are thus added to the financial position at the beginning of the year. 

Since the master budget ordinarily includes the ending inventories, the estimated depreciation, and loss from bad debts, the trial balance prepared from the "T"" accounts is an adjusted trial balance. It therefore provides, without further change, the amounts for the two estimated statements. 

It may seem odd to prepare in January a statement of profit and loss for the year ending in the following December and a balance sheet for a date almost a year hence. But to do this sets up a pattern with which actual transactions may be compared. 

It also co-ordinates in the financial statements the proposed actions for the coming year. .

In order to carry out the comparison month by month, it is necessary, as stated before, to break down the annual budget into twelve monthly budgets. In this way annual sales are identified with each of the months in the fiscal year. Similarly, production plans and selling plans are expressed by months as well as by the year. This break-down makes possible monthly comparisons similar to that illustrated on page 711. 

Illustration of Account- The balance sheet as of December 31, 1939, ing for a Master Budget and the statement of profit and loss for the year ending December 31, 1939, for the Kern Mercantile Company are given on pages 715 and 716. In addition, there are given the budgeted transactions for the year 1940, as planned at the final meeting of the budget committee. The accountant is asked to show the effect of the budgeted transactions on the financial position of the company together with a budget of operations.

Advantages of Bud getary Control 

The primary advantages obtained from the preparation and the use of budgets are: 

1. The employees who prepare the budgets are educated to think through their problems thoroughly.

Co-operation between the executives and their assistants is promoted. 

3. An effective method of co-ordinating all the activities of the business is provided. 

1. Education of Employees. If a proper plan of budgetary procedure is employed, departmental heads, foremen, field supervisors, and even salesmen should obtain benefits from the preparation and the use of budgets. If budgets are to be prepared intelligently, all those who are responsible for their preparation must

52,751 00 think through their problems thoroughly before they submit their future plans in the form of estimates. For example, when the manager of a branch is asked to prepare a budget for his branch for a year, he must try to anticipate the various problems of his branch for the next year, and he must solve these problems before he prepares his estimates.

The employee who is required to make a budget is educated gradually to anticipate his problems and to plan in advance how to handle these. Experience has shown that most problems can be met satisfactorily if plans are made in advance. The budget

Ch. 28] 721 

BUDGETS 

therefore serves a very useful purpose in educating the members of an organization to think more about the problems with which they have to deal. 

Intelligent budgeting also necessarily means considerable cost analysis. Proper cost analysis may bring to light excessive, undue, or unnecessary expenses, or more probably it may make known the relative cost of one commodity or service offered for sale as compared to that of another commodity or service. The defining of actual cost may thus indicate where reductions in expenses can be accomplished and which lines of activity may profitably be given encouragement. 

2. Co-operation between Executives and Assistants. When an employee submits to his superior, at the beginning of each budget period, a definite statement of what he is planning to do during the coming period, the executive is given an opportunity to discuss with his assistant the plans of the latter and to guide the latter in his thinking with reference to the method of handling his problems. The employee also is provided an opportunity to present in a definite form his ideas concerning what he thinks should be done in handling the activities for which he is responsible.

The modern tendency in management is to place larger responsibility on subordinates. When the responsibility of subordinates is great, an effective means must be provided for controlling the activities of the subordinates. The budget provides the executive with a definite means of exercising such control since he can ascertain from the budgets what the plans of his assistants are before these plans are put into execution. The executive can ascertain by a study of the budgets submitted by his assistants whether or not their plans are in harmony with the policies established by the board of directors, and the executive can see by a study of the budgeted expenses whether his assistants are planning to comply with those policies affecting expenses. 

Likewise, if the company has adopted the policy that the sales of some products should be increased and that the sales of other products should be curtailed, the sales executive can see by a study of the estimated sales set up by the field organization whether or not the plans for future sales are in harmony with this policy. 

3. Co-ordination of the Activities of the Business. The relation ship between the activities of the various departments of a business

[Ch. 28 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

is so close that no one department can carry on its activities properly without a consideration of the activities of one or more of the other departments. Nor can the executives of a business judge properly the past or the contemplated activities of any one department independently of the activities of other departments. 

To make possible the preparation of a well-balanced program for the business as a whole, it is essential that the plans of all the departments be presented for consideration and that modifications of the plans of the several departments be made, if necessary, to bring about co-ordination.

If a sales estimate is prepared and co-ordinated with an estimate of production or an estimate of purchases, difficulties can, in the main, be eliminated. Any disagreement between the sales department and the production or the buying department is settled by the budget committee, which is qualified to render impartial judgment.

Importance of Budgets to Organizations Not 

Earning Profits

Since organizations that do not earn profits are interested primarily in providing a particular service to their members at cost, and since income must be provided to cover expenses or expenses must be limited to the income received, budgets play a very necessary and important role in the financial management of such organizations. In the case of a state-supported school the activities for the year can not be planned definitely until the income to be received is known. Very often a proposed budget for activities is drawn up for presentation to the legislative body of the state in order that that body will know the need for funds when it decides what sum may be provided for the school. In other cases in which an organization is interested primarily in providing a certain service for its members, the cost of providing this service must be estimated in order that the amount to be collected from the members for the service may likewise be calculated. A budget is the best means of matching estimated income against proposed expenditures.

In the case of political units, the Federal Government, state governments, and local governments, the budget is an important feature of public administration. The chief administrative officer plans in advance the scope of the various activities under his administration and includes in this plan the costs. This con-

Ch. 28] 723 

BUDGETS 

Estitutes the budget of his administration and is usually submitted for approval to the tax-levying body. The rates and kinds of taxes are an important factor in the preparation of every govern- = mental budget.

Similarly with consumer units, the head of the household plans in a more or less systematic manner his transactions for the ensuing fiscal period. He may plan to include transactions for the purchase of a radio, a new automobile, or a new house if the figures as set up on the personal budget seem to warrant such a transaction. The personal budget deals with estimated future -transactions and should be compared periodically with the actual transactions. Further consideration of this subject will be given in the following chapter on personal accounting.

Since the nature and amount of governmental and personal income can be estimated more accurately than business income, the budget will be more comparable with actual transactions of the ensuing fiscal period. There is, therefore, a tendency to confuse budgeting with accounting in these cases. The recording of the actual transactions is the historical phase of accounting and should be distinguished from the planned transactions of the future, which is the budget. Estimated tax revenue is a budgeted amount; the actual receipts from tax revenues are an accountable fact and therefore an acceptable transaction.

7. "Fifty-one out of 93 companies reported that the executives in charge of budgets are either the treasurer or controller." This seems to differ from the procedure described in the chapter. Can you explain?

13. The sales of the King Manufacturing Company for 1939 consisted of 40,000 units at $20. The cost of manufacturing these units was $15. Selling expenses were $80,000; general and administrative expenses, $40,000. The sales manager estimates he can sell 50,000 units in 1940 if the price is reduced $2 per unit.

14. E. P. Wright, the owner of a large retail store, believes that budgeting will ultimately displace accounting. He argues that accounting deals with the past, about which nothing can be done.

Company or the Smith Manufacturing Company. Will the time come when budgeting and accounting will be stressed equally in the control of both governments and businesses?

CHAPTER XXIX PERSONAL ACCOUNTING 

The Individual and In the last analysis whatever property is Business of value in society is owned by the individuals who compose that society. Whatever is done by business enterprises is done to satisfy the wants of individuals who are willing and able to pay for the satisfaction of those wants. It would be incomplete to consider business enterprises, principally large corporations, as being independent of individuals. People own and operate corporations for the purpose of earning profits, which are distributed to them as dividends, salaries, and bonuses. 

Corporations are able to operate and to earn profits because people are willing to pay for the commodities or the services of these corporations. Either directly or indirectly business property and business operations are "of the people, by the people, and for the people."

Accounting, the tool of business enterprise, studied in previous chapters with reference to business property and activities, may also be a useful tool of the individual. Just as accounting serves business by recording in a systematic manner the transactions of business and summarizing those transactions periodically in the accounting statements, so it may render a similar service to those individuals for whose sake business enterprises exist. 

The balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss may as well report on the business of living as the business of a corporation. The ledger may contain accounts depicting the effect of transactions on the property, the liabilities, and the proprietorship of the individual just as it does those of a business concern. Books of original entry may record, in conformity with some unified scheme of account classification, the transactions of the individual. 

Business papers evidencing transactions may be found in the private safety deposit box of the individual as well as in the vaults of the incorporated business. The Business of Living In economic society as it exists today, it is impossible for people to carry on without engaging in business transactions. These transactions affect the 734

Ch. 29] 735 

PERSONAL ACCOUNTING 

financial position of the ultimate consumer just as truly as they affect the business enterprise. When an automobile is purchased for private use, the transaction represents not only a sale for the automobile dealer but also the acquisition of a fixed asset by the consumer. When a railroad ticket is purchased by an individual, not only a sale of transportation service is made by the railroad, but also transportation expense or vacation expense is incurred by the buyer.

The business transaction in which the ultimate consumer is one of the parties is the logical conclusion of all previous transactions involving business entities. Without the final personal transaction, previous transactions would prove futile. Accounting is incomplete if it does not comprehend the ultimate consumer. 

The ultimate consumer as the party in final business transactions needs an appreciation of the consequences of his transactions upon himself as a business unit. While corporations, by employing individuals in an expert capacity as agents, have in many - cases eliminated the need for individuals to act as entrepreneurs, each individual in his personal transactions is compelled to play the role of an entrepreneur. He buys and uses commodities and services in the business of living. He exchanges his services and time for that which he can use to buy the commodities and the services that he himself consumes. These transactions inevitably affect his financial position and his financial progress. 

Present-day emphasis on big business gives rise to the false conception that enterprise is in the hands of the few. Enterprise may be exhibited in the affairs of each man as truly as it can in those of the business concern. The business of living is a succession of transactions involving purchases of services and property to be consumed or used. It is likewise a succession of transactions involving the exchange of personal services and invested capital for the income required to meet the aforementioned expenses. 

In this country most of the people can be identified as parts of family units. Each family unit represents one household under a unified domestic government, including parents, children, and others who live under the same roof. Personal accounting is, in large part, family accounting, though there may be instances in which individuals within the family unit are sufficiently autonomous to warrant individual accounting comparable to the accounting for a branch or subsidiary company. In this chapter

[Ch. 29 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

emphasis will be placed on the dominant form of personal accounting-accounting for the family unit.

Other Aspects of Per- The gradations of distinction between an sonal Accounting individual's professional or business activity and his personal life are many. A retired businessman may have so many property and income interests that his retirement is rather a change from one kind of business activity to another. 

Such a person doubtlessly benefits from a unified and co-ordinated record of his transactions. His transactions, however, are more like those of the sole proprietor than those of the family head. 

The professional man—the doctor or the lawyer-needs accounting for his professional activities, but this accounting resembles that of the sole proprietor. Such professional and postbusiness activities are more similar to the business activities previously described than to the transactions of the family head. 

In this chapter emphasis will be placed on accounting for living as if living in an economic society were a business problem. 

The Personal Balance 

Sheet is owned directly; the rest is owned indirectly. Public property is owned indirectly, and ostensibly equally, by the citizens who comprise the public. Public property includes such items as schools and universities, highways, parks, state and municipal buildings, and equipment. Although the individual's interest in this property might possibly be listed in terms of dollars and cents, the difficulties of arriving at the effect of transactions of public units on the consumer warrant its omission. The control of the individual over his equity in this property is so remote and so ineffective, as to give additional reason for its omission. 

It was previously stated that all property of value is owned by people. Some property 

Individuals also own indirectly the property of business corporations and of nonbusiness corporations, or institutions. This ownership interest is commonly evidenced by certificates, most of which are transferable. Hence an individual may have certificates that are evidence of an ownership interest that he can sell at his pleasure. He also has a somewhat remote and somewhat ineffective voice in the management of the corporation in which he has an equity. As has been indicated in previous chapters, he receives a return on his stockholdings by sharing in the corporate profits,

Ch. 29] 737 

PERSONAL ACCOUNTING 

distributed at the will of the corporate directors in the form of dividends. It is estimated that the ownership interest in business corporations is held by as many as one third of the total number of families in the country, and that perhaps one third of the cost of property held individually represents this sort of indirect ownership. 

The property that the individual owns directly is that most commonly considered as constituting his assets. This includes such items as cash on hand and on deposit in banks, his home and furniture, his automobile, and his inventories. Against these assets may be set up his obligations, including accounts due mercantile concerns, short-term notes due banks or other credit institutions, and long-term notes secured by mortgages on his real property. In accordance with the accounting. equation assets minus liabilities equal proprietorship, the difference between his assets and his liabilities is his proprietorship.

The balance sheet of Arthur B. Case is shown on page 738. 

The classification of assets and liabilities on this balance sheet follows that used by business concerns. The property owned is of two kinds: assets that produce income and assets that are used in the business of living. If an individual owns a variety of assets that produce income, he might find it helpful to classify them on his balance sheet separately from those assets that do not produce income. The liabilities attached to the two types of assets might also be reported separately. The proprietorship would thus be reported in two amounts: one representing an ownership interest in income-producing property and the other a similar interest in property used in living.

The content of most of the accounts presented in the balance sheet of Arthur B. Case is indicated by the account titles, which correspond to those used by business concerns. Cash is recorded in two accounts to distinguish between that which is immediately available and that which will be available only after a lapse of time. Since Case will receive his salary for December on the first of January, it represents an asset as of December 31. The note receivable represents a temporary loan to another individual. 

Except for its temporary character, it might be included under investments. 

When current assets become excessive for current use, it may be advisable to transfer the excess to investments. This transfer
