Ch. 29] 739 

PERSONAL ACCOUNTING 

rather than as a fixed asset. All income-producing properties may well be reported as investments.

The asset life insurance, listed under the heading "Investments," represents the cash-surrender value of insurance policies. 

In the early years of a policy, the annual premiums overpay the cost of the insurance; hence a portion of each premium payment represents an asset. If the policy is surrendered, the amount of this asset can be recovered in cash. If the policy is not surrendered, the amount of the asset represents a prepayment of the cost of future insurance.

All property owned privately by an individual has been (a) inherited, (b) received as a gift, or (c) earned as compensation for services rendered or as profit in business activity. It would be possible, and might be of interest, for the individual to designate his interest in his property in accordance with these three sources. 

Just as corporate proprietorship, which results from the investments of stockholders and from profits earned, is designated as capital stock and earned surplus, so the interest of the individual in his property might be termed inherited capital, gift capital, and earned capital. On the balance sheet of Case the ownership interest is shown as a single item. Through the comparison of balance sheets year by year, the growth or the contraction of this proprietorship will be made apparent.

The Personal Statement It is perhaps not far from the truth to say of Profit and Loss that most of the property owned by an individual results from the excess of his earnings over personal operating expenses. The function of the personal statement of profit and loss is to give the details resulting in increases or decreases in proprietorship. Personal income tax to be paid the 

Federal Government and the state government is based on an analysis of such details. This matter is discussed in Chapter XXX. 

In the first section of the personal statement of profit and loss the income is itemized according to its sources. Unlike most business enterprises, which derive income mainly from sales, the individual may have a number of sources of income. The salary that he receives for personal services rendered to a corporation or his net earnings as an entrepreneur is the first and most important item. Interest on bonds owned, dividends on stock, rentals from real or personal property, and profits derived from the exchange of assets are other sources of income. These also are

[Ch. 29 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

discussed more completely under the subject of personal income tax.

Various analyses have been made of personal or family operating expenses. In general, such an analysis may be said to depend upon the economist's classification of human wants, food, shelter, and clothing. Modern civilization seems to have added a new want, motor transportation. Whatever classification is followed, the items listed should be expenses of the period covered, rather than merely expenditures occuring within the period. The statement should be one of income and expense rather than one of cash receipts and cash disbursements. Expenditures may include disbursements for assets that will be used in future periods or for investments that will produce income, as well as for the current expenses of living.

In order to facilitate comparisons, it is perhaps helpful to classify these expenses as costs. The cost of food includes not only expenditures for groceries and meat consumed but also meals taken outside of the home. The identification of expenses with one or another living cost is, in nature, similar to process cost accounting. The cost account represents the purpose of the expense in connection with the business of living. 

Such a classification of living costs is given below. The primary wants of shelter, food, and clothing are represented in the first three items. Succeeding costs represent functions of living in present-day economic society. The list given is not the only analysis that could be made of living costs, but it is sufficiently suggestive to be useful for purposes of illustration. 

1. Housing costs. 

2. Food costs. 

3. Clothing costs. 

4. Household operating costs. 

5. Automobile operating costs. 

6. Advancement costs. 

7. Recreation costs. 

8. Deductible donations costs. 

9. Miscellaneous costs. 

The detail to be incorporated in the main analysis of living costs and in the sub-analysis of each item will depend upon the usefulness of the information to the person concerned. In personal affairs as in business activities, accounting is a tool to be used. If information is not used, it need not be kept. It is believed that a

Ch. 29] 741 

PERSONAL ACCOUNTING 

knowledge of the various living costs, incurred period by period, will assist in a more intelligent use of individual income. 

It should be noted that the statement of profit and loss should list costs rather than expenditures. Sometimes the two terms are synonymous, but often they are not. The expenditures made to pay interest on a mortgage loan and at the same time to amortize the principal of the loan are in part housing costs and in part reductions of the liability. The expenditure made for a new automobile represents, not an automobile operating cost, but an increase in the assets that offsets a decrease in the assets and/or an I increase in the liabilities. The use of the so-called "cash-basis method" of accounting is apt to be misleading, a fact indicated by the previous examples.

The statement of profit and loss may be drawn up each month, although the more significant period is perhaps the year. Even though monthly statements may be prepared, it seems advisable to have a formal statement to cover the period of time between annual balance sheets. The statement of profit and loss of Arthur 

B. Case is shown below.

The classification of living costs in the preceding statement is only suggestive. It does, however, follow the analysis commonly made of such costs and should serve as a point of departure for that person who desires a different classification. Any of the items might well be supported by a schedule giving further desired details. 

Housing Costs 

Housing costs are comparable with rent. 

If the place of residence is rented, this account would have a monthly entry to record the payment of rent. 

If the residence is owned, this account would be charged for all expenses incident to ownership. These include real estate taxes, insurance, maintenance, depreciation, and interest on the note if there is a mortgage or trust deed on the property. 

The purchase of a place of residence is a complicated business transaction. It ordinarily involves the payment of a considerable amount of cash by the purchaser and the giving of a note or mortgage for the balance of the purchase price. It also involves adjustments for prepaid insurance premiums and for taxes prepaid or payable. The recording of title and the services of an agent to facilitate the transfer also enter into the transaction. To illustrate, assume that on June 17, 1939, Samuel Olive agreed to purchase a house for $12,000, paying $1,000 down and $4,000 before July 1, and giving a 6% note secured by trust deed for the balance. The purchaser sent a check for $1,000 to the Escrow Department of 

The First National Bank. On June 24 he sent a second check for $2,500, and on June 28 a third check for $1,500. Assume there was a three-year insurance policy on the house costing $90 and running from January 1, 1938, to December 31, 1940. Also assume that there were taxes for the calendar year of $240 and that the first installment of $120 was payable July 20. It is estimated that the house is worth $9,000, the lot $3,000. The fees incident to purchase amount to $30. The journal entry made by Mr. Olive, the purchaser, on July 1, 1939, would be as follows: 

Land.. 

House. 

Insurance Prepaid. 

Cash. 

Trust Deed Payable, 6%

Escrow Department.. 

Taxes Payable... 

3,000.00 9,030.00 45.00 45.00 7,000.00 5,000.00 120.00

Ch. 29] 743 

PERSONAL ACCOUNTING 

This entry would be made up from the statement rendered Mr. Olive by the escrow department of the bank. The statement is accompanied by a check for $45 to close the account. 

If a comparison is to be made between the cost of owning and renting a property, the housing cost total should be increased by an interest charge for the owner's equity in the property. This does not appear in the accounting records since there is actually no interest cost to the owner for his own equity in the property. 

When a tabulation of cost items is made, however, it should be included.

Food Costs 

This account includes all costs incident to meals prepared at home and meals taken outside of the home. The food costs might be classified in whatever way would provide the individual with the most useful information regarding this living cost, and then recorded in a group of accounts. The costs of maintaining the kitchen and the dining room may be included in ascertaining total food costs. It is more customary and less involved to consider such costs as a part of household operating costs. The inventory of food supplies should be set up as an asset at the end of the year and treated as a deduction from expenses in arriving at food costs. 

Clothing Costs 

Since clothing is an individual matter, it is customary in accounting for the family unit to maintain a clothing cost account for each member of the family. The clothing cost accounts will probably have considerable inventory values to be set up in the balance sheet at the end of the year. The value of a clothing inventory can be determined in a manner similar to that followed in finding the present book value of a fixed asset belonging to a business concern. 

Household Operating 

Costs

The account or group of accounts with household operating costs includes all expenses incidental to the operation of the household. The cost of gas, electricity, water, and telephone service is usually included. 

These four expenses are ordinarily determined from monthly bills rendered by public utility companies. In a region with a cold climate the cost of heating the house is sufficiently important to warrant a separate account. In such a case the fuel inventory may represent a deferred charge of considerable amount. The mainte-

[Ch. 29 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

nance and depreciation charges on household equipment and furniture represent household operating costs. So also does the cost of personal service.

A review of items included under this heading suggests the desirability of using a group of accounts rather than one account. 

Such a group might well include accounts for the following costs: 

1. Electricity cost. 

2. Gas cost. 

3. Water cost. 

4. Telephone service cost. 

5. Personal service cost. 

6. Household equipment cost. 

7. Heating cost (or cooling cost).

Automobile Operating A prominent automobile manufacturer has Costs suggested that to food, shelter, and clothing the economist should now add personal transportation. Modern living is characterized by the extensive use of the automobile. No small portion of personal income is expended to permit the individual to go hither and thither.

All expenses incident to the operation of an automobile should be charged to an account maintained with these costs including a periodic charge for depreciation. At the end of the year an inventory of the accessories recently acquired may advisedly be used to give more accurately the present worth of the automobile and the operating costs of the current period. 

Advancement Costs 

Some expenses are incurred expressly for the purpose of improving the individual's appreciation of living. Such a purpose is furthered by good books and magazines, lectures, music, and art. Advancement costs will undoubtedly differ with various individuals. It should be satisfying to the individual to know at the end of the year the extent of his investment in advancement projects. 

Recreation Costs 

Expenditures for recreation are sometimes included with advancement costs. Recreation, however, represents a different type of activity. The cost of the annual vacation, of week-end trips, of frequent visits to the theater may accumulate to an astounding figure within a year's time. Such an amount is an index to the individual's need for outside paid assistance in achieving recreation.

Ch. 29] 745 

PERSONAL ACCOUNTING 

· Deductible Donations Since income-tax legislation permits the de- Costs duction of donations to charitable institutions, it is advisable to set up separately such costs. The knowledge of the smallness of this amount might serve in some cases to make the informed person a more useful member of society. In other cases, the extent of such donations may explain financial difficulties in other costs. The moot question of how much should be donated will be more satisfactorily handled by each person if he knows the balance of this account. 

Miscellaneous Costs All expenses not identifiable with one or another of the defined groups must, of necessity, be summarized in a miscellaneous classification. This group of expenses should be carefully scrutinized periodically to find a possible new classification within it. The need for such scrutiny is determined by the size of the total of the miscellaneous costs compared to the size of the total of the costs previously discussed. If the total of the miscellaneous costs is relatively small, these expenses need not be analyzed. If it becomes large, a study should be made to discover a new classification to be added to those previously given. 

- Personal Records 

The accounts to be kept in the personal ledger will be determined by the amount of detail desired in the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss. Much detail will require the keeping of many accounts; less detail will correspondingly shorten the periodic trial balance. It would not be impossible to use subsidiary ledgers if so extended detail were desired that the general ledger would become too bulky. For example, House Furniture might be a controlling account for a subsidiary ledger patterned after the fashion of a ledger in which a record of manufacturing equipment is kept. In a similar manner the stock account might be a controlling account for a subsidiary ledger showing the amount of the investment in each kind of stock.

A trial balance of the general ledger may be taken at the end of each month, and statements may be prepared at the end of each year. More frequent statements are ordinarily unnecessary. 

The journals are usually restricted to the cash receipts journal, the cash disbursements journal, and the general journal. The infrequency of transactions not involving cash receipts or cash

[Ch. 29 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

disbursements makes inadvisable other special journals. Monthly general journal entries for such costs as those incurred in the operation of an automobile may be made from supplementary records. The supplementary records may be filed to provide the detail represented by the summarizing journal entries. 

The practice of depositing all cash receipts in the bank and of making all withdrawals by check is a business practice that may well be copied by the individual. The reconciliation of the monthly bank statement is a procedure that helps materially in maintaining the accuracy of the journal records. 

Checks, duplicate deposit slips, and periodic statements of account make useful business vouchers in the personal accounting system. Other business papers are those evidencing ownership or creditor interests in corporations and those showing the title to real or personal property. These include such papers as stock certificates, bonds, certificates of title, insurance policies, and notes receivable. It is a common practice to keep these papers ir a safety deposit box in a convenient bank.

The Personal Budget 

An estimate of personal income, expenses, and savings is termed a personal budget. A personal budget can best be prepared after a study has been made of the income and the operating costs of previous periods. The predicting of future income and future operating costs by means of a budget has many advantages, among which the following may be mentioned: 

1. Cash receipts and cash disbursements can be balanced. 

2. A wiser distribution of expenses can be effected. 

3. Savings can be increased.

The personal budget is primarily an estimate of cash receipts and cash disbursements for the coming period. Cash is obtained principally from salary income, though cash income may also be received in the form of interest on bonds, dividends on stock, rentals on owned property, and other miscellaneous sources. In addition, cash may be derived from borrowing. One's ability to borrow, however, depends on the security that can be offered, which in turn depends on one's net worth. Cash is disbursed principally to meet current operating expenses, though the requirements of contractual liabilities to creditors for installment obligations have become an increasing item in current financing. Cash is disbursed, too, for the acquisition of fixed assets and investments

Ch. 29] 747 

PERSONAL ACCOUNTING 

that are not consumed currently but represent properties that appear on the personal balance sheet. Such disbursements con- =stitute savings and tend to improve the financial position of the individual.

It is therefore customary for personal budgets to emphasize savings. A certain percentage of the estimated annual income is 

I usually reserved for this purpose. If the income is large, the percentage can be correspondingly larger; if the income is small, the percentage of savings will need to be less. After the amount of the savings has been determined, the balance of the income is apportioned to the various living costs. This apportionment will depend upon several factors, chief among which are: 

1. The number of persons depending on the personal income for subsistence. 

2. The ages of the individuals so dependent. 

3. The section of the country in which the family lives. 

4. The social customs of the group of which the family is a part. 

Such factors enter into the determination of the budget, but they cannot be considered sufficient in themselves. The one who makes the budget needs a summary of the operating expenses of previous periods as an additional guide. Only a complete personal accounting system, such as that described, can provide this essential information.

By the use of the statement of profit and loss of the past period, or those of several past periods, as a guide, the budget for the coming period can easily be adjusted to the changed conditions. 

The statement of Arthur B. Case illustrated on page 741 showed an operating loss of $50 for the year. Assuming a cessation of dividends on the stock of the Acme Mfg. Co., Inc., Case prepared for the coming year the budget at the top of page 748. 

This budget provides for a decrease of $700 in the annual income and a surplus of $150, as compared with a deficit of $50 in the previous year. This budget is not a cash budget but rather one based on actual costs or expenses. If it is carried out during the coming year, the proprietorship of Case will be increased $150. 

A comparison of the actual costs of the year will show why the increase in proprietorship was more or less than the budgeted amount. 

Personal Finance 

The use of a personal accounting system and the personal budget provides the information and the procedure for intelligent personal finance. The

[Ch. 29 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

ARTHUR B. CASE BUDGET FOR YEAR 19-- 

Estimated Income from Salary and Interest... ..

ESTIMATED OPERATING COSTS: 

Housing Costs.. 

Food Costs.. 

Clothing Costs Household Operating Costs.

Estimated Savings... 3,700 00 1,000 00 800 00 500 00 250 00 250 00 200 00 150 00 150 00 250 00 3,550 00 150 00 financial integrity and security of individuals as consumers is as important as the financial position of big business corporations. 

Personal finance is inescapable among independent consumers.

The three factors mentioned on page 746 under "The Personal Budget" may well be considered in a program of personal finance. 

They are: (1) the balancing of income and expenses, (2) the wise distribution of expenses, and (3) increased savings. Each of these is discussed briefly in the following paragraphs. 

Under modern merchandising methods the appeals to personal desires are so numerous and continuous that there is a constant danger that a person may spend more than he earns. This danger is increased by the prevalent custom of buying on the installment plan. It is easy for a person to promise to pay in the future for goods that he can obtain and enjoy in the present. If the individual is to keep his expenditures within his income, he must watch carefully not only his current expenditures but also his future promises to pay. He can do this satisfactorily only by making a careful estimate of what his income is to be and by planning expenditures so that under no conditions will they exceed his income. Companies selling on the installment plan not infrequently find cases in which people have contracted to pay such large amounts in the future that they do not have sufficient income left to meet their current needs. 

Few people can have all the things they desire. It is necessary to choose what is most necessary and desirable. There is always a danger that the individual may spend his income to satisfy im-

Ch. 29] 749 

PERSONAL ACCOUNTING 

mediate desires and thus neglect to provide for future needs. -Therefore, a careful study should be made of necessities, and the amount available for spending be divided properly to take care of the various needs. This plan is made easier if standards of expenditures are established. For example, the individual should decide on certain percentages of his income that will be spent for rent, clothing, food, and other necessities. After the income has been estimated, it can be distributed according to the standards set. Only by this means can a wise distribution of expenses be effected.

The desirability of accumulating savings is self-evident. One who does not save cannot hope for financial progress. It is not wise to spend all of one's income early in life with an expectation that an increase in income will make possible future savings. 

There is a natural tendency for one's desires to increase in proportion to one's income; and, unless the habit of saving is established early in life, there is a danger that savings will not be accumulated. It is always possible to see other people who live comfortably upon less income than one receives. Such an example should serve to indicate to each person that he can, if he will, control his expenditures in such a way that some savings can be made regardless of the amount of his income. The only safe method of insuring the accumulation of savings is to set aside periodically a definite amount for this purpose. 

After the income has been estimated, the amount to be saved should be determined before estimates of living costs are made. If this plan were not followed, so much might be allotted to certain expenses that nothing would be left for savings. After the amount of the savings has been fixed, the balance of the income can be distributed to living costs.

The Incorporated It is not unusual for family property, par- Family ticularly income-producing property, to be incorporated. Under such circumstances corporate accounts must be set up, records of all transactions must be kept, and reports of the family income must be prepared. Out of this income dividends may be declared periodically.

The use of family property in this manner is not unlike the operation of a business corporation. Interests in the corporate property are represented by shares of stock. Directors are elected by the shareholders, and officers are appointed by the directors,

[Ch. 29 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Annual reports, consisting in a balance sheet and a statement of profit and loss, are submitted to the shareholders. 

The Estate 

The property accumulated by an individual may continue to be held as a unit after his death. It is then known as his estate. The ownership interest in an estate is commonly vested in an individual or a corporation acting as trustee. The beneficial interests, however, belong to the heirs of the decedent.

Business transactions may arise from estates. Such transactions must be recorded, analyzed, and reported. The accounting involved is known as estate accounting.

CHAPTER XXX TAXATION 

Governmental Income The income of governmental units comes and Private Income principally from levies on income-producing private business and private individuals. Business enterprises and persons who have property or income contribute to the support of the various governmental units. These contributions, authorized by legislation and levied on private property and income, form the substance of accounting for taxation. 

With the exception of certain non-profit institutions whose income and property are exempt from taxation, all business units and all persons owning property or receiving income must pay taxes, and these must be accounted for on their profit and loss statements. Whatever amounts appear as additions to income from taxes on the income and expenditures statements of governmental units must appear as tax deductions from income on the profit and loss statements of business units or persons. The relationship of the Government and the taxpayer is a reciprocal one. When taxes are collected, they provide income to the governmental unit and at the same time reduce in like amount the net income of the taxpayer. Cash received by the Government in payment of taxes is at the same time cash disbursed by a private enterprise or party.

It is the purpose of this chapter to discuss accounting for axes on the records of the taxpayer. The amount of taxes to be assessed almost invariably bears some relation to the information found on the records of the taxpayer. Where taxes are self assessed and reported, the taxpayer must rely largely on his accounting records for the information necessary to determine his ax liability. For sales taxes, his record of sales is a determining actor; for income taxes, his records of income and related expenses are essential elements. Where taxes are assessed by the axing unit, the records of the taxpayer may have some bearing on the amount of tax liability. In property taxes, the book value of the property assessed may be admitted as a factor in the amount of the assessment. 757

[Ch. 30 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

The Basis of Taxation 

The taxes to be paid are usually determined by applying a rate to a value expressed in money or a volume of operations also expressed in money. The rate is fixed alike for all taxpayers. The second factor, however, must be determined in each case. In its determination accounting records may be indispensable. 

Taxes Levied on Bal- From the standpoint of the taxpayer, as ance Sheet Items sets appearing on his balance sheet are generally subject to property taxes. Land and buildings are considered real property and may be subject to real property taxes. 

All other assets are considered personal property and may be subject to personal property taxes. For this second group, the values shown in the accounts are often used as a beginning point for assessed valuation. For real property taxes, book values are less useful because of the fluctuations that may occur in market values during the time that the property is held by the taxpayer. 

Property taxes are ordinarily levied by local governmental units such as counties, townships, municipalities, and school districts. They are based on the assessed value of personal and real property. The assessed value is related to book value in the case of such assets as inventories, cash, and receivables. It is more closely related to current market values in the case of real property. 

The amount of the property tax is determined by applying the tax rate to the assessed valuation. If the tax rate is $2.20 per $100 and the assessed valuation is $10,000, the tax would be $220. This assessed valuation is ordinarily less than the market value. In some instances the assessed valuation may be as low as 

40% of the estimated market value of the property.

Some corporation taxes are based on authorized or outstanding capital stock. Both of these items appear among the balance sheet accounts. Such taxes are ordinarily of state origin. 

Taxes Based on State ment of Profit and Loss

Data In addition to the property and capital stock taxes, there is a wide variety of taxes that affect the activities of the business enterprise. The statement of profit and loss as a measure of the volume of such activities becomes the basis for most of such taxes. 

These taxes may include sales taxes, import duties, business franchise taxes, license fees of various kinds, various operating taxes, and last, and perhaps most important, income taxes. The

Ch. 30] 759 

TAXATION 

relation of most of these taxes to statement of profit and loss data is apparent. Sales taxes are based on the volume of sales, and the first item on the statement of profit and loss provides information as to the sales volume. Income taxes are based on net income, and the statement of profit and loss concludes with the disclosure of that amount.

Treatment of Tax Items on the Statement of 

Profit and Loss

Taxes on balance sheet items or on income and expense data must appear on the statement of profit and loss. Property taxes may appear as a single expense item among the general expenses, or they may be identified with that activity with which the property taxed is related. For example, personal property taxes on delivery trucks would appear as a delivery expense, on merchandise inventory as a selling expense, on office equipment as an administrative expense. If the statement of profit and loss is departmentalized, property tax expenses should be apportioned on the basis of the investment in each department. Taxes based on payrolls must also appear on the statement of profit and loss in connection with the proper wage or salary expense. For example, taxes on sales salaries would be classified as a selling expense, but those on indirect factory labor would be included in the factory overhead. However, sales tax is shown as a deduction from sales rather than as an operating expense, inasmuch as the price charged was presumably raised to cover sales tax. Business franchise taxes are treated as a general expense. The income tax based on results of operations for the year is shown as a deduction from net income on the statement of profit and loss as illustrated below:

Sales Tax 

The treatment of sales taxes on the records of a business depends upon the practice followed in their collection. The merchant may include the sales

Ch. 30] 761 

TAXATION 

tax in his sales price or he may add it to the sales price as an additional charge to the customer. When the tax is included in the sales price, the account Sales represents both the sales and the amount collected and owed to the governing body. If this practice is followed, an entry would be made, usually at the end of the month, debiting Sales and crediting Sales Tax Payable. Thus, if the sales for March show a balance of $51,500 and the sales tax is 3%, the actual sales may be determined by dividing $51,500 by 1.03. The actual sales will be found to be $50,000, and the sales tax will be found to be $1,500, which is 3% of sales. The following entry would be made to record the sales tax liability:

Pay-Roll Taxes 

Under recent Federal social security legislation, taxes based on pay-roll figures are assessed on employers and employees. In return for these tax payments employees are entitled to benefits in periods of unemployment and upon retirement. To encourage the states to assume the responsibilities of unemployment insurance, the Federal Social Security Act provided for allowing a credit of 90% of the amount contributed by employers to the state unemployment compensation funds. The states have all taken advantage of this provision by enacting unemployment compensation laws, which have been approved by the Federal Social Security Board. 

For the accountant, social security legislation introduces the problems of handling the deductions from employees' salaries, the tax liability of the employer, and the payment of the taxes. Let us assume that employer Anderson has a pay roll of $1,000 in March and that he is required to withhold 1% of this amount as the employees' share of the old-age taxes and an additional 1% as the employees' share of the state unemployment taxes. He would make the following entry in his books:

Income Taxes 

The income tax differs from the other taxes affecting the statement of profit and loss in that it depends for its determination on practically all of the information shown on the statement of profit and loss. For this reason it seems advisable to deal separately and at considerable length with this particular form of taxation. 

Income-tax legislation and accounting statements of profit and loss are alike in that they seek to disclose elements of income and expense. Since income tax is based upon the amount of net income, income-tax procedures must point the way to determine net income. Income-tax returns are in fact modified accounting profit and loss statements. They cover a given period of twelve months, either the calendar year or the annual fiscal period of the taxpayer. Items on the principal statement are in both instances supported by schedules supplying necessary details. The principles followed in accounting in determining net income are specified as acceptable in determining income for tax purposes. 

Since all who must render returns need accurate data for those returns, income tax has been one of the most compelling incentives for complete accounting records. Businessmen and individuals have recognized in the income tax a tangible reason for adequacy

[Ch. 30 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

in accounts. It has become extremely important now not to overstate net income. Depreciation expenses have suddenly become useful and real to many who had before been unable to appreciate them as expenses.

In the business enterprise income tax may properly be included as disposition of net income. Theoretically, from the standpoint of the law income tax is a sharing of net earnings with the Government. Corporate income is not only to be used for dividends to stockholders, appropriations to reserve, and surplus, but a portion becomes an income tax liability. This liability depends in no sense on an action of the board of directors. As soon as the income for the taxable year is known, the taxable portion should be recorded as a current liability. 

Income tax is self assessed. Taxpayers are required to make their own reports of taxable income. These reports are called 

Income Tax Returns and are described in following sections of this chapter.

The Sixteenth Amend- The Sixteenth Amendment to the Federal ment and Revenue Acts Constitution (ratified in February, 1913) gave Congress the power to levy unrestricted taxes on incomes. 

Congress has passed a succession of income-tax. laws since 1913, taxing the income of individuals and corporations. Individuals, partnerships, corporations, estates, and trustees are required to file returns. However, no tax is imposed on partnerships, but each partner is required to pay an income tax on his distributive share of the partnership profits. Other returns are in the nature of schedules supporting the figures reported on the individual returns. 

While the returns are made annually, they do not necessarily cover the calendar year. Taxes are payable on the fifteenth of the third, sixth, ninth, and twelfth months following the period covered by the return. Since the calendar year is the most common fiscal year, March 15 is the date when most returns with accompanying checks are due.

Corporations have paid an increasing percentage of their net earnings as income tax. In some years this tax has been a flat percentage of net income approximating from 1/10 to 1/6 of the net income. In other years additional provisions have been added to include graduated excess-profits taxes or penalties for earnings not paid as dividends. Even with these additional provisions,

Ch. 30] 765 

TAXATION 

corporate income taxes have been more easily computed than - individual income taxes. 

Individuals pay normal tax on normal incomes. In addition, they pay surtax on more than normal incomes. They pay no tax on = incomes below the exemptions. Exemptions depend on the taxpayer's status and the number of dependents. For example, if the taxpayer is the head of a family, he is entitled to a personal exemption of $2,500, whether married or not, and is entitled to an exemption of $400 for each person who is dependent upon him for his chief support. A married person, who is living with his spouse, is also entitled to a personal exemption of $2,500 and an additional exemption of $400 for each dependent. Thus, if the head of a family or a married person had two dependents, he would be entitled to an exemption of $3,300 ($2,500 plus $800) without liability for normal tax or surtax. With an income of $6,000 he would pay normal tax on $2,700 ($6,000 less $3,300). Four thousand dollars per annum might be the allowable normal income. If he had an income of $15,000 and surtax began at $4,000, he would pay surtax in addition to normal tax on $11,000. 

Since the passage of the first income-tax act in 1913, new I revenue acts have been passed from time to time. These acts have provided for (1) an income tax on individuals and corporations and (2) an estate tax, a gift tax, manufacturer's excise taxes, a capital stock tax, and an excess-profits tax. Although changes in rates, exemptions, and administrative provisions have been made in successive acts, certain basic principles have been established for the computation of taxable net income. 

The majority of states have income-tax acts in large part copied after the Federal law. When states pass income-tax laws, these levies on income are in addition to the Federal tax. It is commonly the practice to follow Federal tax procedure in state income tax legislation and administration. A discussion of Federal tax procedure is therefore considered adequate for this chapter. 

Corporation Income Tax All corporations that are organized for profit and that are not specifically ex- -empted by the regulations are subject to the income tax. In general, corporations that are organized for the mutual benefit of their stockholders or that fulfill some public function and are not formed to earn profits are exempt.

[Ch. 30 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

The amount of the income tax that a corporation must pay is computed after its taxable net income is found. By taxable net income is meant the net income that is subject to tax according to the provisions of the current revenue act. In ascertaining the net income for tax purposes, it is first necessary to compute the gross income and then to subtract certain items known as deductions, which the law allows. 

Gross Income. Whatever a corporation receives from its operations constitutes its gross income. The items of income that a corporation is likely to show are profit from sales; interest on investments and bank deposits; rents; royalties; dividends; and other income. Thus income items are classified according to their source and only the net income or profit from each source is shown on the face of the income-tax return. Supporting schedules for the various items show the gross income from that source with the direct expenses incurred in realizing the income deducted. 

It should be noted that transactions involving the capital accounts of a corporation are excluded from gross income. Premium or discount on the original issue of stock is a part of capital surplus and is not considered as income. The same reasoning applies for forfeited stock subscriptions and donations from stockholders.

Deductions. From the sum of the taxable net income items included under Gross Income on the return, other expense items relating to the business as a whole are deducted. These include other salaries and wages, rent, repairs, bad debts, interest, taxes, contributions or gifts paid, losses by fires, theft or other casualty, depreciation, and depletion. However, the amount that the corporation pays as dividends on its own stock may not be deducted since dividends are a distribution of earnings and not an expense of the business.

Corporation Income Tax The computation of corporation income tax Return is discussed on pages 767 and 768. The corporation return is designed to provide all of the information that the Government may desire as well as to facilitate checking and auditing by the Government officials. The income-tax return thus varies from the statement of profit and loss of the corporation. The manufacturing enterprise analyzes rent, repairs, taxes, losses, and depreciation according to the internal organization of

Ch. 30] 767 

TAXATION 

the business. Manufacturing, selling, delivery, and general administrative analyses for these expenses are made, and the statement of profit and loss shows the portion of the expense applicable to each division. This internal analysis of expenses is not required on the corporation return. For verification of income by the -Government, the business as a single unit is preferred to its organization by departments and functions. For example, the single deduction for depreciation includes depreciation on all factory, office, and delivery equipment. Depreciation on factory equipment cannot then be shown on the cost of manufacturing schedule. 

This results in figures for cost of sales and gross profit that differ from those shown on the usual profit and loss statement because the cost of sales figure includes only such costs as material, direct labor, and supplies. A deduction from gross income for the entire amount of loss from the sale of capital assets in excess of profits from such sales is not allowed for tax purposes. Capital assets include all assets except merchandise held as stock in trade and except assets used in the trade or business and subject to an allowance for depreciation. The figure for Profits from Sale of Land, Stocks, and Bonds, as shown on the income tax return, is computed upon the assumption that capital losses are deductible against capital gains, but that the net deductible loss cannot exceed $2,000. A corporation having capital gains of $15,000 and capital losses of $20,000 would be allowed to deduct $17,000 of capital losses, $15,000 to match the gains and $2,000 additional. 

Computation of Corporation Income Tax 

The amount of income tax payable by a corporation is computed by applying a percentage set by law to the net taxable income computed by the methods just described. The procedure for computing the amount of income tax may be illustrated if certain assumptions are made. 

Assume that (1) The current revenue act provides for a tax of 18 per cent of net income. (2) Under the current revenue act a credit of 85 per cent of dividends received may be allowed against taxable income in order that income will not be taxed both when it is earned by operations of the first corporation and again when it is received as a dividend by the second corporation. Such dividends are therefore included in gross income and net taxable income, but are eliminated to the

[Ch. 30 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

extent of 85 per cent before the tax payable is computed. Thus, double taxation of corporate earnings is partially eliminated. (3) The net taxable income of a corporation was $135,000 for the year. Of this amount, $20,000 had been received as dividends. 

The computation of the tax is as follows: 

Net taxable income. 

Less credit for dividends received (85% of $20,000 dividends received).

Net income subject to tax.. 

Tax at 18%...... $135,000 17,000 $118,000 .$ 21,240

Capital Stock and Under a separate title the revenue acts Excess-Profits Tax have provided for a combined capital stock tax and excess-profits tax. The corporation is permitted to set any figure it desires as the original "declared value" of its capital stock. 

This original declared value is adjusted for income, dividends, etc., and the adjusted figure is known as the adjusted declared value. Assume that the corporation pays $1 per $1,000 of original declared value and this rate on adjusted declared value after the first year. The original or adjusted declared value used in determining the capital stock tax is also used in computing the excess-profits tax. If the net income subject to tax exceeds 10% but is not in excess of 15% of the declared value, as adjusted, such part of the net income is taxed at 6%. If the income exceeds 15% of the adjusted declared value, such excess is taxed at 12%. If the net income does not exceed 10% of the adjusted declared value, no excess-profits tax is assessed.

Computation of the Taxes. To illustrate, assume an adjusted declared value of $1,000,000 for the corporation illustrated above and the above rates to be in effect. The computation of the capital stock tax and excess-profits tax would then be as follows: 

Capital Stock Tax: $1 per $1,000 of declared value $1,000

Excess-Profits Tax: 6% of $18,000 (income between 

10% and 15% of adjusted declared value).. 1,080 

The tax liabilities are now as follows: 

Income Tax.. 

Capital Stock Tax. 

Excess-Profits Tax. 

Total Federal Taxes... 

$ 21,240 1,000 1,080 $ 23,320

Income Tax on Indi viduals The taxable net income of an individual is computed in very much the same manner as that of the corporation. The principal differences are found in the use of the cash basis and in the treatment of capital gains and losses. However, the computation of the income tax payable is quite different as a result of different exemptions and credits as well as different tax rates.

Gross Income. As in the case of a corporation, an individual's - taxable net income is computed by subtracting the total deductions from the gross income figure. Gross income is the sum of all the net income items receivable, including "gains, profits, and income derived from salaries, wages, or compensation for personal service, of whatever kind and in whatever form paid, or from professions, vocations, trades, businesses, commerce, or sales, or dealings in property, whether real or personal, growing out of the I ownership or use of or interest in such property; also from interest, rent, dividends, securities, or the transaction of any business carried on for gain or profit, or gains or profits and income derived from any source whatever."

The full amount of the profit from the sale of capital assets by an individual may not be subject to tax. "Capital assets" have been defined as all assets except (1) merchandise held as stock in trade and (2) assets used in trade or business and subject to an allowance for depreciation. In computing the profit that is taxable and that must be reported, assume the following percentages of gain or loss recognized on the sale or exchange of a capital asset: 

100% if the capital asset has been held for no more than eighteen months. 

66%% if the capital asset has been held for no less than eighteen months but no more than twenty-four months. 

50% if the capital asset has been held for more than twentyfour months.

The effect of such percentages is to lessen the amount of income to be reported on a sale of a capital asset as the length of time it is held increases. For instance, a taxpayer sells property at a profit of $6,000. If the purchase of this property was made within eighteen months, he must report as income $6,000, assuming that the rates indicated above apply. If the purchase was made 2 years ago, he will report $4,000, or 66%%, of the profit.

If the property was held for a period of over 2 years, he will re-

[Ch. 30 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

port $3,000 or 50% of the profit. If capital losses were sustained, the same percentages are applied, and the capital losses would be deducted from the capital gains for corresponding periods of time. 

As in the case of corporations, restrictions may be placed upon the amount of net capital loss that may be reported. 

A number of items need not be included as gross income and are exempt from taxation. Exclusions include such items as property received by gift or inheritance, amounts received under life insurance contracts paid on the death of the insured, health and accident insurance proceeds, interest on certain United States obligations, and dividends payable in stock. 

Deductions. From the sum of the taxable net income items included under Gross Income on the return, items not relating to profit-making activities may be deducted. These include interest and taxes paid, losses by fire, storm, etc., bad debts, and contributions to charitable institutions. However, personal or family living expenses cannot be deducted.

Individual Tax Com putation The individual must pay a normal tax on net income and an additional tax or surtax on total net income above a normal amount as stated by the current revenue act.

The Normal Tax. A personal exemption and a number of credits are allowed in computing the normal tax. The exemption and credits are subtracted from the net income and the balance of the net income is then subject to the normal tax rate. It may be assumed that the following are allowed:

Personal Exemptions: 

$1,000 if single or married but living alone. $2,500 if head of a family or married and living with husband or wife.

Credits: 

$400 for each person "dependent upon and receiving chief support from taxpayer, if such dependent is under eighteen years of age or is incapable of self-support because mentally or physically defective." 

10% of the amount of the individual's earned income. 

The last item demands some explanation. Earned income is defined as "wages, salaries, professional fees, and other amounts received as compensation for personal services actually rendered,"

Ch. 30] 771 

TAXATION 

which is reported as gross income. The Revenue Act further provides that income up to $3,000 may be considered wholly earned regardless of its source. However, the maximum amount that may be considered earned income is $14,000. If the net income is less than the earned income as a result of deductions, then the earned income credit may not exceed 10 per cent of the net income of the taxpayer.

When the taxpayer is engaged in a trade or business in which. both personal services and capital are income-producing factors, a reasonable amount may be considered as compensation for personal services actually rendered. The amount to be considered as earned income cannot exceed 20 per cent of his share of the profits, however, and this amount is also subject to the limitations mentioned in the previous paragraph. 

The normal tax may be computed after the total of the credits that the taxpayer may claim is subtracted from the taxable net income by applying the normal tax rate to this amount. 

The Surtax. An additional graduated tax is levied upon the surtax net income, which is the net income less the personal exemption and credit for dependents. Some items deductible for normal tax are not for surtax. One of these is the earned income credit. When the surtax net income is below a stipulated figure, no surtax need be paid. A typical table of surtax rates is shown below. 

AMOUNT OF SURTAX NET INCOME RATE PER CENT TOTAL SURTAX 

$ 0,000 to $ 4,000 4,000 to 6,000 4% $80 6,000 to 8,000 5% 180 8,000 to 10,000 6% 300 10,000 to 12,000 7% 440 12,000 to 14,000 8% 600 14,000 to 16,000 9% 780 16,000 to 18,000 11% 1,000 18,000 to 20,000 13% 1,260 20,000 to 22,000 15% 1,560 22,000 to 26,000 17% 2,240 26,000 to 32,000 19% 3,380 32,000 to 38,000 21% 4,640 38,000 to 44,000 24% 6,080 44,000 to 50,000 27% 7,700 Do It will be noticed that the $180 total surtax on $8,000 is made up of 5% on the $2,000 from $6,000 to $8,000 plus the previously computed amount of $80. The surtax then for an amount

[Ch. 30 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

of surtax net income that is not shown in the table is computed by adding to the surtax for the largest tabulated amount which is less than the income, the surtax upon the excess over that amount at the succeeding rate as indicated in the table. 

For example, the surtax for a surtax net income of $23,000 would be $1,730, determined as follows: surtax for surtax net income of $22,000, that is, $1,560, plus surtax for $1,000 taxable at 17%, that is $170, or $1,730.

Cash and Accrual Basis Taxpayers may report, income and expense on the cash basis or the accrual basis. 

The essential difference between the two methods is in the matter of the time of reporting income or expense; over a period of years the same net income results. On the cash basis only items of income and expense are included for which cash transactions have been completed. However, certain apparent exceptions to this rule exist. Items of income that have not been received but that are due and available without restriction must be included *$6,800 $2,800+$4,000 (20% of $20,000).

Ch. 30] 775 

TAXATION 

as income. This is known as "constructive receipt" and makes it impossible for a taxpayer to postpone the receipt of income indefinitely or until a later year in which he has less income to report, thus reducing the surtax rate. For example, if a salary has been earned and is available but not yet paid, it must be reported in the return filed for the year in which it was earned, rather than when it is paid him.

On the accrual basis a taxpayer reports all income accrued or earned and deducts expenses incurred, whether cash transactions have taken place or not. If a sale on account is made, it must be reported as income. If bills have been incurred, whether invoiced or not, they can be deducted.

Because depreciation does not depend upon cash disbursements, depreciation may be allowed on the cash basis as well as on the accrual basis. Where inventories are required, the cash basis as to purchases and sales cannot be used. 

The Administration of The administration of Federal income tax 

Federal Income Tax has been delegated to the Income Tax 

Division of the Treasury Department. All returns are audited (reviewed) by the Audit Section of the Income Tax Division. 

This audit may consist of a mere mathematical check or may involve a complete survey of the books and records of the taxpayer. The Audit Section operates to protect the Government's interests in the income of corporations and individuals. 

The Income Tax Division furnishes taxpayers with forms for making annual returns. Separate forms are provided for individuals, corporations, partnerships, and fiduciaries. The forms not only provide a standard arrangement of items but include instructions to the taxpayer. The standard arrangement of items and of the instructions greatly facilitates the work of the Income 

Tax Division. 

To assist the Government in checking the accuracy of returns, reports are required of paying bodies as well as of receiving bodies. 

Employers report on all salaries and wages paid amounting to more than the personal exemption of the individual. Corporations report dividend and interest payments.

Conclusion 

The probability of income-producing corporations and individuals sharing their income with Federal and state government for some years to

[Ch. 30 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

come seems assured. Accounting encompasses not only the determination of income but the participation of all parties in that income. The net profit of a corporation may be accounted for (a) in income-tax obligations, (b) in dividends paid to stockholders, (c) in reserves appropriated for specific purposes, and (d) in surplus unappropriated. The net income of the individual similarly goes for (a) personal income tax, (b) personal expense, and (c) increase of personal fortune.

CHAPTER XXXI SUPPLEMENTARY STATEMENTS 

Additional Accounting The two principal accounting statements, Statements the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss, are being supplemented to an increasing degree by other summaries. Some of these supplementary statements are derived from data found on successive balance sheets and statements of profit and loss. Others are derived directly from the accounts or the journal records. They are used primarily in the analysis and interpretation of accounting reports. For this purpose they provide standard procedures.

A number of supplementary statements have been mentioned in previous chapters. The reconciliation of the bank balance, the report of job orders completed, the departmental expense reports, and reports of physical inventories, all give periodic summaries containing information useful to management. The income-tax report and the budget are also examples of supplementary statements prepared from the accounting records, each of which fulfills a specific purpose. Some supplementary statements are prepared more frequently than the principal statements because the summarizing information provided by them is needed at more frequent intervals than the information contained in the principal statements. For example, a business might have a daily cash report, a weekly report of production, and a monthly report of departmental expenses and costs.

Supplementary statements should be distinguished from accounting schedules. Schedules are formal reports, prepared from ledger accounts, supporting items appearing on one of the two principal accounting statements. They are, therefore, an integral part of the balance sheet and statement of profit and loss. 

The other statements mentioned above are not so closely tied in and tend to supplement in one way or another the formal accounting reports.

The purpose of this chapter is to discuss two additional supplementary statements. These statements are ordinarily presented at the same time as the balance sheet and the statement 783

[Ch. 31 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

of profit and loss and are designed to provide a more complete picture of the financial position and the progress of the business. 

The statements presented here are intended to be representative rather than to complete the list of supplementary reports that might be prepared. The following are chosen for special treatment in this chapter: 

1. The application of funds statement. 

2. The cumulative statement of profit and loss.

The application of funds statement summarizes the effect of the financial transactions of the year on the financial condition of the business, stressing the changes that have taken place. The cumulative statement of profit and loss represents a consolidation of the statements of profit and loss prepared since the beginning of the enterprise.

In addition, there will be presented some modifications of accounting statements to make them more intelligible to stockholders and employees. Illustrations of some of these supplementary reports are included with this discussion. 

Corporate Reports to The two supplementary statements men- Stockholders tioned above are frequently found in annual reports submitted by the officers and board of directors to the stockholders of a corporation. These statements are submitted in addition to the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss.

The widespread ownership in corporations emphasizes the utility of financial statements that will meet the needs of scattered stockholders. Increasingly detailed reports are being submitted to stockholders in an attempt to keep them informed and to gain their support for the corporations. These longer reports are needed because absentee stockholders have no opportunity to supplement, from memory or personal discussion with fellow-owners, omissions in printed reports, as is possible when the ownership group is small. Corporate reports evidence the accountability of the officers and the directors for the stockholders' property in their charge. The intelligent administration of these annual reports will assist materially in solving the corporation problem in this country.

One corporation with approximately 400,000 stockholders has set as its goal-"to present all salient facts of a general and

Ch. 31] 785 

SUPPLEMENTARY STATEMENTS 

economic character so as to enable the stockholders to obtain as complete an understanding as possible of the year's operations and their influence on the corporation's position." To do this, the corporation includes in the reports submitted to its stockholders a "condensed consolidated balance sheet" to show position and a "summary of consolidated income" to give an understanding of the operations of the year. But, in addition, it includes a letter explaining in detail the principal statements and discussing problems concerning the corporation. For example, in a recent year it submitted to its stockholders, in addition to the various reports, a twenty-one page letter discussing (1) the items found on the two principal statements; (2) economic problems of the bank holiday, federal legislation, and policies of transportation taxation; and (3) company policies relating to co-operative plans, public relations, and stockholders. The supplementary accounting schedules and reports submitted at that time included the auditor's statement; a report showing the investments in subsidiary and affiliated companies; tabulations of sales, net profits, and dividends since the founding of the company; and other tables of statistical data. In all there were forty pages in this report. It is evidently the opinion of the management that the best interests of the corporation are served by a recital of these supplementary data to the many owners.

The practice of private business in making more extensive reports to stockholders is to be contrasted with the present situation in governmental business. The financial interest of citizens in governmental business is undoubtedly greater than the financial interests of many stockholders in private corporations, yet the annual reporting done by states and municipalities today is comparatively meager. The average individual has more knowledge of the financial position and operations of the corporation in which he owns stock than of the city in which he lives and pays taxes. 

The Application of Next in importance to the group of trans- 

Funds Statement actions affecting proprietorship is that group affecting cash. Transactions affecting proprietorship are recorded currently in the accounts and summarized periodically on the statement of profit and loss. This statement gives the gross amount for all transactions of each type that affect net worth. A similar summary of transactions revolving about cash and affecting balance sheet accounts provides useful supplementary infor-

[Ch. 31 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

mation. Such transactions, while having no effect on proprietorship, do affect the financial position. A summary of such transactions is sometimes called an application of funds statement. 

The application of funds statement summarizes the net effect of cash transactions on each balance sheet account. To illustrate: 

If Accounts Receivable had a balance of $10,000 at the beginning of the year and was debited for $95,000 and credited for $100,000 during the year, the net effect of these entries would be a decrease of $5,000 in accounts receivable and would indicate a source of funds. If the debits and the credits were reversed, the net effect of the transactions would be an increase of $5,000 in accounts receivable and would indicate an application of funds. What is true with one asset is likewise true with all assets, so the following rules may be drawn up: 

An increase in an asset indicates an application of funds. 

A decrease in an asset indicates a source of funds. 

In the case of fixed assets subject to depreciation the decrease in the asset is evidenced by an increase in the valuation account "Reserve for Depreciation." An increase in reserve for depreciation accounts therefore indicates a source of funds, since the asset account is thereby decreased. For example, if the machinery and equipment account increased $50,000 during the year and the reserve for depreciation account increased $40,000, there would be an application of funds to Machinery and Equipment of $50,000 and a source of funds from depreciation of $40,000. This might be shown as a net application of $10,000 to machinery and equipment. But the net figure provides less information regarding fund movements than the two figures and is therefore less satisfactory. 

As might be inferred from a consideration of the mathematical status of the accounting equation, the effect of increases and decreases in liabilities and proprietorship is just the opposite. To illustrate: If Notes Payable had a balance of $5,000 at the beginning of the year and was debited for $10,000 and credited for $15,000 during the year, the net increase would indicate a source of funds. If the debits and the credits were reversed, the account would balance and the net decrease would indicate an application of funds. The rules for liabilities and proprietorship, corresponding to those given above for assets, are: 

An increase in a liability indicates a source of funds. 

A decrease in a liability indicates an application of funds.

Ch. 31] 787 

SUPPLEMENTARY STATEMENTS 

An increase in proprietorship indicates a source of funds. 

A decrease in proprietorship indicates an application of funds. 

The following tabulation illustrates these rules: 

Source of Funds - Assets +Liabilities +Proprietorship 

Application of Funds + Assets - Liabilities 

- Proprietorship 

The period covered by the application of funds statement is usually one year, although it may be two, three, four, or more years. The data for the preparation of this statement are obtained from two balance sheets, supplemented by such other information from the accounts as needs to be included. For example, if the application of funds statement is to include dividend payments, this information is not available on the balance sheets and must be obtained from the accounts. 

It is customary for a business to use the amounts on the balance sheet for the latest year and those on the balance sheet that corresponds to the beginning of the period for which the application of funds statement is to be prepared. The amounts on these two balance sheets are entered in parallel columns, and the increase or the decrease in each item may be entered in a third column. The balance sheets of The Acme Manufacturing 

Company for the years ending on December 31, 1939 and 1940, are shown on page 788 in comparative form. 

The position of the reserves on the credit side of the balance sheet should be noted. As has been stated previously, this method of reporting valuation items overstates the total of the assets and the total of the liabilities and the proprietorship by the total amount of the reserves. In fact, in this case the credit side is ermed Liabilities, Valuation Reserves, and Proprietorship. This method was used here, however, to avoid the necessity for making additions and subtractions in a single column.

In order that subtotals might be shown as increases or deereases, the increases and decreases were listed in a single column. 

If these subtotals were not necessary, two columns, one for inereases and the other for decreases, might have been used. From the data entered in the column showing increases and decreases, a simple form of application of funds statement was prepared. 

This statement is shown in Illustration No. 107, page 789.

ILLUSTRATION No. 108, APPLICATION OF FUNDS STATEMENT WITH WORKING CAPITAL SCHEDULE 

The number of items in the principal statement is materially reduced in this new form. For this reason it is considered a more effective presentation. The detail of working capital is removed to a schedule, which contains only current assets and current liabilities. The content of the schedule is therefore more homogeneous.

Various forms and various titles are used for the application of funds statement. If the number of items representing the source

Ch. 31] 791 

SUPPLEMENTARY STATEMENTS 

of funds and the application of funds is large, the items may be classified as follows: 

Source of Funds 

1. Creditors (borrowings) 

2. Owners (new stock) 

3. Depreciation of assets 

4. Earnings 

Application of Funds 

1. Creditors 

2. Owners (dividends) 

3. Purchase of assets 

Other titles used for the application of funds statement in clude Statement of Financial Changes; Statement of Changes in Financial Position; Where-Got, Where-Gone Statement; State ment of Sources and Disposition of Funds; and Statement of 

Sources and Application of Funds. With the increased use of this supplementary statement by business enterprises, it is probable that both the form and the title will become more standardized.

Emphasis should be placed on the fact that the application of funds statement deals with net amounts, whereas the statement of profit and loss gives total volumes. The delivery equipment account might show the purchase of twenty new trucks costing $10,000 and the disposition of fifteen old trucks costing $9,000. 

The application of funds statement would show only the net increase of $1,000 during the year. In some cases it may be desirable to use gross amounts. In such a case the balance sheet figures will have to be supplemented by an analysis of the ledger accounts. 

The Cumulative State- The application of funds statement reports ment of Profit and Loss the financial changes that occurred within the period of the statement of profit and loss. The cumulative statement of profit and loss presents a summary of the operations that have been completed during the life of the business. The addition of the application of funds statement and the cumulative statement of profit and loss to the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss provides the following parallel reports: 

1. A financial report. a. For the life of the business (the balance sheet). b. For the latest fiscal year (the application of funds statement). 

2. An operating report. a. For the life of the business (the cumulative statement of profit and loss).

[Ch. 31 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

b. For the latest fiscal year (the statement of profit and loss).

The cumulative statement of profit and loss may be compared to the consolidated balance sheet, but in the former statement the units consolidated are time units. The only items to be eliminated in consolidating a series of statements of profit and loss are the intermediate inventories.

The following balance sheet of the Frisbee Manufacturing Company is from the twelfth annual report to the stockholders:

ILLUSTRATION No. 109, CUMULATIVE STATEMENT OF PROFIT AND Loss SHOWING DISPOSITION OF PROFITS 

The statement shown in Illustration No. 109 treats the history of the Frisbee Manufacturing Company as a unit. The results of twelve years of operations, and the disposition of the profits, are incorporated within the scope of the statement. No attempt has been made to identify earnings or expenses with specific years.

Such identification was made in the statement of profit and loss prepared at the close of each year. The trend of sales, cost of sales, net earnings, and dividends cannot, then, be determined from the cumulative statement of profit and loss.

It is sometimes desirable to classify the operations by years. 

In this case a few of the more significant items on the cumulative statement of profit and loss are chosen in order to simplify the resulting tabulation. The significant operating items on the cumulative statement of the Frisbee Manufacturing Company have been classified by years in the tabulation shown on page 794.

ILLUSTRATION No. 110, TABULATION OF OPERATING DATA 

Such a supplementary report by years gives significant information regarding the activities of the company during its history. The person who receives such a report gains a much more satisfactory understanding of the operations of the company than one who receives a report of the latest period only. While the analytical detail of the operations of any one fiscal period may be restricted, the useful information provided by such a tabulation more than compensates for this limitation. 

Both the cumulative statement of profit and loss and its supporting tabulation are being used today by large organizations in an attempt to give a better understanding to stockholders. It is believed that the lengthening of the period to include the life of the enterprise provides a summary that effectively supplements the balance sheet and the annual statement of profit and loss. 

Annual Reports to 

Employees reports to employees.

Many corporate managements have undertaken the responsibility of making financial In the interest of stockholders, who receive

Ch. 31] 795 

SUPPLEMENTARY STATEMENTS 

their dividend return out of what is left after labor is paid, attempts are made to inform the workingman regarding the nature of the employing corporation. 

The accounting terms are modified for this purpose. Technical language is avoided and long sentences tend to replace short concise accounting words. To avoid the use of large figures, the data are reduced to dollars per employee. Such reports are shown on pages 797 and 799.

Charts Showing Financial Data 

Other supplementary reports are set up in the form of charts, graphs, and illustrations. These reports usually select some idea such as the analysis of the sales dollar or the breakdown of the dollar of income and present it pictorially. The amounts reported in these illustrations are obtained from the accounts or the accounting reports. 

Supplementary State- The reworking and restating of accounting ments and Statistics information involves procedure essentially statistical in character. Modern accounting reports exhibit a tendency toward the increasing use of statistical devices. The inclusion of the number of units manufactured, the number of units sold, a territorial analysis of units sold, the number of employees, the average annual income of employees, and many other quantitative data in reports serves as evidence of this trend.

The increasing size of annual corporate reports is evidence of the inclusion of an ever larger amount of statistical information. 

Much of this is obtained from the accounts, but a great deal of it must be derived from other measuring devices. 

The combination of quantitative data with accounting amounts is perhaps most commonly expressed in terms of costs. 

It is reasonable to expect a considerable expansion of these cost summaries supplementing the formal statements. In preparing such cost summaries, procedures described in the previous chapters on cost accounting will be more largely used.
