CHAPTER XXXII ANALYSIS AND INTERPRETATION OF FINANCIAL Necessity for Analysis STATEMENTS 

The full significance of the information to and Interpretation be found on accounting statements is perhaps best appreciated by the accountant who prepares the statements. In order that these significant facts may come to the attention of those who can make use of them, it is becoming more and more the practice of accountants to analyze and interpret the information that the statements contain. The responsibility of the accountant does not end when the statements are finished. 

He must be ready to analyze and interpret them in order that they may be of greatest use to the management. 

The preparation of supplementary statements, discussed in the previous chapter, is designed to increase the utility of accounting summaries. These statements present significant data not available on the balance sheet and its accompanying statement of profit and loss. This deeper understanding of financial condition and operating results is particularly helpful in planning for the new fiscal period. The accountant can give great assistance when the budget of the business is prepared. He does this by explaining items found on the accounting statements. 

and Use of Financial 

Basis for Interpretation To make possible a correct interpretation and use of financial statements, it is necessary: 

Statements 

1. That the statements be prepared in the proper forms with proper classifications of the items that they contain. 

2. That the items appearing on the statements be correctly stated both as to amount and as to title. 

3. That proper comparisons be made to show the present financial condition and the change that has taken place during the fiscal period.

The forms of statements and the classifications of items have been discussed in previous chapters. The accounting procedures outlined in those chapters are designed to insure accuracy in 805

[Ch. 32 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

amounts and in terminology. It remains for this chapter to emphasize relationships that are significant. These relationships, involving comparisons, assist in forming judgments regarding the financial position and the progress of the business. 

Value of Comparisons To arrive at conclusions concerning financial condition from a consideration of financial reports, it is necessary to make comparisons. Any one item taken by itself has little significance. For example, the item of cash on the balance sheet of the X Manufacturing Company is $20,000. This amount in itself indicates little with reference to the financial condition of the company. If the company does a small volume of business, this may be too large an amount of cash for it to carry and may indicate poor management, especially if the company has interest-bearing obligations outstanding. For a somewhat larger company, this amount may represent a normal cash balance and may indicate proper financial management, especially if it is found to be the average balance of the company. For a very large company, such a cash balance may be much too small and may indicate poor management and a precarious financial condition. To determine the size of the cash balance that the company should carry, and hence to judge the adequacy of the present balance, it is necessary to ascertain: (1) The volume of business performed by the company and the immediate needs for cash. This necessitates a consideration of the asset and the liability items appearing on the balance sheet and of the income and the expense items appearing on the statement of profit and loss. (2) The cash balance that has been maintained in the past and the adequacy of this balance. This necessitates a consideration of past financial statements. 

The consideration of any other item on the balance sheet or of any item on the statement of profit and loss will involve a similar procedure. For example, the annual net profit shown on the statement of profit and loss of the X Manufacturing Company is $400,000. The first thought may be that this profit is a favorable indication; that the company must be in strong financial condition to earn such a large net profit. Investigation of the statements of profit and loss for the three preceding years may show, however, that the company during these years made an average

Ch. 32] 807 

profit of $800,000 a year. Furthermore, an investigation of the balance sheet may show that the outstanding capital stock of the company is $10,000,000, and that the net profit of the latest year therefore represents only 4 per cent on the amount of outstanding stock. After this investigation it will probably be concluded that the past year has not been a satisfactory one. 

Any statistical data, in order to be of the highest significance, must be presented in such a way as to show a comparison or relationship between facts. This principle applies to the facts shown by the balance sheet, and it applies even more to the statistics regarding the operations of the business that are shown by the statement of profit and loss. The more important of the comparisons that can be made in connection with these two financial statements will now be discussed. 

Classification of Possible The comparisons that may be made with 

Comparisons reference to the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss may be classified as follows: (1) Comparison of one item with another on the same balance sheet. For example, notes receivable may be compared with accounts receivable. (2) Comparison of an item on one balance sheet with the same item on another balance sheet. For example, the inventory shown by the balance sheet prepared at the end of one year may be compared with the inventory shown by the balance sheet made at the end of the preceding year. Such comparisons of the same items are usually made by means of a comparative balance sheet. (3) Comparison of one item with another on the same statement of profit and loss. To illustrate: The selling expenses for the year may be compared with the sales for the year, and the percentage may be determined. (4) Comparison of an item on one statement of profit and loss with the same item on another statement of profit and loss. 

To illustrate: The sales of one year as shown by the statement of profit and loss for that year may be compared with the sales of the preceding year as shown by the statement of profit and loss for that year. Such comparisons are usually made by means of a comparative statement of profit and loss. (5) Comparison of information shown on a comparative balance sheet with information shown on a related comparative

[Ch. 32 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

statement of profit and loss. For example, the increase in the inventory as shown by the comparative balance sheet may be compared with the increase in sales as shown by the comparative statement of profit and loss. 

Each of these classes of comparisons will be discussed. 

Comparison of Items on a Particular Balance 

Sheet 

That the following discussion may be made concrete, typical financial statements will be used as illustrations. For the discussion of the comparison of items on a balance sheet, Illustration No. 111 will be used.

To make the comparisons less complicated and more easily followed, amounts have been expressed in even hundreds of dollars. In a larger company it would be advisable to express balance sheet and profit and loss statement amounts in even thousands or in some cases in even millions of dollars.

ILLUSTRATION No. 111, BALANCE SHEET 

All the financial statements given as illustrations in this chapter have been made as simple as possible. The important

Ch. 32] 809 

comparisons that may be made with reference to the balance sheet shown in Illustration No. 111 are as follows: 

(1) Ratio of Current Assets to Current Liabilities. Since the funds to be used in paying the current liabilities must be obtained from the liquidation of the current assets, this ratio is exceedingly important. Because assets often shrink in value and liabilities never do, it is desirable that the current assets always be materially larger than the current liabilities. Banks for a long time have insisted on a rule-of-thumb ratio of 2 to 1; that is, that the current assets shall be twice as large as the current liabilities. This is not a safe rule to follow arbitrarily since the ratio that should exist between the current assets and the current liabilities will vary according to the nature of the business, the market, and other financial conditions. As shown by the balance sheet in Illustration 

No. 111, the current assets of the Standard Mercantile Company amount to $418,100, and the current liabilities to $198,250. The ratio is, then, slightly more than 2 to 1. 

The excess of current assets over current liabilities is referred to as working capital and represents the margin of protection to short-time creditors. The working capital must be, at the least, sufficient to absorb any shrinkage that might occur in the value of the current assets and to provide funds for the current operating expenses. If the amount is insufficient, additional working capital will have to be contributed by the owners of the business or will have to be obtained through borrowing. The working capital of the Standard Mercantile Company is $219,850; it is found by subtracting the total of the current liabilities from the total of the current assets.

The current ratio is a measure of a concern's working capital and an index of its debt-paying ability. The greater the current ratio, the greater the relative amount of working capital, and the greater is the assurance that the concern will have sufficient funds to meet its current debts as they mature and the costs of carrying on its current business operations. (2) "Acid-Test" Ratio. This ratio indicates the ability of a business to satisfy immediately the current debts. In the determination of this ratio, consideration is given to only those assets that could readily be turned into cash if the need should arise. 

A ratio of 1 to 1, or 100 per cent, is usually deemed favorable. 

The usual formula is as follows:

[Ch. 32 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

CASH RECEIVABLES + MARKETABLE SECURITIES CURRENT LIABILITIES 

The acid-test ratio of the Standard Mercantile Company is slightly more than .6 to 1. This ratio appears to be unfavorable. 

Whether it represents a dangerous situation depends upon whether the notes payable must be paid in the near future or whether they can be renewed or refunded. The nature of the notes payable should be determined before a definite conclusion is made from the acid-test ratio. This ratio is useful, however, in indicating that the notes payable should be given special attention. 

(3) Ratio of Notes Receivable to Accounts Receivable. In most kinds of businesses, goods are sold on account and notes are not received in payment at the time of sales. It is customary, therefore, for the accounts receivable to be considerably larger than the notes receivable. If a large item of notes receivable appears on the balance sheet of such a business, it is an indication of the probability that overdue accounts have been settled by means of notes. 

Such a situation is especially apt to arise in times of financial stringency. There are, of course, some businesses that sell habitually on terms providing for the taking of notes in payment. In such businesses it is expected that the item notes receivable will be larger than the item accounts receivable. On the balance sheet of the Standard Mercantile Company, the notes receivable are $8,800.00 larger in amount than the accounts receivable. The ratio of the notes receivable to the accounts receivable is slightly more than 1.17 to 1. The terms of sale of this company should be determined before a decision is made as to whether this excess of notes receivable over accounts receivable is justified. 

(4) Ratio of Notes Payable to Accounts Payable. On the bal ance sheet in Illustration No. 111 the notes payable are more than two and a half times as large as the accounts payable. To determine whether this ratio indicates a desirable condition, it will be necessary to analyze the notes payable according to whether they were issued to (a) merchandise creditors, (b) note brokers or banks, or (c) others. If they were issued to merchandise creditors at the time merchandise was purchased, or if they were sold to note brokers or issued to banks in order to obtain funds with which to discount accounts payable, there is no objection to the excess of the notes payable over the accounts payable. If they were issued

Ch. 32] 811 

to merchandise creditors in payment of overdue accounts, or were issued to officers or outsiders in order to obtain funds because the bank credit of the company is limited, an unfavorable financial condition is indicated. (5) Ratio of Fixed Assets to Fixed Liabilities. This ratio measures the asset protection of the present mortgage holders or bondholders and is an indication to a prospective long-term creditor whether any additional loans on the fixed assets of the borrower may be safely granted. Usually the minimum allowable ratio is 2 to 1; that is, the value of the fixed assets should be at least twice the amount of the mortgage debt. The fixed assets of the Standard Mercantile Company amount to $132,000, and the mortgages and bonds payable amount to $60,000. The ratio is therefore 2.2 to 1.

A concern that has a low current ratio may obtain additional working capital by mortgaging its fixed assets. The Standard 

Mercantile Company will not be able to obtain funds in this manner, however, because its fixed assets have already been mortgaged nearly to the limit. It will have to rely on stockholders for additional contributions. 

(6) Ratio of Owned Capital to Borrowed Capital. The total capital employed in the conduct of the Standard Mercantile Company amounts to $577,700. Of this amount, $319,450, or more than half, has been provided by the stockholders of the company; and $258,250 has been provided by the creditors. The ratio of the owned capital to the borrowed capital is slightly more than 1.23 to 1. This ratio in itself indicates a satisfactory condition, but it should be compared with the same ratio at the end of preceding years to see whether the stockholders are increasing or decreasing their interest in the business. It is a wise and conservative policy for a corporation to use its accumulated profits as a basis for expansion, and for the stockholders of the corporation thus to increase their equity in the business gradually. (7) Ratio of Capital to Fixed Assets. The fixed assets and the goodwill of the Standard Mercantile Company amount to $152,000, and the capital supplied by the stockholders amounts to $319,450, or a little more than twice the amount of the fixed assets and the goodwill. This comparison taken by itself does not mean a great deal; but if it is made for several years, it shows whether there is a tendency to convert the investment of the stockholders into

[Ch. 32 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

fixed assets, and thereby to rely on the creditors of the company to supply working capital, or whether there is the opposite tendency for the stockholders to supply an increasing amount of the working capital. The latter tendency is undoubtedly the desirable one.. If the stockholders supply a large part of the working capital, the corporation is not liable to become embarrassed in times of financial stringency; whereas if the corporation must rely at such times upon capital supplied by creditors, it may be hampered in its operations.

Comparison of Corre sponding Items and

Ratios on Different Balance Sheets 

In order that a more complete interpretation of the balance sheet of the Standard 

Mercantile Company (Illustration No. 111) 

may be made, the balance sheet of the same company as of June 30, 1938, will be used. In Illustration No. 112 both of these statements are shown in comparative form.

ILLUSTRATION No. 112, COMPARATIVE BALANCE SHEET 

Comparisons may be made of all items on a comparative balance sheet, and some benefit may usually be derived from these comparisons. There are, however, some comparisons that are of special significance. With reference to the comparative balance sheet shown in Illustration No. 112, these comparisons are as follows: (1) The notes receivable for the year 1939 are more than twice as large as those for the year 1938. At the same time the

Ch. 813 

accounts receivable for 1939 are smaller than those for 1938. 

Unless there has been a change in the terms of sale of the company, these two comparisons indicate an unfavorable condition. An increase in business will cause an increase in both the accounts receivable and the notes receivable; but if the notes receivable increase and the accounts receivable decrease, there is an indication that sales are being made to customers who are not paying promptly and are giving notes to obtain extensions of credit. At least the facts shown on the comparative balance sheet warrant a careful investigation to ascertain the reason for the condition. (2) The merchandise inventory of June 30, 1939, is almost twice as large as the inventory of June 30, 1938. Any one of several reasons may account for this increase: (a) There may have been a large increase in the volume of business, that necessitated an increase in the inventory. The comparative statement of profit and loss of the company will have to be consulted to ascertain if the volume of business actually increased. (b) A large amount of merchandise may have been purchased because of an anticipated increase in price or because of an estimated increase in business within the immediate future. 

Investigation will show whether much merchandise was purchased for either of these reasons. (c) A large amount of unsalable goods may have been accumulated during the past year; or a large amount of merchandise may have been purchased because increased sales were anticipated during the year, but this merchandise is now on hand because the sales failed to materialize. A calculation of the merchandise turnover as shown by the comparative statement of profit and loss will make it possible to determine whether either of these conditions exists. (3) The balance sheet of June 30, 1939, shows an increase in the assets land and equipment, over the amounts of these assets on the balance sheet of June 30, 1938. An increase in the value of fixed assets may arise from any one of the following sources: (a) Additional assets may have been purchased. If the actual or anticipated increase in business warranted such additions, they were properly made. (b) The cost of repairs on the assets may have been charged to the asset accounts instead of to expense accounts. Charging the cost of repairs to the asset accounts is, of course, highly improper since it overstates the assets and understates the expenses for the year.

[Ch. 32 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

(c) The assets may have been appraised, and, on the basis of this revaluation, their values may have been increased. 

It is contrary to conservative accounting and management to enter the appreciation of fixed assets in the accounts or to show it on the financial reports. Since fixed assets are not to be sold, but are to be used in the conduct of the business, an increase in their market values does not increase their efficiency, and their value to the business is therefore not increased.

A careful inspection should be made to ascertain the cause for the increases in fixed assets shown on the comparative balance sheet of the Standard Mercantile Company. An analysis of the surplus account of the company and of the expense accounts for the year will assist in the determination of the cause for these increases. (4) By turning to the liabilities and proprietorship side of the comparative balance sheet, it will be observed that the notes payable have greatly increased during the year 1939. The amount outstanding on June 30, 1939, is six and one-half times the amount outstanding at the end of the previous year. The accounts payable also have increased, but not in proportion to the increase in the notes payable. As explained in connection with the balance sheet illustrated on page 808, an analysis of the notes payable item is desirable in order to determine to whom the notes have been issued. If the notes have been issued to banks in order to obtain funds with which to discount merchandise invoices, good financial management is indicated. An inspection of the comparative statement of profit and loss to see whether the purchases discounts for the latest year are larger than those for the previous year will indicate whether notes were issued for that reason. This, however, will not be conclusive evidence, and an analysis of the notes payable account should be made. (5) Both the mortgages payable and the bonds payable have increased during the year 1939. This increase is more than the increase in fixed assets during the year; hence, even if the increase in fixed assets is found to be proper, some additional funds for use as working capital have been obtained by means of liens on the fixed assets. Although this procedure may be justified in certain cases, it is usually looked upon with disfavor. More commonly the desirable method is to have the additional working capital supplied by the stockholders and by earnings.

Ch. 32] 815 

(6) The capital stock of the company has remained stationary during the year, but the surplus has increased $8,130.00. The increase in the surplus looks favorable; but before a final decision on this point can be made, it will be necessary to ascertain: (a) The origin of the increase in the surplus. It is important to know whether this increase has originated from the earnings of the year, whether it is the result of the writing up of fixed assets, or whether it has been obtained from other sources. (b) The total earnings for the year. These earnings should be determined to see what part of them has been retained as surplus. It will be desirable to ascertain also whether the regular dividends have been paid. (7) A comparison of considerable significance is that of the ratios of current assets to current liabilities for the two years. 

For the year 1939 this ratio was slightly more than 2 to 1, whereas for the year 1938 it was slightly more than 4.5 to 1. There has therefore been a marked decrease in this ratio. Although the ratio for 1939 does not in itself appear unfavorable, the tendency indicated by the decrease in this ratio during the year may be decidedly undesirable. It seems necessary to ascertain whether the plans for the coming year will maintain the present ratio or whether a continued decrease is probable. of Profit and Loss

Comparison of Items on The statement of profit and loss of the a Particular Statement 

Standard Mercantile Company for the year ending on June 30, 1939, is shown in the illustration on page 816. The most important comparisons that may be drawn in connection with a single statement of profit and loss are illustrated by the percentages entered on this report. 

Each percentage represents the ratio of the particular item on the statement to net sales. These ratios are of a very great importance when taken in connection with the same ratios of previous years, but are of little significance when taken by themselves. This is due to the fact that, unless there is some standard by which to judge these ratios, there is no means of knowing whether they are too large or too small.

The ratios given in the illustration do, however, indicate that the Standard Mercantile Company is operating on a very narrow margin of net profit, 1.5%. This margin may be a normal one for the kind of business in which the company is engaged. It is possible

Comparison of Corre sponding Items and

Ratios on Different Statements of Profit and Loss

The comparative statement of profit and loss for the Standard Mercantile Company for the years 1938 and 1939 is shown in the illustration on page 818. A number of interesting comparisons can be made from this comparative statement. The most significant are: (1) Sales have increased 50 per cent. This increase in itself looks very favorable, but the results of the sales must be considered before a final conclusion is drawn. (2) For the year 1938 the sales returns and allowances represent 1.1 per cent of the gross sales. For the year 1939 they represent 2.5 per cent of the gross sales. This increase probably indicates one or the other of two things: (a) That poorer service is being given to the customers since more goods are being returned and more allowances are being claimed; or (b) That new territories are being entered and, in the process of establishing new relations, more liberal privileges are being accorded to customers.

An analysis should be made of these returns and allowances to ascertain the reason for this increase.

(3) In 1938 the gross profit on sales was 15.4 per cent of net sales, whereas in 1939 it decreased to 13.9 per cent of net sales. 

This decrease indicates that the cost of goods sold has increased more than have sales. Examination should be made to ascertain if the plans for the coming year contemplate making provisions for this increase in the cost of goods sold. (4) The merchandise turnover for the year 1938 was 3.36, whereas that for the year 1939 decreased to 2.46. Since there is a decided decrease in the rate of turnover, careful attention should be given to this matter.

Turnover is found by dividing the cost of goods sold by the average inventory. It represents the number of times the average inventory was exchanged for cash or other assets. It is important since the greater the merchandise turnover the greater the profit that can be made from the investment in merchandise. (5) The selling expenses were 6.3 per cent of net sales for the year 1938 and 8.3 per cent for 1939. This increase shows that, though the sales are increasing, they are costing more. By reference to the detailed items, it will be seen that the causes of this increase are the increases in salesmen's salaries, salesmen's expense, and advertising. These increased expenses may have been incurred to obtain additional business and to build up goodwill for the company. The tendency for such expenses to increase faster than sales is, however, a dangerous one, and care should be taken that it does not continue too long. (6) In 1938 the ratio of the general expenses to the net sales was 4.0 per cent; in 1939 it was 3.2 per cent. This decrease indicates a desirable tendency and shows that the increase in sales has not brought about a proportionate increase in the general expenses. (7) The net operating profit for the year 1939 was smaller than that for 1938. This is the most discouraging information shown by the comparative statement. Since the sales have increased 50 per cent, it is unsatisfactory to find that the net profit has decreased. It may be found upon examination that some of the expenses incurred during 1939 are expected to result in increased business during the next year. Under such circumstances there would be some excuse for the unprofitable showing for the past year. A careful examination should be made to ascertain if this is the situation.

[Ch. 32 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

(8) Although the net operating profit for 1939 was more than $5,000 less than that for 1938, the net income for 1939 was only about $2,000 less than that for 1938. At first these facts may seem to be encouraging; but if an examination is made of the items appearing under "Other Income" and "Other Expense," it will be seen that the smaller difference between the net incomes is due to the decrease in the sales discounts taken by customers. 

In the year 1938 customers took discounts of $9,900; whereas in the year 1939 they took discounts of only $1,090. This indicates that sales are being made to customers on less favorable terms, or that the credit department of the business has been lax by approving sales to customers who are slow in paying their accounts. As stated previously, the comparative balance sheet shows that these indications are probably correct. Relation of the Comparative Balance Sheet to the Comparative 

Statement of Profit and Loss 

When the comparative balance sheet in Illustration No. 112, page 812 is studied in connection with the comparative statement of profit and loss in the illustration on page 818, a number of significant facts are (1) The comparative balance sheet shows that the working capital, the difference between the current assets and the current liabilities, has increased from $195,320 to $219,850. The comparative statement of profit and loss, however, shows that the volume of net sales has increased from $435,000 to $644,000. By dividing working capital into sales, working capital turnover is obtained. In 1938, the working capital turnover was 435/195, or 

2.2 times; in 1939 it was 644/220, or 2.9 times. This change in working capital turnover is of itself of little significance. The higher rate of turnover may indicate efficient utilization of working capital, although it may also indicate a shortage of working capital. (2) The comparative balance sheet shows that the notes receivable of the company are increasing and that the accounts receivable are decreasing. The comparative statement of profit and loss shows that the discounts taken by customers have decreased greatly. These comparisons bear out the probability indicated by the comparative balance sheet of the company; that is, customers are giving notes in settlement of overdue accounts. 

It is needless to emphasize the undesirability of this tendency.

Ch. 32] 821 

(3) The comparative balance sheet shows a large increase in the merchandise inventory, and the comparative statement of profit and loss indicates that this increase is much larger proportionately than the increase in sales. Whereas the sales have increased 50 per cent, the merchandise inventory has increased almost 100 per cent. The comparative statement of profit and loss also shows that the merchandise turnover has decreased greatly. 

These comparisons indicate that unsalable merchandise is being accumulated and that there has consequently been a large increase in the merchandise inventory. (4) In studying the comparative balance sheet, it was pointed out that the increase in the value of the equipment might be due in part to the charging of repairs to the asset account. The absence of any charges for repairs on the comparative statement of profit and loss strengthens this probability. (5) The allowance for depreciation for the year 1939 amounts to $1,100. When this figure is compared with the amount of fixed assets subject to depreciation as shown on the balance sheet, $57,000, it is found that the allowance is but 1.9% of the value of the assets. (6) The provision for bad debts of $1,500 for 1939 might seem adequate until it is considered in relation to net sales and is found to be less than one fourth of one per cent of net sales. (7) The comparative balance sheet shows a very large increase in the notes payable. The comparative statement of profit and loss shows that the discounts taken on purchases have decreased during the year. This decrease indicates that notes have not been issued to obtain funds with which to discount merchandise invoices. As previously stated, an analysis should be made of the notes payable to ascertain to whom they have been issued. (8) The surplus account shows an increase of $8,130.00 for the year 1939. The statement of profit and loss shows that the net income for the year is $9,520. These facts seem to indicate that the amount paid in dividends has been a small percentage of the earnings and that the proprietorship in the business is being increased. Any increases in the assets should be examined since they may be due to the writing up of the values of the assets with corresponding credits to Surplus. If this has been done, the dividends may have absorbed practically all the earnings and the

[Ch. 32 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

proprietorship may not have been increased through profits. An examination should be made to ascertain whether this condition actually exists. 

Summary 

The analysis made of the comparative balance sheet and the comparative statement of profit and loss of the Standard Mercantile Company indicates the advantages of comparative statistics. The balance sheet of this company as of June 30, 1939, and the statement of profit and loss for the year ending on the same date seem to indicate that the company is in a very good financial condition, although the profit earned during the year is small in comparison with the investment in the company. But when the comparative balance sheet and the comparative statement of profit and loss of the company are studied, there are indications that the financial policies and the financial management of the company are far from satisfactory.

It is in connection with such businesses as the Standard Mercantile Company that comparisons are of the greatest value. If a company is in a very poor financial condition, this fact will be indicated by its balance sheet alone, or at least by its balance sheet studied in connection with its statement of profit and loss. 

But when a company has been in a prosperous condition and is commencing to pursue policies that are having a detrimental effect upon its financial condition, it is very possible that there may be a failure to detect this fact unless proper comparisons are made. The comparative balance sheet, studied in connection with the comparative statement of profit and loss, should indicate such tendencies. Of course, additional information should be obtained for use in checking up the indications shown by the comparative financial reports. Much of this additional information is provided by the supplementary statements discussed in the preceding chapter. 

1. The King Chain Stores System made a profit of $150,000 in a certain year. What other information is necessary before one can judge the sufficiency of this profit?

Ch. 32] 823 

2. The profits of the Taylor Mfg. Co. were $300,000, $200,000, and $100,000 in three successive years. Does the decrease in the profits prove the incompetency of the management? Explain. 

3. The sales of the King Chain Stores System increased in five years from $3,000,000 to $5,000,000. Does this increase prove the soundness of the policies in force? 

4. The ratio of current assets to current liabilities in the King 

Chain Stores System was as follows for each of the five years mentioned above: 3 to 1, 2.5 to 1, 2 to 1, 1.5 to 1, 1.2 to 1. Is a favorable trend indicated? 

5. The Cook Department Store wishes to borrow $150,000 from its bank in order to pay accounts payable. (a) What effect will this transaction have on the ratio of current assets to current liabilities? (b) on the acid-test ratio? 

6. The ratio of current assets to current liabilities in the Cook 

Department Store is 2.3 to 1. The acid-test ratio is 1 to 1. If the inventories amount to $390,000, what is the amount of the current liabilities? 

7. The sales of the Simons Stores System increased from $3,000,000 to $4,200,000. The average inventory, calculated at selling prices, increased from $600,000 to $700,000. Do these facts indicate sound management? 

8. The ratio of fixed assets to total assets has increased steadily from 40 per cent to 65 per cent over a period of five years. During the same period the ratio of cost of sales to sales has decreased from 

75 per cent to 65 per cent. Are these facts related? Explain. 

9. On successive balance sheets of Acme Motor Parts, Inc., accounts receivable are reported at $40,000 and $60,000. The corresponding statements of profit and loss show sales of $360,000 and $600,000 respectively. (a) Are these facts related? (b) What executive is responsible for the condition shown? 

10. The statement of profit and loss of X shows purchases discounts of $4,256 and interest expense of $1,520. The statement of profit and loss of Y, who is engaged in the same kind of business, shows purchases discounts of $152. The sales of both are approximately equal. Interpret this situation. 

11. The Wright Corporation was capitalized at $1,000,000 of 7 per cent preferred stock and $1,000,000 of common stock. It had $2,000,000 of 6 per cent bonds outstanding. Rote, Inc. had a

[Ch. 32 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

capitalization of $4,000,000 in common stock. The net operating profit of each corporation in a certain year was $400,000. In each case all of this amount was paid as dividends, no part being added to surplus. How much did the common stockholders of each corporation receive? 

12. Four years later the net operating profit of each of these corporations amounted to $200,000. In each case all of this amount was distributed as dividends. How much did the common stockholders of each corporation receive?

CHAPTER XXXIII  The Accounting Func tion as a Business Utility Accounting exists not for itself but for the useful information that it provides for the various groups interested in an enterprise. 

So far as it has served the needs of these groups, it has made for itself a lasting place in the business unit. 

There has not always been the close relationship between accounting and management that is found today. In the earlier days, information regarding debtors and creditors was stressed, and the accounts therefore primarily served the treasurer. More recently, accounts have furnished detailed information regarding corporate earnings and financial position to stockholders and other interested parties. These accounting reports are being modified and improved in order that information regarding management addressed to stockholders may be more accurate. In addition, accounting informs management regarding its financial progress. 

Day in and day out, accounting is a tool assisting in the accomplishment of the purposes of management. 

In order that management may be served best by accounting, it is desirable that the activities of the enterprise be viewed in accounting from the point of view of the management. Hence accounting for transactions should reflect the managerial purpose and should measure the effectiveness of the policies of the management. In so doing, it will serve most effectively those interested in the enterprise.

If management is to receive credit for its accomplishments from the stockholders, its reports to stockholders must have their confidence. Stockholders must feel they can rely on the accuracy and the adequacy of the accounting statements furnished them. 

To achieve this confidence, it is the invariable practice to have a review of the accounting reports by an independent accountant. 

This certification of management's reports is known as auditing and constitutes an important phase of accounting and management. 830

Ch. 33] 831 

Management Relation- In any given business, management stands ships between two groups: an external group with which it has relations of one kind and an internal group with which it has relations of a different kind. The position of management with respect to these groups is shown in the following illustration: 1. Stockholders 2. Bondholders- 

3. Bankers 

4. Merchandise Creditors´ 5. Governmental Agencies 

Management Manager of 

1. Sales 

2. Purchases 

3. Production 

4. Finances 

5. Accounting and Statistics 

It should be apparent that if accounting is to serve as an aid in management, it must provide information making possible proper control over the two kinds of relations suggested by the foregoing chart: (1) relations with those who are external to the business, such as stockholders, creditors, and governmental agencies; (2) relations with those who are responsible for the internal management of the business.

Previous chapters have dealt with the accounting principles and procedures incidental to obtaining this information. In summary, the reports needed by these various groups may be classified 

1. Reports showing the present financial condition of the enterprise. The balance sheet in any of its various forms is the principal report of this type. 

2. Reports showing the results of past operations. The various forms of income and expense analyses and the statement of profit and loss belong to this classification. 

3. Reports showing pertinent information that is needed by the executives and the employees in their daily activities. Such reports may consist of a statement for the treasurer showing the accounts falling due on the current day; a report to the collection manager showing accounts thirty, sixty, and ninety days past due; a report to the sales manager showing the items of stock that are moving slowly; and various other reports of a similar nature.

It remains for this chapter to discuss some adaptations that make accounting more useful to management and the related groups just mentioned. None of these adaptations has been discussed heretofore, yet each of them involves accounting information and each of them contributes to the greater usefulness of accounting.

[Ch. 33 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

One of these adaptations serves management primarily; the second serves outside groups primarily. Each, however, serves both groups in some way. They are: (1) system building, or the fitting of accounting reports, records, and papers to a particular enterprise and (2) auditing, or the providing for an independent and expert examination of the accounts and records. Both systems and audits are intimately connected with accounting, though they derive their utility from the peculiar services that they render to management and its affiliated groups. 

Uniform Systems of The need for adapting accounting reports, Accounting accounts, books of original entry, and business papers to the requirements of businesses in a given industry is well illustrated by the preparation of uniform systems of accounting. A uniform system of accounting comprises the accounting principles and procedures that apply to a given industry, such as the cement industry, department stores, or railroads. Careful study of the particular needs of various industries has resulted in systems of accounting fitted to the needs of those industries. 

Uniform systems commonly result from conferences between representatives of the industry who have an intimate knowledge of their industrial problems, and experienced accountants whose understanding of accounting is well founded. Out of these conferences come recommendations relating to the accounting that will be most useful in the industry. 

Examples of uniform systems are the system that must be used by all the railroads of the country under order of the Interstate Commerce Commission, the systems imposed on all public utilities by state commissions, and the systems prepared by associations of business enterprises for members of the associations. 

Each system represents an effort to adapt accounting to the needs of a particular industry. 

Classifications of

Accounts In the construction of an accounting system for an industry or for a particular enterprise, consideration should be given to future needs as well as to present needs. For example, in the determination of the accounts to be set up, provision should be made for a more comprehensive classification than that needed at the particular time. It is advisable, in setting up an expansible chart of accounts, to use some

Ch. 33] 833 

sort of coding system. An example of the Dewey decimal system applied to the coding of accounts is shown in Illustration No. 113 on page 834. In this system the digits in the number given to an account indicate the exact position of that account in the entire scheme of accounts. For example, Reserve for Depreciation of 

Office Equipment is given the code symbol 122V. The V means that this account is a valuation, or offset, account and is therefore a deduction from account 122. In the number 122 the 1 designates the assets classification; the first 2 indicates the second group of assets, which is the fixed assets; and the second 2 stands for the second group under fixed assets, office equipment. 

There are two distinct advantages of a Dewey decimal system of coding accounts. In the first place it is expansible; new accounts may be introduced at any time in their proper place. For example, if a new cash deposit were opened in a St. Louis bank, it could be given the number 1114, following New York Bank. If salesmen's salaries and commissions were to be expressed in relation to branch territories, a new digit could be added and the account numbers would be 6211, 6212, 6213 up to the number of branches. In the second place, since the system makes use of numbers, its symbols fit bookkeeping and other mechanical equipment designed for figures. 

Installation of Systems The conference between the representatives of management and of accounting provides the understanding necessary for the installation of an accounting system. When sufficient data regarding the various activities of the organization have been obtained, the following steps may be taken in building the system: 

1. The activities to be recorded are analyzed and classified, and the volume of transactions in each group is ascertained. 

2. A chart of accounts for balance sheet and statement of profit and loss items is prepared. 

3. The records to be used are outlined. 

4. The forms to be used are designed. 

5. The specifications or instructions for the operation of the system are written.

When the accounts have been charted and the underlying forms and records have been designed, the accountant should write detailed instructions about the operation of the system. 

The purpose of each account included in the chart of accounts

should be explained; the items that should be charged and credited to each account should be listed; and the disposition to be made of each account should be given. These instructions will be useful not only to employees who are in doubt as to the correct procedure but also to new employees.

The Controller and His The accounting system that has been de- Department signed for a particular business and has been installed will not operate by itself. A responsible officer should be designated to supervise all the accounting activities in the enterprise. This officer is one of the principal executives of the business and is called the controller. In order that his work may be effective, he should have the same official rank as the executives in charge of sales, production, and finance. Otherwise he is liable to have difficulty in obtaining the co-operation that is necessary for the successful accomplishment of his task. 

The controller should be the final authority on accounting methods and procedures, but he delegates the execution of these methods and procedures to members of his department. He is held responsible for the interpretation of the accounting reports and submits to other executives of the organization recommendations based on these reports. He is responsible for the construction and the use of the accounts. 

In general terms, the function of the controller is to direct, and the function of the chief accountant is to perform. The controller either outlines the accounts and the supporting records that are to be used, or approves those which have been prepared by the chief accountant. The chief accountant is responsible to the controller for the maintenance of the accounts and the records after they have been authorized.

It is customary for the chief accountant, in co-operation with the controller, to organize the accounting department into sections, to each of which he delegates a certain part of the work under his jurisdiction. Typical sections are indicated in the chart at the top of page 836.

The position of the company auditor should be noted. He is not under the controller but is an agent of a superior, "checking" the accounts of the controller and his staff. To distinguish him from a public accountant who serves as auditor, he is called an internal auditor. In order that his work may be effective and acceptable, he is given the more independent position.

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES

Measuring the Effective- The various accounting statements that ness of Management report the accomplishments of manage ment should be attested by an outside person who is free from managerial influence. It would scarcely be satisfactory for management to make its own conclusions regarding its service. If the reports are certified by an outside professional accountant, they carry more weight in the eyes of the stockholders, the creditors, and other external groups. 

It has become increasingly the practice for the accounts to be investigated by a professional accountant, who renders an inde pendent judgment after his investigation. This activity on the part of a professional accountant is known as auditing. While accounting deals with the construction and the maintenance of records and reports, auditing deals with their verification. 

Purpose of an Audit 

The primary purpose of an audit is to verify the accuracy of accounting records and reports, and thus to ascertain the actual financial condition and operating experiences of an enterprise. In the process of verification any errors that may exist must be detected and corrected.

Ch. 33] 837 

The errors that may be found in the accounting records and reports may be classified in the order of their importance as follows: 

1. Errors of principle. 

2. Errors of omission. 

3. Errors of fraud.

The most important errors that the auditor finds are usually those due to a failure to follow correct accounting principles. 

Items of expense may be charged to capital accounts; the merchandise inventory may be valued at improper prices; depreciation of fixed assets may have been omitted; and credit balances in accounts receivable may have been classified as deductions from assets rather than as liabilities. The most useful service of the auditor is in the detection and correction of such errors so that the accounting records and reports will show the correct financial condition of the business and the correct results of operations. 

Errors of omission are of two kinds: (a) failure to record a transaction and (b) failure to post. Errors of the first kind are difficult to detect unless the auditor makes a detailed audit and compares all vouchers with the entries in the books of original entry. The modern practice is to number the business vouchers of each class in consecutive order. For example, the sales invoices, the purchases invoices, and the checks that show disbursements are numbered consecutively. By noting the numbers in the books of original entry, the auditor can detect the failure to make necessary entries. The omission of a posting will be reflected in the trial balance unless both the debit and the credit for the transaction have been omitted, in which case the equilibrium of the trial balance will not be disturbed. 

Errors of fraud are due to the efforts of an employee or an executive to misstate the financial condition of the business for either of two reasons: (a) to hide defalcations on the part of the employee or the executive or (b) to mislead those to whom the accounting reports are to be submitted. If an employee is guilty of theft of cash or merchandise and has access to the accounting records, he may seek to hide his theft by falsifying the records. 

Executives of the company may desire to misstate the financial condition of the business to mislead the directors and the stockholders, creditors, prospective purchasers, or governmental agencies. 1 The duty of the auditor is to detect all attempts to state the financial condition erroneously. In the early development of

[Ch. 33 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

auditing, the detection of fraud was regarded as the chief function of auditing; but, with the modern development of accounting records and improved methods of internal check, the possibility of fraud has been decreased and the importance of this phase of the auditor's duties has been comparatively minimized. 

Kinds of Audits follows: 

The classes of work that, in current practice, are performed by the auditor are as 

1. Balance sheet audit. 

2. Detailed or complete audit. 

3. Investigations.

The purpose of a balance sheet audit is to verify the accuracy of the financial condition of the business at a specific time as shown by the balance sheet prepared as of that date. In performing a balance sheet audit, the auditor is primarily responsible for the correct statement of the financial condition of the business on the audit date.

A detailed audit comprises an extended examination of the accounting records, documents, and supporting vouchers of a business organization. It contemplates the detection of errors of principle, omission, or fraud that have been committed at any time during the period covered.

An investigation comprises the verification of certain facts that are needed for some particular purpose. For example, the ascertainment of the earnings for a certain period of time may be required as a basis for the calculation of the goodwill of a business that is being sold. The accuracy of the cash records may be desired at the time a cashier or a treasurer is resigning or is suspected of a misappropriation of funds. A verification of the analysis of the expenditures chargeable to repairs or to betterments may be desired for use in the preparation of an income tax report to the 

National Government. 

Auditing Procedure 

The exact procedure to be followed by the auditor will, of course, depend upon the type of audit to be performed. In general, however, the auditor works backwards from the accounting statements through the accounts to the books of original entry. Each item appearing on the statements is verified by the auditor's tracing backwards in this manner as far as is deemed desirable.

Ch. 33] 839 

 Items on the Balance 

Sheet

The following paragraphs contain an analysis of the information that may appear under each of the items on the balance sheet. 

Cash. The cash item reported on the balance sheet usually represents cash on hand and cash on deposit in a bank. By "cash on hand" is meant that in the cash drawer. If cash funds are maintained at branch offices, the amounts of these funds should be shown as separate items. Cash on hand represents currency and checks that have been received but not yet presented for deposit or payment. It does not include dishonored checks, due bills, and 

I. O. U.'s received from employees. By "cash on deposit in a bank" is meant funds that are subject to withdrawal without notice. Time deposits should be reported as a separate item. 

The total of all outstanding checks should be subtracted in determining the balance on deposit. All cash received should be deposited daily, and only a small amount should be withdrawn for use as petty cash.

Notes Receivable. Notes receivable are commonly received from customers in exchange for merchandise or to apply on accounts. If notes are received from other sources, such as employees, subsidiary companies, or purchasers of fixed assets, they should be reported separately under properly descriptive titles. 

If notes have been pledged as collateral for loans, the amount of such notes should be stated as a separate item. Notes past due should be shown under a separate classification, particularly on a balance sheet made for credit purposes.

Notes received from customers are sometimes discounted at banks. In such cases the company offering the notes for discount is required to indorse them, and thus becomes contingently liable on these notes. This contingent liability should be reported on the balance sheet. It is commonly shown as a footnote and is not included in the balance sheet proper. 

Accounts Receivable. The accounts receivable should be classified as follows: 

1. Accounts due from customers for merchandise sold to them. 

2. Accounts due from employees for advances made to them. 

3. Accounts due from subsidiary companies or branches for merchandise shipped to them or for advances made to them.

[Ch. 33 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

There are two reasons why this classification is desirable: 

1. If customers do not pay their accounts, collection can ordinarily be effected by legal process; whereas it is usually not expedient to bring legal action against employees, subsidiary companies, or branches. 

2. When the ratio of accounts receivable to sales is determined, only accounts receivable arising from sales should be taken into consideration.

Past-due accounts should be stated separately from accounts receivable not yet due. When the balance sheet is prepared, both past-due accounts and past-due notes may preferably be listed separately from accounts and notes not yet due. 

All accounts ascertained to be worthless should be charged off the books and not reported on the balance sheet. Adequate provision for probable losses on the remaining accounts should be made by establishing a reserve for bad debts, and the amount of this reserve should be shown on the balance sheet as a deduction from the total of accounts receivable. 

Inventories. On the balance sheet of a trading concern, only one inventory, consisting of finished goods, will be reported. On the balance sheet of a manufacturing concern, three inventoriesraw materials, goods in process, and finished goods will appear. 

In connection with all inventories, however, careful attention must be paid to three things: (1) quantity, (2) quality, and (3) price.

With reference to quantity, only the commodities to which the business has full and valid title should be included in an inventory. Goods received on consignment should not be included; but, on the other hand, goods shipped on consignment and not yet sold should be included since the title to them is still vested in the consignor.

Only goods of such quality that they have some salable value should be included in the inventory. 

All inventories should be valued at cost or market price, whichever is lower. By "cost" is meant the invoice price plus all expenditures incurred in placing the commodity in a salable condition. For example, the freight, drayage, and unpacking cost may be added to the invoice price. By "market price" is meant the present cost to replace the article-not the price at which the article may be sold. The market price of goods in process may be difficult to determine. The proper rule to follow is to determine

Ch. 33] 841 

the cost to replace the goods in their present state of manufacture, and then, if this is less than their original cost, to use the replacement cost.

Accrued Income. At the end of the fiscal period there may be items of accrued income, such as interest on notes receivable due from customers or interest on bank balances, that should be treated as assets on the balance sheet. Since such items are usually due and payable within a relatively short time, they are reported as current assets. 

Investments. Many companies own stocks or bonds of other companies or bonds of the United States Government. Although such stocks or bonds may be readily salable, the company usually does not intend to sell them in the near future. It is better, therefore, to exclude them from the current assets and to report them under the separate heading of "Investments" or "Securities."

The amount of notes held against officers of the company or subsidiary companies is sometimes listed under this heading instead of being reported as a current asset. If it is not the intention of the company to collect such notes within the near future, this procedure is desirable.

Stocks and bonds held as investments should be reported on the balance sheet at cost, unless the present market value is below the cost price. In the latter case their cost may be marked down to market value, or a reserve for the difference between the cost and the market price may be established. In case of a slight fluctuation between the cost and the market value, an investment may be reported at cost and the market price shown in parenthesis immediately after the title of the investment. 

Fixed Assets. Care should be taken to see that all fixed assets are reported at cost with proper reserves for depreciation. All repairs incurred in connection with such assets should be charged as expenses of the period in which they are made and should not be charged to the asset accounts. Care must be taken to see that any mortgages or liens outstanding against these assets are properly recorded as liabilities. Only assets to which the business has title should be reported on the balance sheet. It is usually considered wise to have such destructible assets as buildings protected by insurance.

Intangible Assets. The intangible assets of most frequent occurrence are goodwill and patents. Goodwill and patents are

[Ch. 33 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

sometimes shown as fixed assets on the balance sheet, but it is preferable that they be reported under a separate heading. Fixed assets, such as land and buildings, are subject to more or less accurate valuation; whereas the valuation of intangible assets, such as goodwill and patents, is very difficult. Assets of the latter type should be shown separately from the fixed assets so that all who read the balance sheet can see the values placed on the intangible assets.

Deferred Charges to Expenses. Deferred charges represent expenditures made during the current or preceding periods for services or supplies that will not be used until succeeding periods. 

Prepaid items, such as insurance, taxes, and interest, are the most frequent examples of such charges. Inventories of supplies that are not to be sold but are to be used in the operation of the business are included under this heading. 

Notes Payable. Notes payable should be classified to show the notes (1) due to merchandise creditors, (2) due to banks, (3) due to note brokers, and (4) due to others. This classification is desirable because it indicates the method by which a business finances its operations. In many businesses a large item of notes due to creditors may indicate the unfavorable tendency of issuing notes in payment of overdue accounts; but in those businesses that usually issue notes in payment of merchandise, it is expected that the balance sheet will show a large item of notes issued to merchandise creditors. Nearly all business firms borrow from banks to some extent. When cash discounts are offered on purchases, a business usually finds it profitable to borrow from a bank so that it can take advantage of the discounts. A large amount of notes due to banks and a small amount of notes due to creditors may indicate sound financing.

Notes issued to officers of the company may indicate that the business has some difficulty in obtaining funds and that the officers have been forced to come to its rescue. The amount of such notes should be reported separately. If any assets have been pledged as security for notes issued, this fact should be clearly set forth on the balance sheet.

Accounts Payable. Accounts payable should be classified to show the accounts (1) due to merchandise creditors and (2) due to others. This classification is useful, for a firm is not expected

Ch. 33] 843 

to incur accounts payable to other than merchandise creditors except under unusual circumstances, and consequently the amount of liabilities due others is significant. If any accounts payable have been secured by the pledging of assets as collateral, this fact should be indicated on the balance sheet. 

Accrued Liabilities. Accrued liabilities may arise from wages, interest, and similar items that have accrued but are not yet due. 

During recent years accrued taxes are an item of considerable importance that must be shown as a liability. Since items such as those mentioned must be paid within the near future, they should be reported as current liabilities.

Bonds Payable. Bonds payable should be reported as a liability and not as a deduction from the asset on which they are a lien. When serial bonds, which provide for the payment of a certain amount in each year, are issued, the amount that will become due within the coming year should be listed separately as a current liability. 

Mortgages Payable. Mortgages payable, like bonds payable, should be shown as a liability and not as a deduction from the asset on which they are a lien. It is well to indicate on the balance sheet the particular assets that serve as security for the mortgages payable. The dates of maturity of the mortgages may also be shown.

Contingent Liabilities. Full information about any contingent liabilities should be shown on the balance sheet. Contingent liabilities may arise from notes receivable discounted, from accounts receivable sold, or from a pending lawsuit that may result in a judgment against the firm. When there is considerable probability of an adverse decision in the pending lawsuit, a reserve to provide for the liability arising from this decision should be set up by appropriating a part of the surplus. If contingent liabilities are not recorded in the accounts, they may be reported on the balance sheet by means of footnotes that explain their nature, or they may be listed among the liabilities with their amounts written directly after the titles and not extended into the amount column. 

Capital. The capital of a corporation may be represented by three items:

[Ch. 33 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

1. Capital stock outstanding 2. Capital surplus 

3. Earned surplus a. Appropriated (Appropriated surplus consists of reserves that have been set aside for particular purposes and will later be returned to Surplus. The reserve of this kind that is of most frequent occurrence is the reserve for a sinking fund.). 

b. Unappropriated 

Items on the Statement The following is an analysis of the informaof Profit and Loss tion that may appear under the main headings of the statement of profit and loss. 

Income from Sales. The amount of gross sales represents the selling price of all the commodities or the services, the title of which has passed to the vendees. Under this heading should not be included the value of goods shipped on consignment or of goods for which orders have been received but the title of which has not passed. The test as to whether the title has passed should be based on whether a legally enforceable claim for the value of the goods exists against the vendee. The value of all goods returned and the amount of all allowances made to customers should be deducted from the gross sales to determine the net returns from sales.

Cost of Goods Sold. Under this heading should be shown the determination of the cost of those goods that were reported as sales under the preceding heading. By "cost," as used in this sense, is meant all expenditures incurred up to the time when the goods were put into condition to be offered for sale. The expenses incurred in making sales and in delivering the goods to customers should be reported as selling expenses and not as a part of the cost of the goods sold. One of the principal reasons for this classification is that there is usually one executive who is responsible for the cost of the goods and for placing the goods at the disposal of the selling department, and there is another executive who is responsible for the sale of the goods and for delivery to customers. 

In order that the efficiency of these executives can be judged, the expenditures incurred under the supervision of each should be reported separately.

Operating Expenses. Under this heading should be shown all the expenses incurred in connection with the selling and the ad-

Ch. 33] 845 

ministrative functions of the business. Not only the expenses that have been paid but also those that have accrued should be included. Care must be taken to include in the operating expenses the estimated depreciation of the fixed assets of the business. 

The selling and the general expenses should be classified in such a way as to place the responsibility for their incurrence upon the proper executives of the company. The extent to which these expenses must be classified depends upon the organization of the particular business. For example, if one executive is responsible for the sale of goods and another for the delivery of the goods sold, the selling expenses and the delivery expenses should be listed separately so that the efficiency of these two executives can be better judged.

Other or Financial Income. Under this heading should be reported income that has been derived from financial sources rather than from the regular operations of the business. For example, in a mercantile firm, discounts on purchases and interest on bank balances and notes receivable may appear under this heading. Such income is listed separately in order that the profit arising from the regular operations of the business can be determined independently of the income arising from capital. 

Other or Financial Expenses. Under this heading should be listed expenses incurred for long-term or short-term capital. 

Discounts on sales, interest on notes payable and bonds payable, and other similar items may appear under this heading. Financial income and financial expenses are reported separately from the operating income and the operating expenses for two reasons: 

1. Reporting financial income and financial expenses separately provides more satisfactory comparisons of operating income and operating expenses, because the former items may vary from period to period. 

2. The executives who are responsible for the operating expenses, such as selling expenses and general expenses, are ordinarily not responsible for the other expenses. Consequently, if any items that do not relate to operating expenses are included in this group, not only those examining the statement of profit and loss will be confused as to the placing of responsibility for the incurrence of these expenses, but also the executives themselves will find difficulty in checking increases in expenses.

[Ch. 33 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Qualifications of an 

Auditor

The auditor must have a thorough grounding in correct principles of accounting.

He should be experienced in the methods of keeping business records, so that he can interpret the information found in them and subject it to the test of reasonableness. He should know the devices by which dishonest employees alter records to conceal their wrongdoings. He must have tact in dealing with the accounting staff so that he will be assisted rather than hindered in his work of verification. Finally, he must be able to express his own conclusions in terms that his client will understand readily. 

The Auditor's Report 

The The content of the auditor's report will depend upon the understanding the auditor had with his client at the time the audit was arranged. report should include as much authoritative opinion of the auditor as the scope of the audit will permit. A report for a detailed audit is much more complete than a report for the briefer and less costly balance sheet audit. The investigation report is restricted to the subjects investigated.

Accounting practice differs as to the extent of the comments that the report should contain. The early practice was to make these comments very limited or to eliminate them entirely. The present tendency is to increase their number and scope. Some accountants comment upon each item on the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss, explaining the method employed to verify the item and anything unusual with reference to the item. Suggestions for improvement in the methods of handling some items may be included. Other auditors confine their comments to those items about which there is something unusual or with reference to which the auditor has some doubt. 

Since the report may be desired for public use-that is, for submission to creditors, such as banks, or to others who are interested some accountants believe that the better practice is to place in a separate report all comments and suggestions that are not necessary for the proper interpretation of the financial statements. There is considerable merit in this point of view. The important point is that the auditor should recognize as part of the responsibility the offering of all suggestions that may be helpful to his client or to his client's accounting staff.

Ch. 33] 847 

 The Professional 

Auditor and

Accountant In a small business it is not profitable to maintain an auditor permanently because the volume of operations is too small to occupy his time. The financial statements to be presented to stockholders, merchandise creditors, and bankers should, however, be verified by some disinterested person who is not connected in any way with the business. This verification is accomplished by hiring a professional auditor and accountant. The professional auditor and accountant offers his services to the public in much the same manner as the physician and the lawyer offer theirs. 

In the early development of public accounting, the chief function of the public auditor and accountant was to do auditing work. For this reason he was termed an auditor. In later years his work broadened until now the services he renders are of a varied nature. The installation of accounting systems, the preparation of income-tax returns, the installation of systems of budgetary control, the preparation of reports for the use of executives, and the giving of counsel and advice with reference to business organization and management are some of the important activities in which the accountant of today participates. In performing these 

I worth-while services, however, he is not acting in the capacity of an auditor.

Relation of the Private 

Auditor to the Public

Auditor The private auditor and the public auditor should co-operate fully in the performance of their tasks. The private auditor can usually do a large amount of preliminary work for the public auditor. For example, he can prepare numerous schedules, such as an accounts receivable schedule and an inventory schedule, that can be used by the public auditor. 

Every effort should be made to prevent a duplication of the work on the part of the private auditor and the public auditor. 

Each of these should realize that the other can render a valuable service to him. For example, the private auditor can do a great amount of detailed work that cannot profitably be performed by the public auditor. On the other hand, the public auditor provides an outside point of view and an independent check that are useful to the company and necessary for the maintenance of proper relations with stockholders and creditors.

[Ch. 33 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Use of Accounting as a The management delegates responsibilities Co-ordinating Mechan- to individual specialists who are best fitted ism by the Management to perform the delegated functions. That management will be most successful which wisely assigns responsibilities to individuals who have the capacity to perform them. In order that the management may know the effectiveness of its delegating activities, it should have a measuring device. 

From a slightly different point of view, it might be said that the management employs individuals who can perform necessary services at the least cost to the enterprise. Whatever services are necessary to the conduct of the enterprise are purchased under the policy of the management. Inasmuch as such services are continuous, it becomes necessary to have some kind of continuous record of them. Frequently prepared summaries of current transactions provide this continuous record for the management. 

Not every act of every individual can be made a matter of record for purposes of summary. For this reason accounting becomes a matter of selecting that type of information that is of greatest significance to management. In order that no significant information may be omitted, and in order that summaries may be prepared in intelligible form, the controller or the chief accounting officer is increasingly being made a member of the managing committee. As a member of this committee, he is able to appreciate the information needed by his associates and to provide the accounting procedures necessary in obtaining that information. 

In its responsibility for the successful conduct of business enterprises, management is finding accounting to be a useful agency and those in charge of accounting to be helpful associates. As accounting continues to provide more accurate, more timely, and more complete information to those in the management group who can best use it, it will acquire for itself an increasingly important place in the field of business administration.
