CHAPTER XXXIV  Management of Busi ness Assets

Only that business which incurs no obligations to creditors can be positively assured of the absence of creditor control or management. As the existing management makes added use of credit and permits the creditor interest in the enterprise to increase, the possibilities of control of the activities of the business by the creditors are likewise increased.

In the accounting equation A=L+P, the left side represents property; the right side, the interests of groups of people in the property. One group is composed of those who are owners and conduct businesses as sole proprietors, partnerships, or corporations. The significance of this group has been presented in previous chapters. Another group is made up of creditors, an unorganized group selected by the owners through transactions involving credit.

The left side of the accounting equation portrays the contributions in property to the business by the two groups of people represented on the right side. The interest of the liabilities group in the property of the business continues until it is discharged by the proprietorship group, this discharge being made almost always by payment in cash. The interest of the liabilities, or creditor, group is usually not identified with specific assets because the owners in a going concern eventually cancel the obligations by cash payments.

The two groups of people are alike in that both of them have interests in the properties of the enterprise. In fact, the sum of their interests has equalled the values attaching to the properties. 

Full management of all these properties lies with the ownership group as long as they discharge their obligations to the creditor group. Such obligations involve cash payments in accord with the credit terms. As long as the ownership group can provide for these cash payments, their control over the properties will be uninterrupted. 857

[Ch. 34 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

As long as they are in control of the properties, the ownership group takes all profits from operations and suffers all losses. The property interest of this group is a buffer protecting the claims of the creditor group. Before creditors may suffer any losses, the equity of the ownership group must be wiped out. This fact accounts for the superior credit standing of enterprises with large proprietorships.

As long as a business operates successfully, the creditor group is unorganized. Creditors rely on the proprietors to manage the properties in such a way that they will receive payment in accordance with the terms of their contracts. Hence, whenever a creditor group shares an interest in the properties of a business, the proprietorship group, in order to continue as the controlling group, must comply with the contractual obligations to creditors and must pay such claims upon maturity. If the proprietorship group cannot meet the obligations, the creditor group may assume control of the business in an attempt to protect and realize its interest. 

Creditor control of an enterprise becomes an actuality only in occasional cases. This possibility is, however, always in the background, especially when the management commits indiscretions and when the swing of the business cycle is unfavorable. 

Incident to the organization of the creditors is the recognition of different classes of creditors. In relation to a going concern, the creditors are grouped into two classes: short-term creditors and long-term creditors. If a concern is liquidated, the relation of the creditors to the cash resulting from liquidation becomes important. 

In such a case the creditors may generally be divided into four classifications: (1) preferred creditors, (2) fully secured creditors, (3) partly secured creditors, and (4) unsecured creditors. These classifications will be explained later in this chapter. It is sufficient now to call attention to the necessity for the reclassification of creditors when an enterprise ceases to be a going concern. Insolvency

The possibility of creditor control arises with the insolvency of a concern. A business that cannot meet its debts as they mature is considered insolvent. The assets may exceed the liabilities; but if the liquid assets available are not sufficient to satisfy maturing debts, the concern is insolvent. The state of insolvency may arise as the result of operating losses; inability to obtain credit; credit policies

Ch. 34] 859 

that are too liberal; or excessive investment in inventories, fixed assets, or other assets that cannot be converted into cash to meet liabilities as they become due.

If the business can obtain credit and borrow cash to settle current obligations, insolvency can be avoided. In that case the new loan provides the cash so that the business can meet its debts. 

It is when no source of funds is available to the enterprise that insolvency exists.

A business is also insolvent when its total assets at a fair valuation are less than its total liabilities. Insolvency under these circumstances obviously affects the claims of creditors more adversely than insolvency under the conditions previously explained. 

The illustrations that follow illustrate insolvency under the two sets of conditions.

Company A is insolvent because it cannot meet obligations that are due. Although the company has a surplus, its cash and collectible items total only $25,000, which is one fourth of the total liabilities due. The balance sheet of Company A is:

This second situation is far more serious than the first. In the first case, because of the equity of the ownership group, there is a chance that creditors will not suffer a loss. The loss would have to exceed $30,000 for creditors to be affected. In the second case the creditor's equity is already affected. Any further loss would only increase the loss to be borne by creditors. 

Procedure in Insolvency Every state gives legal remedies to creditors when debtors are insolvent. Such laws vary in the states, but in their application they must not conflict with federal bankruptcy legislation. 

By mutual agreement the creditors may decide to allow the insolvent to continue business operations, extending the time for the payment of the indebtedness until the debtor will be able to meet his obligations. Creditors may, on the other hand, mutually agree to accept a pro rata share as full payment of their accounts, and in this way allow the proprietor to pay off his obligations and to continue business activity. Such a settlement is known as composition. In some cases the proprietor and the creditors may agree to the disposal of all assets and the payment of liabilities so far as possible. The last method, which involves the discontinuance of the indebted business, is known as liquidation. 

The proprietor or the creditors of a business may apply to a court for the appointment of an individual who will assume management of the business and will protect the interests of all con-

Ch. 34] 861 

cerned. When the insolvent makes such application to a court, a voluntary assignment is effected, and an individual known as an assignee is appointed to conduct the business under the direction of the court. When the creditors apply to a court, an involuntary assignment is effected, and the one appointed by the court is known as a receiver in equity. The duty of the assignee or receiver is to proceed in such a manner as to serve best the interest of all creditors. It may be advisable to continue business operations, to pay off debts with the income, and then to return the business to the control of the proprietor; reorganization may be desirable; or the assignee or the receiver may decide, after consulting the creditors, that it is best to dispose of all assets, to utilize the cash in satisfying the debts of the business, and to return any excess cash to the proprietor. 

Insolvency and Bankruptcy

It is possible for one to be insolvent, yet not bankrupt. Under state insolvency laws the creditors and the owner of the business can arrange for the continued existence of the business and for various means of settling the obligations. The owner is not discharged from liability until all claims have been met in full through insolvency proceedings.

The Constitution gives Congress the power "to establish uniform laws on the subject of bankruptcies throughout the United States." Because the bankruptcy law is federal legislation, it governs whenever there is a conflict between it and the state law on the subject. Furthermore, unless there is a complete agreement among all parties to adjust affairs under the state insolvency law, any creditor or the owner may apply to a federal court for bankruptcy proceedings. If this application is approved, it operates to adjudge the debtor legally a bankrupt and to remove the case from state jurisdiction.

Action in bankruptcy means the surrender of the assets of the debtor to the creditors. A debtor in many cases seeks to be adjudged a bankrupt because he will then be legally discharged from all liabilities incurred in the past, even though his assets do not realize enough to pay all creditors. Creditors may desire bankruptcy proceedings in order that they may be assured a pro rata share without discrimination in all the assets of the bankrupt.

The Federal Bankruptcy Act provides that a debtor may file a voluntary petition in bankruptcy in a federal district court. A

[Ch. 34 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

list of all the assets and the liabilities must be attached to the petition. The federal law also provides that a creditor or creditors may bring involuntary bankruptcy proceedings against an insolvent who has committed an act of bankruptcy. 

If the court finds that the individual or the business is insolvent within the meaning of the law and has committed an act of bankruptcy, the individual or the business is declared bankrupt; a receiver in bankruptcy is appointed; and the creditors are notified. The creditors then elect a trustee, who takes over the assets from the receiver and proceeds to sell the assets and to liquidate the liabilities. As cash is realized from the sale of the assets, and the liabilities are liquidated, the trustee is required to render to the court reports and statements on the progress of dissolution and liquidation.

The Statement of 

Affairs.

Upon taking over the assets of an insolvent concern, the assignee, receiver, or trustee commonly arranges for the preparation of a statement showing creditors what they will probably receive from the liquidation of the concern. This statement, known as the statement of affairs, shows what the assets will probably realize and the amount of cash that will probably be available to the general creditors after the preferred and secured claims have been met. Creditors find the statement of affairs valuable as a basis for the determination of their action or policy with regard to the insolvent. After analyzing the conditions represented by this statement, creditors may decide to let the debtor have more time in which to meet his obligations; they may demand a reorganization of the business or the control of the business by the receiver or trustee until the debts have been paid; or they may demand immediate liquidation.

The Statement of 

Affairs Compared with the Balance Sheet

When a balance sheet is being prepared for a business it is assumed that the business will continue operations. The assets are accordingly stated at their value to the going concern; and the liabilities are shown in the order of time of payment, without reference to the priority of their claim on the assets, since it is assumed that all liabilities will be paid. The statement of affairs differs from the balance sheet (1) in the method of evaluating and the order of presenting the assets and (2) in the order of presenting the liabilities.

Ch. 34] 863 

Assets on the State- Assets are reported on the statement of ment of Affairs affairs at the amounts that are expected to be realized from their sale under current market conditions. These amounts will often be less than the book values of the assets, although in some cases they may be more. The assets are usually classified into the following three groups: (1) Assets that will realize no more than the amount of the liabilities for which they have been pledged as security are listed as fully pledged assets. No part of such assets will be available for unsecured claims, that is, claims that have no security or special priority at law. (2) Assets that will realize more than the amount of the liabilities for which they have been pledged as security are listed as partly pledged assets. The excess realized on such assets will be available for unsecured claims. (3) Assets that have not been pledged as security are listed as unpledged assets. The full amount realized on these assets will be available for unsecured claims.

Although the assets are commonly grouped in this manner, the regular balance sheet classifications may be used. The assets division of the statement of affairs usually has columns for the book value of the asset, the name of the asset, the appraised value of the asset, the amount available for unsecured claims, and the estimated loss or gain on the realization of cash. This form of the statement is shown in Illustration No. 115 on pages 868 and 869. 

Liabilities on the

Statement of Affairs 

Liabilities are commonly shown in five groups: (1) Creditors who must be paid before any unsecured creditors receive payment are listed as preferred creditors. Taxes, wages not exceeding $300 a claimant, expenses incurred in realizing the equities of creditors, as well as other debts that are given priority or preferred status under federal or state acts, represent claims of such creditors. The total owed to preferred creditors must be subtracted from the total amount available for unsecured creditors, reported in the assets section of the statement, in order that the net amount available for unsecured creditors will be shown. (2) Creditors who hold as security assets that will realize as much or more than their claims are listed as fully secured creditors.

[Ch. 34 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Since the claims of such creditors will be satisfied in full from the specific pledges by which they have been secured, nothing will be shown as unsecured claims. (3) Creditors who hold pledged property that will realize less than their claims are listed as partly secured creditors. The excess of the claims over the amount to be realized from the pledged property represents unsecured debts and is reported as unsecured claims. (4) Creditors who hold no security and are not preferred at law are listed as unsecured creditors. (5) Potential claims that may have to be met are listed as contingent liabilities, and the amounts are reported as unsecured claims.

In the liabilities division of the statement of affairs, columns are usually provided for the book values of the liabilities, the names of the liabilities, and the amounts of liabilities that are unsecured. 

The total of the unsecured claims represents debts upon which pro rata payment will be made as cash is realized on the assets available for unsecured creditors. The excess of the unsecured debts over the total amount available from the realization of the unpledged assets is the estimated deficiency to unsecured creditors. 

Asset and Liability 

Classifications

Compared 

A fully pledged asset is related to a partly secured creditor; a partly pledged asset, to a fully secured creditor. Only if the amount to be realized from the sale of the asset were exactly equal to the liability would there be a fully pledged asset with a fully secured creditor. For example, if an asset is estimated to bring $1,200 in cash and it has been pledged to secure an obligation of $1,500, the asset is fully pledged and the creditor is only partly secured. If, on the other hand, the asset had been pledged to secure an obligation of $1,000, the asset would be only partly pledged and the creditor would be fully secured. In the first case the creditor would have an additional unsecured claim for $300; in the second case there would be left from the sale of the pledged asset $200 to apply on the unsecured claims. If, by chance, the obligation for which the asset had been pledged were $1,200, then the asset-sale would just meet the liability-claim; the asset would be fully pledged and the claim would be fully secured. 

Unpledged assets apply against unsecured claims except for preferred claims. These last must be subtracted first from the

Ch. 34] 865 

cash realized from the sale of unpledged assets. To the unpledged assets are added the unpledged portion of the partly pledged assets. This portion is in reality unpledged. 

The chief function of the statement of affairs is to estimate the number of dollars available for unsecured creditors. This is usually expressed as so many cents on the dollar. For example, if the unpledged assets are estimated to yield $5,000 in cash and the unsecured claims total $10,000, the statement of affairs would show a probable payment of 50 cents on the dollar. of Affairs Procedure in the Prepa- Before the statement of affairs can be con ration of a Statement structed, a balance sheet must be prepared. 

With the balance sheet as a guide, each asset must be investigated; the present realizable value must be estimated, or the asset must be appraised by an individual competent to do so; and all claims must be accumulated. The resulting statement of affairs is then submitted to the creditors for consideration. To illustrate the preparation of a statement of affairs, the following example is offered.

The balance sheet on page 866 has been prepared by the receiver of Five H, Inc. The following data are obtained from an examination and an appraisal of the assets and the liabilities reported on the balance sheet:

The accounts receivable will probably realize $60,000. 

The note receivable will probably realize $12,000. 

The accrued interest is on a good note. 

The inventories can be sold for $80,000.

The machinery and equipment will probably realize $60,000. 

These assets have been pledged to secure $75,000 of the notes payable.

The buildings will probably realize $50,000; and the land, $50,000. Both assets have been pledged to secure the firstmortgage bonds.

The investment in the subsidiaries will probably realize $40,000. 

This asset has been pledged to secure the collateral trust-mortgage bonds.

Nothing will be realized on the goodwill. 

The cash value of the unexpired insurance at the time all the assets have been disposed of is expected to be $180. 

There is an additional contingent liability of $5,000 on a note that was discounted. It is assumed that Five H, Inc., will have to pay this note.

It is estimated that the cost of liquidation will be $3,000.

A statement of affairs is then prepared from the balance sheet and the additional data. This statement is illustrated on pages 868 and 869. 

Several points should be observed in the preparation of a statement of affairs. A valuation reserve is subtracted from the amount of the asset to which it applies, and only the net value is entered in the Book Value column. Accrued interest on a liability that is secured is added to the amount of the liability, for the creditor may hold that the asset is security for both the principal of the claim and the accrued interest. Similarly, an asset and the accrued interest on it should be listed in one amount if the asset has been pledged, for both the asset and the earnings of the asset are security to the creditor.

In the illustration shown on pages 868 and 869 there are two fully pledged assets, Machinery and Equipment and Investment in Subsidiary Companies. In both cases the appraised values are less than the amounts of the claims. The result is fully pledged assets and partly secured creditors. The balance of the creditors' claims appears in the column Unsecured Claims; in the case of 

Notes Payable this is $15,000, in the case of Trust Mortgage 

Bonds, $13,000.

There are also two partly pledged assets, Buildings and Land, which are appraised at $100,000 against First-Mortgage Bonds of $35,000 with $2,000 of accrued interest. The result is a balance of $63,000 available for unsecured claims. On the right-hand side of the statement the bonds appear as fully secured. 

The appraised realizable values of the unsecured assets are added to the balance of the partly pledged asset appraisal value to get an estimated amount available for preferred claims and unsecured creditors. Preferred creditors appear first on the righthand side; consist of three items, Wages Payable, Taxes Payable, and Cost of Liquidation; and amount to $6,500. This amount is subtracted from the Gross Amount Available to get the Net 

Amount Available for Unsecured Creditors. 

The difference between the total of Unsecured Claims and Net Amount Available is the Deficiency. In this case it amounts to = $86,700. In obtaining the number of cents on the dollar that unsecured creditors will realize, $222,800 is divided by $309,500 to obtain an amount of 72 cents on the dollar.

The difference between $317,200 and $230,500 gives the estimated deficiency to creditors of $86,700. Thus it is seen that no new material is added in the deficiency statement, but that this statement is only an improved arrangement to display the items leading to the deficiency. 

Operation of a Business The statement of affairs may indicate so by the Creditors great a loss from the liquidation of the business, that continued operation of the business may be deemed advisable in the interest of the creditors. Such a decision affects primarily the liabilities side of the balance sheet from which the statement of affairs was prepared. The existing creditors step into the place of the proprietors, and the latter therefore become inactive. A new group of creditors with preferential rights may then be created. The assurance of preferred claims to this new group makes possible operations requiring additional credit. These operations could not be possible if the new creditors were to be classed with the original creditors.

Ch. 34] 871 

The accounting technique for transactions performed under creditor management is too elaborate for treatment here. In general, two points must be observed: (1) a clear distinction must be made between the transactions completed under the original control and those performed under the new creditor control; (2) new credit claims must not be confused with the old claims, since the former have preference over the latter.

Liquidation of a Busi- As a general rule, the creditor group must ness by the Creditors liquidate the business either at once or after a short period of operation. The nature of the transactions of liquidation is suggested by the statement of affairs. When each asset is sold, the cash account is debited for the amount of cash received; the asset account is credited for the recorded value of the asset; and the account Loss on Realization is debited or the account Gain on Realization is credited for the difference between the book value of the asset and the amount realized. 

The balances of the loss on realization account and the gain on realization account are closed into the proprietorship account. 

The debit balance of the proprietorship account then represents the deficiency, which is charged pro rata to the unsecured creditors. The final entry records the cash payments to creditors.

It is estimated that the notes receivable will realize $10,000. 

The accounts receivable will probably realize $30,000. The finished goods and the goods in process inventories will suffer a 20 per cent loss at forced sales. The raw materials are worth 90 per cent of their book value. The machinery will probably bring $20,000. The building and the land are estimated to be worth $75,000. When the ledger was closed, accrued wages of $650 were overlooked. The costs of liquidation are estimated at $850.
